Codebook — CSUMF

Congressional Support for the Use of Military Force

Version 2.0 — one schema, four datasets. 2026-08-15. Companion to Hulme (2025), War and Responsibility (APSR). Supersedes Codebook v1.0 (January 2025).

1. What is new in Version 2

An earlier, unreleased version of this dataset measured congressional sentiment toward the use of military force in 191 U.S. crises between 1945 and early 2022, using human-coded Congressional Record floor speeches plus lawmaker tweets for 2017–2022. It promised future expansion "temporally (i.e., prior to 1945 and after 2022), and via the media analyzed." This release — the first public one — delivers that expansion, and adds a second sampling design. Because the earlier version never circulated, this codebook is written to stand alone: everything a user needs is documented here, with no prior codebook assumed.

What this release contains beyond the original hand-coded crisis corpus:

  • Temporal expansion backward (1873–1944). Twenty-nine pre-WWII crises — from the Virginius Affair (1873) to Pearl Harbor (1941) — with roughly 25,000 Congressional Record speeches, each assigned a 1–15 escalation level and, where applicable, a combat-initiation date.
  • Temporal expansion forward (2022–2026). Post-2022 crises, including the 2026 Iran war, via the Quorum corpus through mid-2026.
  • Media expansion. Beyond floor speeches and tweets: press releases, Facebook posts, YouTube transcripts, committee-hearing statements, newsletters, letters, and more — 13 document types, 1993–2026.
  • Full LLM re-labeling. Every statement in every corpus is labeled by three independent frontier models — xAI grok-4-fast-non-reasoning, OpenAI GPT-5.6 Luna, and Google gemini-3.5-flash-lite — under two instruments (the original 8-item binary instrument and a tripartite classification), plus an ensemble. Each label carries a confidence (1–5) and a verbatim key quote.
  • A second sampling design: continuous war-long coverage. Version 1 sampled time only in crisis windows. Version 2 adds the complement — every keyword-matched statement across the entire span of a war, month by month, from the first year of the conflict to its end:
    • CR_wars_wide.csv — 157,273 Congressional Record speeches across seven wars: World War I, World War II, Korea, Vietnam, the Persian Gulf, Afghanistan, and Iraq. Vietnam alone contributes 61,510 speeches over twenty-one years.
    • Quorum_wars_wide.csv — 102,390 statements of all public lawmaker output across the Iraq and Afghanistan wars. This file reaches 2021-08-31, the day after the last U.S. aircraft left Kabul, and is the only part of CSUMF that covers the end of the Afghanistan war.
  • Nine war-specific instruments (Appendices C and D), each the crisis codebook with a war-long referent and a dated chronology of U.S. action injected into every request, so support and opposition are judged relative to the policy in force on the statement's date rather than relative to a crisis. Every 8-item war prompt is the crisis 8-item prompt byte-for-byte with an additive war block appended.
  • Classification windows. Two wars are coded over less than their retrieved span, because the force question ceases to exist at a definite date: WWI stops at the armistice, WWII at the surrender ceremony (§3.2).
  • Roll-call linkage. 53 verified Vietnam-era use-of-force votes with party breakdowns (07_aux/vietnam_votes_master.json), plotted against the monthly speech-based series.
  • Four release files with an identical 102-column schema — CR_crises_wide.csv, Quorum_crises_wide.csv, CR_wars_wide.csv, Quorum_wars_wide.csv — each row one statement, carrying all raw human and machine labels side by side. Every figure and table in the article and in the LLM validation reproduces from these files alone (the published human Figure 9 CSS reproduces at r = 0.9999).

2. Process of collecting the data

2.1 Project goals

The long-term goal is to systematically measure congressional sentiment toward the possible use of military force in all crises relevant to the United States throughout history. Version 1 gauged lawmaker sentiment in nearly two hundred crises via the Congressional Record between 1945 and 2022; Version 2 extends the domain in both temporal directions (1873–2026) and across communication media, and replaces exploratory single-model labeling with a three-model, two-instrument ensemble applied to every statement. Version 2 adds continuous war-long coverage, so that sentiment can be observed not only at crisis moments but month by month across an entire conflict.

The question of interest remains how congressional sentiment in military crises influences the crisis behavior of the American president, allies, and adversaries.

2.2 Defining congressional sentiment toward the use of military force

Unchanged from Version 1: the attitudes and preferences expressed by American lawmakers toward the (potential) use of American military force in pursuit of a foreign policy objective in a specific crisis — not latent hawkishness in general. Because members of Congress frequently avoid formal votes on force, the vast majority of lawmaker opinion is conveyed through speech; this informal congressional sentiment is what the dataset measures.

Version 2 extends the referent in one respect. In a crisis file the object is force in that crisis. In a war file, where there is no window, the object is force in that war as of the statement's date, judged against the policy then in effect. The distinction matters: in 1965 supporting escalation is support, while in 1971 opposing a withdrawal deadline is support — both are pro-force positions relative to the status quo of their moment. Each war's instrument states this explicitly and supplies the chronology needed to apply it (Appendix C).

2.3 Temporal, geographic, and media domain

Congressional Record — crises (CR_crises_wide.csv). 150,782 statements, 216 crises, 1873–2022. Parsed floor speeches 1873–2016 from the Stanford Congressional Record collection (Gentzkow, Shapiro, and Taddy 2018); post-2016 speeches and lawmaker tweets (13,518; 2017–2022) collected at UC San Diego cPASS. Retrieval is by keyword match (Terms, e.g. Iraq|Hussein|Saddam) over the crisis period plus the three months prior.

Quorum — crises (Quorum_crises_wide.csv). 382,664 statements by 2,477 lawmakers, 96 crises, 1993–2026, across 13 document types (tweets 177,108; Facebook posts 66,613; press releases 51,956; floor statements 31,062; YouTube transcripts 26,084; committee-hearing statements and verified transcripts; newsletters; letters, Dear-Colleague letters, Instagram, Medium, committee reports). Non-congressional sources that leaked in via keyword match have been removed; only members of the U.S. House and Senate remain.

Congressional Record — wars (CR_wars_wide.csv), new in v2. 157,273 speeches, seven wars, 1914–2016, from the same Stanford hein-bound and hein-daily collections. Retrieval is by war-level keyword rather than crisis-level: WWI german; WWII german, japan; Korea korea; Vietnam viet, indochin, cambodi, lao; Gulf iraq, kuwait, saddam, persian gulf; Afghanistan afghan, taliban; Iraq iraq. All matches across the war's entire span are retained, and the corpus files in 03_corpora/ record which term hit in their matched_terms column (matched_terms is not one of the 102 master columns). This corpus includes Extensions of Remarks (19,113) — text inserted into the Record but not spoken on the floor — flagged in document_type so it can be excluded.

Quorum — wars (Quorum_wars_wide.csv), new in v2. 102,390 statements, two wars, 2001–2021, retrieved from the Quorum API on iraq* and afghan*/taliban* and normalised to the shared schema.

Two coverage limits are worth stating plainly. CR Afghanistan stops at 2016-09-08, where hein-daily coverage ends, not where the war ended. And the Spanish-American War corpus (12,131 speeches, 1895–1902) is built but not classified — no instrument was written for it; it ships in 03_corpora/ for future use.

2.4 The crisis universe

Before any speech could be coded, a universe of crises in which the use of American military force might reasonably have been considered had to be identified. This is the list in CSUMF_Crises_v2.csv.

The aspiration was to be as comprehensive as possible — not merely to reproduce an existing event list, but to capture every occasion on which force was seriously on the table. Three sources were combined:

  1. International Crisis Behavior (ICB). ICB codes roughly seventy crises in which the United States is a crisis actor. That list alone is too narrow, because it omits "dogs that didn't bark" — cases where military action was considered and rejected. The 1975 Fall of Saigon is the clearest example: ICB does not code the U.S. as a crisis actor, yet the omission of force was not for lack of serious consideration, and there is good reason to think the administration's restraint owed substantially to congressional opposition to re-engagement in Indochina after the 1973 Paris Accords.

    To capture such cases, the universe includes all ICB crises in which at least one crisis actor experienced economic or greater U.S. involvement — UNSINV ≥ 4 at the actor level, which also sweeps in propaganda involvement, covert involvement, semi-military involvement (military aid or advisers without participation in fighting), and direct military involvement. Economic involvement signals that the U.S. had some stake in the outcome even where its interest was too small to justify kinetic intervention; requiring that minimum eliminates crises in which American interests were negligible. Frederick et al. (2021) and Schub (2022) take similar approaches to identifying U.S. opportunities to use force. This yields 141 ICB-defined crises between 1945 and 2016.

  2. Correlates of War Militarized Interstate Disputes (MIDs). Every MID in which the U.S. was an actor was examined and included, excepting only the most de minimis — fishing disputes and inadvertent airspace violations.

  3. Hand-added cases. Non-interstate crises (Somalia in the early 1990s, ISIS in the mid-2010s), crises after 2017 (the 2020 Soleimani strike), major decisions taken during a war (the Iraq and Afghanistan surge decisions of early 2007 and late 2009), and ICB crises where the U.S. was not even economically involved but where documentary evidence shows intervention was weighed. The White House followed Russia's 2008 invasion of Georgia closely and consciously ruled out direct intervention, for instance.

Together these yield 191 crises between 1945 and 2022 in the original universe. Version 2 adds twenty-nine pre-1945 crises — from the Virginius Affair (1873) to Pearl Harbor (1941) — and the post-2022 crises, bringing the released total to 317 crisis × corpus rows.

Crises are global. Congressional attention tracks the major lines of American foreign policy: a ring around the Sino-Soviet bloc during the Cold War (Berlin, the Middle East, Korea, Vietnam, the Taiwan Strait), Cuba and Central America, the former Yugoslavia, and the greater Middle East from the 1990s on. Many of today's adversaries carry crisis histories running the whole length of the series — China from the Chinese Civil War through the Taiwan Strait crises of 1955, 1958, and 1996 to Taiwan tensions in the 2020s; North Korea from 1950 to the recent nuclear crises; Russia from the post-1945 confrontations over Iran and Turkey to the war in Ukraine.

2.5 Coding Procedure I — human coding

The human coding was carried out at the University of California, San Diego's Center for Peace and Security Studies (cPASS) between 2019 and 2023, with the bulk of the labeling done in 2020–2022. It covers 29,832 statements in the Congressional Record corpus and is released unchanged in the human_* columns.

Data-generating process

Speeches come from the Congressional Record and range from a couple of sentences of procedural remarks to thousands of words spanning several pages. Parsed speeches for 1873–2016 come from the Stanford Social Science Data Collection (Gentzkow, Shapiro, and Taddy 2018); cPASS collected post-2016 speeches directly, along with all lawmaker tweets from official accounts for 2017–2022.

From that corpus, potentially relevant speeches were identified by keyword search during the crisis period and the three months preceding it. Across all crises this produced over one hundred thousand matches — far more than could be read by hand.

Why hand-coding, and why only part of the corpus

When the dataset was being built, automated text-analysis tools were not yet good enough to measure policy prescriptions (Schub 2022), so qualitative hand-coding was the only option. That put a full manual review of every match beyond available resources. Coding was therefore concentrated on the lawmakers whose positions matter most.

Foreign Policy Elite (FP-elite) lawmakers — roughly forty per Congress:

Senate: Majority Leader; Minority Leader; Chairman and Ranking Member of Foreign Relations, Armed Services, Intelligence, and Appropriations; all members of Foreign Relations; all members of Armed Services.

House: Speaker; Minority Leader; Chairman and Ranking Member of Foreign Affairs, Armed Services, Intelligence, and Appropriations.

Where the speaker was in this group, the coding sheet's Evaluate column was flagged "Yes" and the speech was read. For a handful of crises every speech was hand-labeled regardless of speaker, because the underlying speech data lacked speaker identification for those periods, making FP-elite identification impossible.

The coding process

Each speech was read by a single coder, with coders assigned to specific crises and world regions — students with background in Latin America drew crises there, as did students focused on the Middle East and East Asia. Recruitment favoured existing knowledge of world politics and regional expertise.

On assignment, coders read a general background of the dispute to ensure contextual knowledge: the ICB narrative for ICB-based crises, comparable material (e.g. an encyclopedia entry) otherwise. Coders were encouraged to consult one another and were active in requesting further resources and clarification.

Each crisis had its own online spreadsheet containing every keyword-matched speech from the crisis window and the three months prior. The matched keyword was highlighted in capitals and stars (*****IRAQ****) so that coders could locate the relevant passage inside very long speeches.

The relevance question put to coders was:

"Suppose you were an adviser to the president working in the White House. In the context of the crisis at hand, would this speech give you an idea of how this member of Congress felt toward potentially using military force?"

If not, the speech was marked irrelevant. If it was relevant, the coder marked any of ten sentiment dummies — five supportive, five oppositional — asking whether the speaker seemingly advocates for (or against) the use of American military force in general, ground forces, air assets, naval assets, and aid or sanctions, in that crisis.

Coders could mark more than one. A 2014 speech supporting the bombing of ISIS might be marked as supporting force in general and air assets specifically, while simultaneously opposing ground troops — the items are not mutually exclusive, and that is the point of the instrument.

The released data carry the eight force items. The aid/sanctions pair was collected but is not part of the force-sentiment measures, since aid and sanctions are alternatives to force rather than uses of it.

Quality control and reliability

Coders were paid for their time rather than the number of speeches evaluated, deliberately removing any incentive to rush; on average a speech took about six minutes. They received initial feedback on their codings and were then subject to random audits.

Roughly 70% of speeches were marked irrelevant — evidence that coders were discriminating rather than reading sentiment into every keyword match, and the base rate the LLM prompts later inherit.

Ten percent of speeches were double-coded. This yields Cohen's κ = 0.61 excluding irrelevant speeches — "substantial agreement" — and, more tellingly, coders disagreed about whether a speech was supportive versus oppositional in only 1.5% of cases. Disagreement was overwhelmingly about relevance, not direction: the same pattern that reappears between the human coders and the language models (§7).

Acknowledgments

The dataset was developed while writing a dissertation at UC San Diego between 2017 and 2023, and would not have been possible without cPASS under the leadership of Erik Gartzke, which funded a team of undergraduate research assistants over two and a half years. The data gathering, processing, and quality-control process followed the norms of cPASS's Machine Learning for Social Science Lab. Thanks are due to Rex Douglass, Thomas Scherer, and staff director Jason Lopez, and to fellow graduate students including Jack Zhang, Shannon Carcelli, Andres Gannon, Nick Smith, and Kennedy Pitcher.

The undergraduate research assistants were, more than anything else, the key to this research: Gurman Dhaliwal, Vorathip Plengpanit, Natalie Gold, Duy Nguyen, Carlos Paredes, Dillon Cooke, Gianna Pedro, Erika Villarreal Garcia, Manuel Aguilera-Prieto, Christa Cheung, Alexandra Vignau, Luis Favela, Jinneva Santiesteban, Jiayi Li, Kevin Chin, Anurag Asthana, and Cole Reynolds. The project also benefited from financial support from the UC San Diego graduate division, the department of political science, and the Stand Together Trust. Any faults are the author's alone.

No human coding exists for either Quorum file. The hand labels are keyed on Congressional Record speech_ids, which have no counterpart in the Quorum universe, and no crosswalk exists. Of the 29,832 hand-coded statements, 11,001 also fall inside the war corpora and are carried into CR_wars_wide.csv by speech_id match.

2.6 Coding Procedure II — large language model coding

Models

Column prefix Model Provider Inference
luna_ GPT-5.6 Luna OpenAI Batch API; reasoning_effort="none"; strict JSON-schema output
grok_ grok-4-fast-non-reasoning xAI Batch API (OpenAI-compatible); temperature=0; JSON object
gemini_ gemini-3.5-flash-lite Google Batch API; temperature=0; JSON MIME type

All three labs code every statement under both instruments. Batch APIs were used throughout for cost (roughly 50% off list at OpenAI and Google) and prompt caching; the war runs measured 25–68% cache hit rates on the fixed prompt prefix.

The two instruments

8-item (binary CSUMF) instrument. The Version 1 human instrument, verbatim: eight independent binary items — {General, Ground, Air, Naval} × {Support, Opposition} — plus relevant. Items are not mutually exclusive (air strikes yes / ground no scores AirSupp = 1 and GrdOpp = 1).

Tripartite instrument. A single overall label: support / oppose / mixed / irrelevant (numeric 1 / 0 / 0.5 / missing), where mixed means the speaker both supports and opposes force — including support conditioned on congressional authorization. Where the 8-item instrument records which instruments of force a speaker engages, the tripartite instrument records the speaker's overall posture, and is the basis for the ISS/CSS measures in §5.

Prompt design

System prompts (roughly 5,200–11,400 characters) were developed iteratively against the human-coded corpus, each revision bake-off-tested for agreement with the hand codes. Components, in order:

  • Role and blinding. "You are a nonpartisan congressional analyst… You apply a fixed codebook exactly as written." The speaker's name and party are withheld deliberately: judge only the text, never guess partisanship.
  • Wrong-war guard. Because retrieval is by keyword, the crisis country constantly appears inside statements about a different conflict; the prompt's stated "most common error" rule requires the position to concern force in this crisis or war.
  • Relevance gate. Relevant only if at least one item can be answered yes; the CR prompts state the empirical base rate ("58% of speeches were marked IRRELEVANT… Do not manufacture a stance to avoid returning one").
  • Support and opposition criteria — bulleted operationalizations mirroring the human codebook, including the rule that neutral War Powers procedural talk is not opposition.
  • Carve-outs: sanctions hawk (maximal pressure plus explicit force opposition = opposition); regime delegitimization (denunciation alone is support only absent an explicit force rejection).
  • Branch specificity: ground/air/naval items only when that instrument is explicitly engaged.
  • Attribution and tone: own position only, not quoted or rebutted views; sarcasm read for intent; no hindsight.
  • Corpus conventions (CR only): OCR artefacts — masked country names (**** COUNTRY ****), comma-to-period substitution, page-split fragments.
  • Parliamentary vehicles: opposing an amendment or motion is not opposing the force.
  • Worked examples spanning the hard cases.
  • Output contract: JSON only — the labels, a key_quote (shortest verbatim justifying phrase), and a confidence integer 1–5.

Quorum adaptation — exactly three edits. The Quorum prompts differ from the CR prompts by three code-asserted edits and nothing else: the input framing describes a congressional statement of any register; the 58%-irrelevant prior is neutralized to a plain relevance test; and the OCR conventions block is replaced by a register/length note. All items, carve-outs, examples, and the output contract are byte-identical.

War adaptation — additive only. The war prompts append to the crisis instruments rather than modifying them: a war-long referent block defining support relative to current policy, a per-war chronology, and carve-outs for that conflict's characteristic traps (Appendix C). Every 8-item war prompt is quorum_8item.SYSTEM_8Q byte-for-byte plus the appended block, so war and crisis labels remain comparable.

Context provided with every request

Each user message contains: the crisis name and date span (crisis files) or the war and its span (war files); the statement's own date, and its document type or chamber; a hand-written chronology of U.S. action, introduced as "WHAT THE UNITED STATES WAS ACTUALLY DOING (use the statement's date together with the timeline below to determine what action was under debate AT THE TIME OF THIS STATEMENT…)" — sourced from crisis_context.csv for crises and from the per-war timelines in Appendices C and D; the task sentence; and the statement text in full. The speaker's identity is never provided.

Ensemble

Computed from the three labs at build time, never separately queried. Tripartite: the majority label where at least two labs agree; where all three differ, the ensemble is irrelevant — a three-way split is treated as an unreliable reading, not as a position. 8-item: each item is 1 iff at least two labs scored it 1. Where fewer than two labs have valid labels the ensemble is degraded — per-lab null counts, not the ensemble columns, are the correct coverage measure.

3. The 2x2 design

Every CSUMF master answers the same question about a single statement by a member of Congress: does this speaker support or oppose the use of American military force? Because the question is constant, the 102 columns are identical across all four files, in the same order, with the same value conventions. They concatenate directly:

df = pd.concat([pd.read_csv(f) for f in
                ["CR_crises_wide.csv", "Quorum_crises_wide.csv",
                 "CR_wars_wide.csv",   "Quorum_wars_wide.csv"]])

The corpus column (CR, Quorum, CR_war, Quorum_war) tells you which file a row came from.

The four files exist because they cross two independent design choices: where the text comes from and how time is sampled.

Crises — ICB windows Wars — continuous
Congressional Record
floor speech only		 CR_crises_wide.csv
150,782 rows · 216 crises · 1873–2022		 CR_wars_wide.csv
157,273 rows · 7 wars · 1914–2016
Quorum
all public output		 Quorum_crises_wide.csv
382,664 rows · 96 crises · 1993–2026		 Quorum_wars_wide.csv
102,390 rows · 2 wars · 2001–2021

793,109 statements in total.

3.1 The first axis — medium

Congressional Record files contain what members said on the floor (plus, in the war files, Extensions of Remarks — text inserted into the Record but not spoken aloud). The CR is the only source reaching back to the nineteenth century, and it is a formal, deliberative register: long, argued, addressed to colleagues.

Quorum files contain everything a member said in public — floor statements, press releases, newsletters, committee remarks, and social media. The register is far wider, from a 4,000-word committee transcript to a fourteen-word tweet, and it is addressed to constituents rather than colleagues. Quorum coverage begins in the 1990s and is thin before roughly 2009.

Dataset Floor speech Press releases Social media Other
CR crises 137,264 — 13,518 (tweets, post-2016) —
CR wars 138,160 — — 19,113 Extensions of Remarks
Quorum crises 31,127 51,246 261,462 38,829
Quorum wars 42,782 21,774 34,690 3,144

document_type and the boolean is_social carry this. Composition is not constant over time: in the Quorum files the mix shifts from press-release-dominated before 2009 to social-media-dominated after 2015. A pooled Quorum time series is therefore partly measuring a change of medium. Hold document_type fixed (e.g. == "Floor Statement") when that matters, which also makes a Quorum series directly comparable to a CR one.

3.2 The second axis — crises versus wars

Crisis files sample time in windows. Each row is a statement matched to one of the International Crisis Behavior crises, retrieved from up to 90 days before the crisis start through its end. The unit is the crisis; crisis_id joins to CSUMF_Crises_v2.csv, and crisis-level scores live in CSUMF_CSS_v2.csv. This design gives broad coverage — 216 crises over 150 years — at the cost of seeing only bursts.

War files sample time continuously. Every keyword-matched statement across the entire span of a war is classified, month by month. There is no window and no crisis id (crisis_id is null; crisis_name carries the war). This gives a dense series — you can watch a war's politics turn — for a small number of conflicts.

The same underlying speech can appear in both a crisis file and a war file. They are different samples of the same universe, not disjoint sets; deduplicate on the underlying speech id if you need statement-level independence.

Coverage at a glance

Dataset Span Units Human-coded
CR crises 1873-12-01 → 2022-03-16 216 crises 29,832
Quorum crises 1993-05-26 → 2026-07-29 96 crises 0
CR wars 1914-01-12 → 2016-09-08 7 wars 11,001
Quorum wars 2001-01-22 → 2021-08-31 2 wars 0

The seven CR wars: World War I (15,916), World War II (15,916), Korea (7,694), Vietnam (61,447), Persian Gulf (7,279), Afghanistan (20,705), Iraq (34,817). The two Quorum wars: Afghanistan (65,276), Iraq (37,114).

Two wars are classified over less than their retrieved corpus, because the force question ceases to be live at a definite date: WWI stops at the armistice (1918-11-11), excluding 5,905 later speeches — the Versailles and League debate is a treaty question, not a use-of-force question; WWII stops at the surrender ceremony (1945-09-02), excluding 1,002. Excluded speeches remain in 03_corpora/ and can be coded later with a purpose-built instrument.

3.3 What is identical

Regardless of file, every row carries the same label block: the human codes where they exist, each of three model families under two instruments, and the computed ensemble — with a 1–5 confidence and a verbatim key_quote on every model label. Section 4 documents the columns once; the appendices give each dataset's exact prompts, which is the only part that genuinely differs.

4. Column reference (102 columns)

The 102 columns are identical in name, type and order across all four masters. They are laid out in reading order — what the row is, when, who said it, what they said, then the measures — with the per-lab detail and the constant provenance columns last:

28–29 document document_type, is_social

55–96 labs grok_*, luna_*, gemini_*, fourteen columns each

The subsections below group the same columns thematically rather than positionally.

Populated in CR_crises_wide.csv (29,832) and CR_wars_wide.csv (11,001). Empty in both Quorum files — the hand labels are keyed on hein-bound Congressional Record speech ids, which have no counterpart in the Quorum universe, and no crosswalk exists.

4.4 Model labels (42) — three blocks of 14

For each of grok, luna, gemini:

Column Values
{lab}_tri_class support / oppose / mixed / irrelevant
{lab}_tri_num 1.0 / 0.0 / 0.5 / NaN
{lab}_tri_conf 1–5

{lab}_8_GenSupp … {lab}_8_NavOpp 0/1 (8 columns)

4.5 Ensemble (10)

ensemble_tri_class, ensemble_tri_num, ensemble_8_GenSupp … ensemble_8_NavOpp.

The ensemble rule is identical in all four masters. Tripartite: the majority label where at least two of the three labs agree; where all three differ, the ensemble is irrelevant — a three-way split is treated as an unreliable reading, not as a position. 8-item: an item is 1 if at least two labs scored it 1.

Earlier builds of the war files resolved three-way splits to mixed; they were rebuilt on the crisis rule so that a single convention holds across the release.

4.6 fp_elite, committee assignment, and party leadership (6)

fp_elite

fp_elite is derived from the committee and leadership columns, on the CSUMF definition of a foreign-policy elite:

Speaker of the House, or Senate Majority Leader, or Minority Leader of either chamber, or chair or ranking member of Foreign Affairs/Relations, Armed Services, Appropriations or Intelligence, or any member of the Senate Foreign Relations or Senate Armed Services committee.

Note the asymmetry, which is deliberate: rank-and-file membership counts in the Senate but not the House, because SFRC and SASC seats carry standing that House committee seats do not.

fp_elite is null wherever the committee columns are null, so False means "covered by the source and not an elite", not "unknown". It is now populated in all four masters — the war files previously carried an empty column.

Master fp_elite True Share of covered rows
CR_crises_wide.csv 38,024 30.2%
Quorum_crises_wide.csv 93,972 24.8%
CR_wars_wide.csv 39,009 26.2%
Quorum_wars_wide.csv 23,944 24.3%

Committee and leadership columns

Five columns describing the speaker's institutional position at the time of the statement. They sit immediately after fp_elite, and are present in all four masters on the same definitions.

Column Values
cmte_foreign_affairs_relations Chair / Ranking_Member / Member / Not_Member / null
cmte_armed_services same
cmte_appropriations same
cmte_intelligence same
leadership Speaker / Majority_Leader / Minority_Leader / Not_Leader / null

Null means no source coverage for that Congress, so absence of an assignment cannot be distinguished from non-membership. Not_Member / Not_Leader mean covered and not holding the position. Coerce accordingly: != "Not_Member" is only meaningful where the column is non-null.

Sources

Committee assignments come from Charles Stewart III and Jonathan Woon, Congressional Committee Assignments, 103rd to 115th Congresses (ICPSR 3371 for Congresses 1–102, plus Stewart's House and Senate assignment workbooks for 103–115). We are grateful for that dataset; any errors in the mapping onto CSUMF are ours. Congresses 116–119 were compiled for this release from published per-Congress committee rosters, with the Congress 116 Intelligence roster taken from the Congressional Directory. Leadership for Congresses 80–117 comes from Stewart's leadership files and the same workbooks; Congresses 43–79 and 118–119 were compiled from the historical record.

Mapping decisions

  • House Foreign Affairs and International Relations are one family. The committee was renamed twice; it is treated as continuous.
  • Military Affairs and Naval Affairs map to armed services. Both chambers ran separate Military and Naval Affairs committees until the 1946 Legislative Reorganization Act merged them into Armed Services. Pre-1947 members of either are coded armed_services.
  • Intelligence begins in the 1970s. Before the select committees existed the column is Not_Member, not null.
  • Membership is resolved at Congress level and is deliberately over-inclusive. A member who held a seat at any point during a Congress is coded as holding it for the whole Congress, even though Stewart records exact appointment and termination dates and 43% of assignments do not span a full Congress. This is a decision, not an oversight: it matches the convention used in the original CSUMF coding. Measured cost is small — statements in the over-credited window fall inside the member's true service dates 80.3% of the time, against 84.3% for assignments that are not over-credited.
  • Chair and ranking member are the rank-1 member of the majority and minority party respectively, taken from Stewart's party-status field rather than inferred from chamber control.
  • Whips are not leadership. leadership covers the Speaker and the floor leaders only.

Coverage

Master Committee data Leadership data On ≥1 FP committee Chair/ranking rows
CR_crises_wide.csv 83.5% 83.5% 49.5% 11,020
Quorum_crises_wide.csv 99.2% 99.2% 63.6% 24,946
CR_wars_wide.csv 94.7% 94.7% 52.2% 10,900
Quorum_wars_wide.csv 96.1% 96.1% 59.5% 6,486

Coverage is limited by icpsr (or, for Congresses 116–119, by name and state), so the gap is concentrated in the same pre-1945 rows that lack a Voteview match.

Known limits

  • Two leadership posts are null for their pre-institutional periods. The House minority leader is left null before Congress 51 — the position was not institutionalised, and pre-1899 values are the minority party's nominee for Speaker, the standard proxy. Senate floor leaders are null before Congress 66 (1919): the posts did not formally exist, Democrats designated a floor leader from 1920 and Republicans from 1925, and the earlier party conference chair is a materially different office. These are left empty rather than back-projected.
  • Member identifiers are reconciled to Voteview ICPSR before joining, with a name fallback within Congress and chamber; roughly 0.5% remain unresolved and are coded null.
  • Congresses 116–119 join on name and state, because Voteview's member file ends at Congress 117. 972 of the 989 compiled roster entries were confirmed against independent membership records.

5. Derived measures: ISS, CSS, and CSUMF_CSS_v2.csv

The masters hold statement-level labels. Analysis normally happens one or two levels up.

5.1 The two aggregation steps

The masters hold statement-level labels. Analysis normally happens one or two levels up. The two instruments are scored differently at the statement level (step 1) and identically from the speaker average onward (steps 3–4).

Step 1 — per statement, score the statement

The two instruments differ here, because their units differ. The 8-item instrument counts instruments of force, so one statement can contribute several units; the tripartite instrument records one overall posture, so one statement contributes exactly one observation.

8-item instrument. Sum the four support items and the four oppose items:

supp_stmt = GenSupp + GrdSupp + AirSupp + NavSupp        # 0-4
opp_stmt  = GenOpp  + GrdOpp  + AirOpp  + NavOpp         # 0-4

Tripartite instrument. Score the class on the 0–1 support scale:

*_tri_class score
support 1
mixed 0.5
oppose 0
irrelevant — dropped —

Step 2 — Individual Support Score (ISS), one per speaker per unit

8-item — pool the raw counts across all of that speaker's statements in the slice, then divide:

ISS(speaker) = SUM(supp_stmt) / ( SUM(supp_stmt) + SUM(opp_stmt) )

Tripartite — average the speaker's statement scores:

ISS(speaker) = MEAN( score )        over that speaker's non-irrelevant statements

Speakers with no scoreable statement are dropped; that test subsumes the relevance filter, so no separate filter on relevant is applied.

These two are not interchangeable, and the difference is load-bearing in both directions.

For the 8-item measure, pooling the item counts is not the same as computing a ratio per statement and averaging those ratios: a statement marking three support items contributes three units to the pooled sum but only one observation to a mean-of-ratios. Getting this wrong shifts roughly a quarter of 8-item CSS cells by 0.001–0.01.

For the tripartite measure the averaging is deliberate, so that every statement counts once. Running the tripartite classes through the 8-item pooling instead — scoring mixed as one support unit and one oppose unit — would give a mixed statement twice the weight of a clear one, and changes 2,439 of the 19,971 cells in CSUMF_CSS_v2.csv (by up to 0.028). The two instruments share everything from step 3 onward, but not step 1.

Step 3 — Congressional Support Score (CSS), one per unit

CSS = mean_over_speakers( ISS ) - 0.5          in [-0.5, +0.5]

Positive is net hawkish, negative net dovish. Averaging over speakers rather than statements is deliberate: a member who speaks forty times should not outweigh forty members who speak once.

Step 4 — proration on thin slices

Where a slice rests on few speakers, the score is shrunk toward zero:

CSS_reported = CSS_raw * (n / T)          when n < T
CSS_reported = CSS_raw                    when n >= T

n is the speaker count in that slice — the value in the companion _n_ column.

Slice Threshold T
Party splits — *_dem, *_rep — for every coder, human included 3
FP-elite slices *_fp, and all human non-party slices 5
Machine all and pre slices 25

The unprorated value is recoverable as CSS_reported x T / n whenever 0 < n < T.

The machine threshold of 25 is higher than the human 5 because the models code every speaker in a crisis rather than the FP-elite subset, so a machine slice with only a handful of scoreable speakers is a much stronger signal of a thin crisis than the same count under human coding.

5.2 Slice definitions

Slice Rows included
all every statement in the crisis window
all_dem / all_rep party == "D" / party == "R"
pre statements dated before US_force_init; where the crisis has no US_force_init, all statements
pre_fp pre restricted to fp_elite == True
pre_dem / pre_rep pre restricted by party

pre is the specification used for Figure 9: sentiment measured after force has begun is contaminated by rally effects, so the pre-force window is the cleaner test.

Quorum CSS is computed only over single-speaker-attributable media — Floor Statement, Tweet, Facebook Post, Press Release. Committee transcripts, newsletters, and letters are excluded because they cannot be cleanly attributed to one member. CR CSS uses all statements. This is why n_statements_css can be lower than n_statements in CSUMF_Crises_v2.csv.

5.3 CSUMF_CSS_v2.csv - 317 rows x 134 columns

One row per crisis x corpus (crisno, corpus). Crisis-level only: there is deliberately no war-level CSS file, because a war's CSS is a time series rather than a scalar. Those live as monthly aggregates in 04_figures/*.csv.

Identifier and crisis-level context (8): crisno, corpus, crisname, US_high_act, escalation_label, continuing_war, Prez_party, Vietnam_War.

CSS columns (126) follow one naming pattern:

{coder}_{instrument}_css_{slice}      and its companion      {coder}_{instrument}_n_{slice}
Part Values
coder human, grok, luna, gemini, ensemble
instrument tri, 8item (human is 8item only)
slice all, all_dem, all_rep, pre, pre_fp, pre_dem, pre_rep

Every _css_ column has a matching _n_ giving the speaker count behind it. Use it. A CSS resting on three speakers is not comparable to one resting on eighty, and _n_ is always populated even where _css_ is null — so a null CSS means "no qualifying speakers," not missing data. Human CSS is present for 41–46% of crises (the hand-coded subset); model CSS for 65–94%, varying by instrument and how narrow the slice is.

Five crises with zero statements (crisnos 380, 458, 10112, 10126, 10131) are carried with n = 0 and CSS = 0, preserving the congressional-silence convention.

Verification

The formulas above were re-derived from the statement-level masters and checked against the released file: 19,656 of 19,656 CSS cells reproduce to within 5x10^-7, and 100% of speaker counts match exactly. The reconstruction script is audit_css.py.

6. Crisis metadata: CSUMF_Crises_v2.csv

One row per crisis (317), the lookup table for crisis_id in the crisis masters and for crisno in the CSS file.

Column Meaning
crisno, crisname crisis id and name
corpus which master the crisis appears in (CR / Quorum)
start_date, end_date crisis window
US_force_init the pre-use-of-force cutoff — the date U.S. force began. Populated for 28.1% of crises; null where the U.S. never used force, which is itself informative
US_high_act, escalation_label 1–15 escalation level and its label (Threats, Show of Force, 6-25 US deaths, 1000+ US deaths, …)
continuing_war 1 = crisis inside an ongoing war (dropped from Figure 9 per the paper)
Prez_party ICPSR code of the president's party (100 = D, 200 = R)
Vietnam_War flag for Vietnam-era crises
period, era coarse time buckets
n_statements, n_statements_css, n_speakers corpus size for the crisis; n_statements_css counts only those contributing to CSS
first_statement, last_statement observed date range, which can be narrower than the window
Terms the pipe-separated keyword string used for retrieval (e.g. Romania|Rumania)

Terms is worth noting for anyone assessing recall: it records exactly which strings pulled the crisis corpus, the crisis analogue of the per-war keyword table in Appendix C.

The war files have no equivalent lookup table — a war's metadata is carried inline on every row (crisis_name, us_force_init, start_date, end_date), because there are only seven of them.

7. Validation and known properties

Against the 11,001 human-labeled speeches overlapping the war corpora:

Measure Value
Krippendorff's α — ISS interval, human + 3 labs 0.856
Krippendorff's α — 3 labs only 0.910
Human–ensemble r, tripartite ISS 0.80
Human–ensemble r, 8-item net support 0.83
Inter-lab r 0.91–0.93

By war (human–ensemble r): Iraq 0.84 · Gulf 0.79 · Vietnam 0.78 · Afghanistan 0.71 · Korea 0.39.

Korea is a known, unresolved outlier (α 0.48 against 0.69–0.89 elsewhere). The gap is in relevance, not direction: 159 speeches the human coders marked irrelevant are coded directional by all three labs. A sample suggests the models are often defensible — e.g. Majority Leader Lucas, 1950-06-27, "I wholeheartedly concur in what has been done," coded irrelevant by hand — which points to a narrower crisis-specific relevance convention in the original coding rather than model error. Flag it; do not pool Korea silently.

Relevance is the weak joint throughout (φ ≈ 0.40–0.43 human-vs-lab against 0.70–0.78 lab-vs-lab); directional agreement is strong everywhere.

Reproducibility. Re-running luna reproduces ~93% of its own tripartite labels (reasoning_effort="none", no temperature control); grok and gemini at temperature 0 reproduce 98–99%. Aggregate ISS moves by ~0.004 — inside month-to-month noise, and an argument for using the ensemble rather than any single lab.

8. How the appendices are organised

Each dataset was coded with the same two instruments but a different framing, because the referent differs: a crisis file asks about force in that crisis, a war file asks about force at that date in that war. Appendices A–D give, verbatim:

  • the tripartite system prompt and the 8-item system prompt;
  • the context block injected into every user message — a per-crisis action/timeline note in the crisis files, a per-war chronology in the war files;
  • a rendered example user message.

Everything below is pulled directly from the run modules and is byte-identical to what was sent.

Appendix A — CR_crises_wide.csv (Congressional Record × crises)

Congressional Record floor speeches, retrieved by keyword match per crisis, coded against the ICB crisis they were retrieved for. Text is OCR'd and carries the artefacts of that (commas rendered as periods; the crisis country masked as **** COUNTRY ****), which the prompt addresses explicitly. The relevance posture is strong: human coders marked 58% of this corpus irrelevant, and the prompt states that base rate.

Context injected per statement: the crisis's ACTION/TIMELINE note from data/crisis_context.csv, selected by crisis_id, so the model can date the statement against what the U.S. was actually doing.

A.1 Tripartite system prompt

You are a nonpartisan congressional analyst coding U.S. floor speeches for a political science project on war powers. The speaker's name and party are withheld; judge only the text, and never guess partisanship.

You are shown ONE floor speech, the international crisis it concerns, and a factual note on what military action the United States was actually considering or taking at the time. Assign EXACTLY ONE overall label for the speaker's position on the use of American military force IN THAT CRISIS: support, oppose, mixed, or irrelevant.

support = LEANS TOWARD SUPPORTING U.S. military force in this crisis
- Explicitly endorses strikes, intervention, or deployment; praises force taken or threatened
- Hawkish framing ("we must act," "all options on the table," "show strength") -- in an active crisis these imply force even without the word
- Frames the adversary as an urgent/intolerable threat requiring a forceful response
- Criticizes restraint, caution, or withdrawal as weakness or appeasement
- Emphasizes the adversary's repression/atrocities/proliferation to justify a harder U.S. posture
- Presses for a "strong"/"maximum pressure" deal, warns against a "weak deal" or premature sanctions relief, or urges keeping military options on the table

oppose = LEANS TOWARD OPPOSING U.S. military force in this crisis
- Explicitly opposes or warns against strikes, intervention, or deployment
- Calls for diplomacy, restraint, or de-escalation instead of force
- Raises the costs of force as a reason NOT to act: casualties, expense, quagmire, war-weariness, wider war
- Argues escalation would be counterproductive, or would wreck a negotiated settlement
- Invokes a cautionary precedent (Vietnam, Iraq, "another endless war") against force
- Argues the crisis is not a vital U.S. interest, or urges sanctions/aid/pressure INSTEAD OF force

mixed = genuinely BOTH support and opposition
- Backs one instrument but rejects another (e.g., supports air strikes but firmly opposes ground troops)
- Endorses the action but raises serious SUBSTANTIVE objections; or supports force only on conditions
- Cannot reasonably be placed in support or oppose

irrelevant = takes NO position on U.S. military force in this crisis
- Procedural, ceremonial, or a different topic; the country/crisis appears only incidentally

THE MOST COMMON ERROR IS CODING THE WRONG WAR. These speeches were retrieved by keyword match, so the crisis country turns up constantly inside speeches about a DIFFERENT conflict. A 2006 speech that argues for 900 words about withdrawing from Iraq and mentions Syria once expresses no position on American force against Syria: it is irrelevant. Hawkishness or dovishness about any OTHER conflict is not a position on THIS crisis, however forcefully expressed.

RELEVANCE -- READ THIS CAREFULLY. This corpus was pulled by keyword match against the crisis country, and human coders marked 58% of speeches IRRELEVANT. Irrelevant is the single most common correct answer -- most hits are incidental (the country in a list, a statute's name, a speech on trade or human rights with no bearing on force). Do NOT assume relevance, and do NOT manufacture a stance to avoid returning irrelevant. (This differs from a Quorum-style feed of already-on-topic statements; here, most speeches are off-topic.)

DO NOT MISTAKE THESE FOR OPPOSITION:
- A demand for congressional authorization, or neutral procedural/legislative-history discussion of War Powers compliance (the 60-day clock, confirming a resolution is consistent with a statutory requirement), is NOT opposition unless the speaker voices a substantive objection to the force itself. Process talk alone is not opposition.
- An otherwise-supportive speech that merely acknowledges costs, risks, or difficulty is still support -- do not downgrade it to oppose or mixed for a passing caveat.
- Opposing a specific amendment, substitute, or procedural motion is not opposition to the underlying use of force.

TWO CARVE-OUTS:
- SANCTIONS HAWK: pressure or maximalist-deal rhetoric COMBINED WITH explicit opposition to military force is oppose, not support. Hawkishness about sanctions is not hawkishness about force.
- REGIME DELEGITIMIZATION: denouncing the adversary regime is support only where no explicit rejection of force accompanies it; denunciation plus "but war is not the answer" is oppose.

ATTRIBUTION: code the speaker's OWN position, not a view they quote or paraphrase in order to rebut it. SARCASM: read rhetorical questions for intended meaning ("Shall we send another generation to die in the desert?" is oppose).

TEXT CONVENTIONS (OCR'd Congressional Record): the crisis country is masked as **** COUNTRY ****, and the mask can sit inside a word ("the **** SYRIA ****ns" = "the Syrians") -- treat it as an ordinary mention. Commas became periods, so sentences look chopped ("Madam Speaker. the people have spoken.") -- read as prose. Some entries begin or end mid-sentence; code whatever is expressed, else irrelevant.

Return ONLY a JSON object with: `classification` (exactly one of: support, oppose, mixed, irrelevant); `key_quote` (the shortest verbatim phrase justifying it, "" if irrelevant); `confidence` (integer 1-5: 5 = clear-cut, 3 = leaning but real ambiguity, 1 = genuinely borderline). No prose, no markdown.

A.2 8-item system prompt

You are a congressional analyst coding U.S. congressional floor speeches for a political science project on war powers. You apply a fixed codebook exactly as written.

You will be shown one floor speech and told which international crisis it is being coded against. Record the speaker's expressed policy preferences about the use of American military force IN THAT CRISIS, as eight INDEPENDENT binary items.

The items are not mutually exclusive and do not sum to anything. A speaker who wants air strikes but no ground troops scores 1 on air support AND 1 on ground opposition. Code each item on its own; never force a single overall verdict.

THE MOST COMMON ERROR IS CODING THE WRONG WAR. These speeches were retrieved by keyword match, so the crisis country turns up constantly inside speeches about a DIFFERENT conflict. A 2006 floor speech that argues for 900 words about withdrawing from Iraq, and mentions Syria once as a country worth talking to, expresses no position whatever on American force against Syria: it is irrelevant. Hawkishness or dovishness about Iraq, Vietnam, Afghanistan, Korea, or any other conflict is NOT hawkishness or dovishness about this crisis, however forcefully expressed. Before scoring any item, ask: is the speaker expressing a view about American force directed at THIS crisis, or at some other one? Reserve every item for force committed to the named crisis.

RELEVANCE GATE -- READ THIS SECOND. A speech is relevant only if at least one of the eight items can be answered yes. In the human-coded portion of this corpus, 58% of speeches were marked IRRELEVANT. That is the base rate you should expect. These speeches were pulled by keyword match against the crisis country, so most hits are incidental: the country appears in a list, in the name of a statute, as background to a different argument, or in a speech about trade, immigration, or human rights with no bearing on American force. Irrelevant is the single most common correct answer. Do not manufacture a stance to avoid returning one. If relevant is false, set all eight items to 0.

The speaker's name and party are withheld from you deliberately. Code the text. Do not guess at partisanship and do not let an inferred party lean the classification.

WHAT COUNTS AS SUPPORTING FORCE (general support item):
- Endorses strikes, intervention, or deployment; praises a strike already carried out
- Hawkish framing: "all options on the table," "show strength," "we must act," "cannot
  stand by" -- in an active crisis these imply force even without the word
- Frames the adversary as an urgent or intolerable threat: "must be stopped," "cannot be
  tolerated"
- Criticizes restraint, caution, or withdrawal as weakness, appeasement, or dithering
- Urges the President to act more forcefully, or faults him for acting too slowly
- Emphasizes the adversary's repression, executions, terrorism, or proliferation to
  justify a harder American posture
- Frames the regime as fundamentally illegitimate or untrustworthy
- Presses for a "strong," "real," or "maximum pressure" deal on stringent terms
- Warns against a "weak deal" or premature sanctions relief
- Calls for keeping military options on the table during negotiations

WHAT COUNTS AS OPPOSING FORCE (general opposition item):
- Opposes or warns against strikes, intervention, or deployment
- Calls for diplomacy, negotiation, restraint, or de-escalation instead of force
- Raises costs: casualties, expense, quagmire, war-weariness, burden on troops
- Argues escalation would be counterproductive or self-defeating
- Argues force would wreck a negotiated settlement or diplomatic opening
- Invokes a cautionary precedent (Vietnam, Iraq, "another endless war")
- Substantively opposes force, OR withholds support UNLESS Congress authorizes it because
  the speaker objects to unilateral force. But NEUTRAL procedural or legislative-history
  discussion of War Powers compliance -- noting the 60-day clock, confirming a resolution
  is consistent with a statutory authorization requirement, reporting/timeline mechanics --
  is NOT opposition when the speaker voices no substantive objection to the force itself.
  Do not code an opposition item for process talk alone.
- Argues the crisis is not a vital American interest, or is someone else's to handle
- Urges sanctions, aid, or pressure INSTEAD OF military force
- Warns of unintended consequences, regional spillover, or civilian harm

TWO CARVE-OUTS, BOTH IMPORTANT:
- SANCTIONS HAWK. Pressure rhetoric and maximalist deal terms COMBINED WITH explicit
  opposition to military force is opposition, not support. Hawkishness about sanctions is
  not hawkishness about force. Score general opposition, not general support.
- REGIME DELEGITIMIZATION. Denouncing the adversary regime is support only where no
  explicit rejection of force accompanies it. Denunciation plus "but war is not the
  answer" is opposition.

BRANCH-SPECIFIC ITEMS. Score ground, air, and naval only where the speech engages that
instrument specifically -- troops/boots on the ground/invasion/occupation; air strikes/
bombing/no-fly zone/standoff missiles; carriers/naval blockade/escorting/fleet movements.
General hawkishness with no branch named scores the general item ONLY, leaving all three
branch items at 0. Do not infer a branch from the nature of the crisis.

ATTRIBUTION AND TONE:
- Code the speaker's own position, not one they are quoting, describing, or attributing to
  an opponent, the President, or a foreign government. Speeches that recite an opposing
  view in order to rebut it are common; the rebuttal is the speaker's position.
- Read sarcasm and rhetorical questions for intent, not surface form. "Shall we send
  another generation to die in the desert?" is opposition.
- Conditional support ("I would support strikes if the President came to Congress") scores
  BOTH the relevant support item and general opposition, since it is support conditioned
  on authorization.
- Retrospective criticism of a past war is not, by itself, a position on this crisis.
- Do not add an opposition item to an otherwise-supportive speech merely for a passing mention
  of costs, risks, troop readiness, or conditions. Code an opposition item only where the speaker
  actually argues against using force in this crisis, not where a supporter acknowledges difficulty.
- Do not use hindsight about what actually happened afterward. Code what is on the page.

TEXT CONVENTIONS IN THIS CORPUS. The speeches are OCR'd from the Congressional Record and
carry three artifacts you must read through rather than be confused by:
- The crisis country's name is masked wherever it appears, as **** COUNTRY ****. The mask
  can sit inside a word: "the **** SYRIA ****ns" is "the Syrians", and "Iran**** SYRIA ****
  Nonproliferation Act" is "Iran-Syria Nonproliferation Act". Treat a masked mention as an
  ordinary mention of that country.
- Commas were converted to periods, so sentences look chopped: "Madam Speaker. the people
  have spoken." Read them as ordinary prose.
- Some entries begin or end mid-sentence because the record was split by page. Code
  whatever position is expressed in the fragment; if none is, it is irrelevant.

PARLIAMENTARY VEHICLES. Opposing a specific amendment, substitute, or procedural motion is not opposition to the underlying use of force. Code the member's stance on force itself, not on the vehicle.

WORKED EXAMPLES (apply the same logic; **** COUNTRY **** is the masked crisis country):
1. "I confirm this resolution is consistent with our requirement that the President obtain statutory authorization for any expanded role of our forces in **** COUNTRY ****; the 60-day War Powers clock is noted." -> Neutral War Powers procedure, no objection to the force. relevant=false; code NO opposition item.
2. "I strongly support the President's decision to strike **** COUNTRY ****, though our forces are stretched thin and this will be costly." -> A supporter noting difficulty. GenSupp=1 only; do NOT add an opposition item for the passing cost/readiness caveat.
3. "I oppose the gentleman's amendment, which ties the President's hands. The underlying resolution authorizing air strikes deserves support." -> Opposes an amendment but backs the force. GenSupp=1, AirSupp=1; the amendment opposition is NOT opposition to force.
4. "We must not send another generation to die in the desert. Diplomacy, not bombs." -> Genuine opposition. GenOpp=1.

Return ONLY a JSON object matching the schema: `relevant`, the eight items, and `key_quote` -- the single shortest verbatim phrase from the speech that best justifies your non-zero items ("" if irrelevant or all 0). Also return `confidence` (integer 1-5): how confident you are that these labels correctly capture the speaker's stance on U.S. military force in THIS crisis (5 = clear-cut and unambiguous; 3 = leaning but with real ambiguity; 1 = genuinely borderline, could reasonably be coded otherwise). No prose, no markdown, no explanation.

A.3 Injected crisis contexts — all crises, verbatim

One ACTION/TIMELINE block per crisis, injected into the user message for every statement in that crisis under both the CR and Quorum crisis instruments (Appendices A and B share this table). Source: data/crisis_context.csv (post-1945) and data/crisis_context_preWWII.csv. Crises with no block coded receive no context paragraph; the model then works from the crisis name, its date span, and the statement date alone.

221 crisis context blocks.

Crisis 1 — Russian Civil War I

ACTION: As part of an Allied intervention after Russia's Bolshevik revolution, President Wilson committed U.S. forces to North Russia (Archangel) and Siberia (Vladivostok). About 5,000 U.S. troops of the 339th Infantry landed at Archangel and were drawn into active combat against Bolshevik forces; some 8,000 troops in Siberia mainly guarded railways amid skirmishes. Combat in North Russia continued past the November 1918 armistice.

TIMELINE:
- 1918-06: U.S. sailors from USS Olympia land at Murmansk
- 1918-08-15: U.S. Army infantry begin landing at Vladivostok
- 1918-09-04: 339th Infantry lands at Archangel, North Russia
- 1918-09: U.S. troops enter combat against Bolshevik forces
- 1918-11-11: WWI armistice; U.S. forces in North Russia keep fighting
- 1919-06: U.S. North Russia expeditionary force withdrawn
- 1920-04-01: Last U.S. troops depart Vladivostok

Crisis 39 — Mukden Incident

ACTION: On 18 September 1931 Japan's Kwantung Army used a staged railway explosion near Mukden as a pretext to invade and occupy Manchuria. The United States took no military action and imposed no sanctions; its response was limited to diplomacy, culminating in Secretary Stimson's 7 January 1932 note declaring Washington would not recognize territorial changes achieved by force (the Stimson Doctrine).

TIMELINE:
- 1931-09-18: Mukden Incident; Japanese troops seize Mukden
- 1931-09: U.S. takes no military action; no forces committed
- 1931-12: Japanese forces expand control across Manchuria
- 1932-01-07: Stimson issues the non-recognition doctrine (diplomatic note only)
- 1932-01-28: Shanghai Incident; U.S. still takes no military action
- 1932-02: Japan consolidates control of Manchuria

Crisis 52 — Spanish Civil War I

ACTION: The Spanish Civil War began in July 1936. The United States remained strictly neutral and took no military action; instead it applied a voluntary 'moral embargo' on arms in August 1936 and then a binding statutory arms embargo after Congress extended the Neutrality Act to cover civil wars in January 1937.

TIMELINE:
- 1936-07: Spanish Civil War erupts; Nationalist uprising against the Republic
- 1936-08: Roosevelt administration declares a voluntary 'moral embargo'
- 1936-12: A loophole allows a private U.S. arms shipment to Spain
- 1937-01-06: Congress extends the arms embargo to the Spanish civil conflict
- 1937-01: Statutory embargo on arms exports to both sides takes effect
- 1937-03: Embargo remains fully in force; U.S. takes no combat action

Crisis 54 — Spanish Civil War II

ACTION: During this phase the United States continued strict neutrality and non-intervention. The statutory arms embargo enacted in January 1937 remained in effect for both belligerents, and the administration resisted domestic pressure to lift it in favor of the Republic. U.S. action stayed at the Aid or Sanctions level, with no U.S. military forces engaged.

TIMELINE:
- 1937-01: Neutrality Act arms embargo on Spain remains in force
- 1937-03: Continued heavy fighting in Spain; U.S. holds to non-intervention
- 1937-04: U.S. reaffirms the embargo; cooperates with the Non-Intervention framework
- 1937-04: American volunteers fight privately for the Republic; U.S. government takes no official role
- 1937-05: Embargo and neutral posture unchanged; no U.S. combat action

Crisis 56 — Marco Polo Bridge Incident

ACTION: The Marco Polo Bridge Incident of 7 July 1937 escalated into the full-scale Second Sino-Japanese War. The United States took no military action: it issued diplomatic protests, and Roosevelt gave the rhetorical 'Quarantine' speech in October 1937, but declined to invoke the Neutrality Act. When Japanese aircraft sank the U.S. gunboat Panay on 12 December 1937, the U.S. accepted Japan's apology and indemnity rather than responding with force.

TIMELINE:
- 1937-07-07: Marco Polo Bridge clash ignites the Second Sino-Japanese War
- 1937-08: Fighting spreads to Shanghai; U.S. reinforces protection of nationals but takes no combat role
- 1937-10-05: Roosevelt's 'Quarantine' speech condemns aggression rhetorically
- 1937-12-12: Japanese aircraft sink USS Panay; 3 killed
- 1937-12-25: Japan apologizes; U.S. accepts, taking no military reprisal
- 1938-01: U.S. remains neutral with no combat action

Crisis 67 — Spanish Civil War IV

ACTION: This final phase covered the collapse of the Spanish Republic and Franco's victory in early 1939. The United States kept its arms embargo in place to the end and took no military action, then extended diplomatic recognition to the Franco government in April 1939.

TIMELINE:
- 1939-01-26: Barcelona falls to Nationalist forces
- 1939-01: U.S. arms embargo on Spain remains in force
- 1939-02: Britain and France move toward recognizing Franco; U.S. maintains neutrality
- 1939-03-28: Madrid falls to Nationalist forces
- 1939-03: Crisis window closes with the Republic near defeat; U.S. took no military action

Crisis 68 — Czechoslovakia Annexation

ACTION: On 15 March 1939 German troops occupied the rump Czech lands. The United States responded only with diplomatic and moral condemnation: Acting Secretary of State Sumner Welles denounced the occupation, and Washington withheld recognition. No U.S. military action, threat, or sanction was taken.

TIMELINE:
- 1939-03-14: Slovakia declares independence under German pressure
- 1939-03-15: German troops occupy Prague and the Czech lands
- 1939-03-17: Sumner Welles publicly condemns the occupation; U.S. withholds recognition
- 1939-03: U.S. takes no new sanction or military step
- 1939-03: Crisis window closes with U.S. limited to moral condemnation

Crisis 74 — Entry into WWII

ACTION: The crisis is the outbreak of World War II, marked by Germany's invasion of Poland on 1 September 1939. The United States responded by reaffirming neutrality: Roosevelt issued a Neutrality Proclamation, kept the arms embargo in effect, and ordered the Navy to organize a defensive hemispheric Neutrality Patrol. No U.S. forces were committed to combat.

TIMELINE:
- 1939-05: Neutrality Act arms embargo in force
- 1939-09-01: Germany invades Poland; war begins in Europe
- 1939-09-05: Roosevelt issues Proclamation of Neutrality
- 1939-09-08: Roosevelt proclaims a limited national emergency; Neutrality Patrol organized
- 1939-09: U.S. warships begin patrolling the western Atlantic (no combat)

Crisis 78 — Fall of West Europe

ACTION: The crisis is Germany's May-June 1940 conquest of the Low Countries and France. The United States remained a non-belligerent but shifted decisively to material aid short of war: after Dunkirk it released surplus WWI arms for sale to Britain, and in his 10 June 1940 Charlottesville speech Roosevelt pledged to extend the material resources of the nation to the Allies. No U.S. forces entered combat.

TIMELINE:
- 1940-05-10: Germany invades the Low Countries and France
- 1940-05-16: Roosevelt asks Congress for a major defense buildup
- 1940-06-03: U.S. begins releasing surplus WWI arms for sale to Britain and France
- 1940-06-10: Roosevelt's Charlottesville speech pledges aid to the Allies; Italy enters the war
- 1940-06-22: France signs armistice with Germany

Crisis 85 — Barbarossa

ACTION: Following Germany's 22 June 1941 invasion of the USSR, the United States expanded aid short of war and slid into an undeclared naval war in the Atlantic. Lend-Lease was extended to the Soviet Union on 7 November 1941, while the U.S. Navy took over Atlantic escort duty, producing armed clashes with U-boats (USS Greer 4 September, USS Reuben James sunk 31 October 1941).

TIMELINE:
- 1941-03-11: Lend-Lease Act signed
- 1941-06-22: Germany invades the USSR
- 1941-07-07: U.S. forces occupy Iceland
- 1941-09-04: USS Greer exchanges fire with U-652 (first U.S.-German naval combat)
- 1941-09-11: Roosevelt's 'shoot on sight' order
- 1941-10-31: USS Reuben James sunk; over 100 U.S. sailors killed
- 1941-11-07: USSR declared eligible for Lend-Lease

Crisis 88 — Pearl Harbor

ACTION: Japan's surprise attack on Pearl Harbor on 7 December 1941 brought the United States into full-scale war in the Pacific. U.S. forces were engaged in combat immediately across Hawaii, the Philippines, Guam, and Wake, and Congress declared war on Japan the next day.

TIMELINE:
- 1941-07-26: U.S. freezes Japanese assets and embargoes oil
- 1941-11-26: Hull note delivered; negotiations deadlock
- 1941-12-07: Japan attacks Pearl Harbor, the Philippines, Guam, and Wake
- 1941-12-08: United States declares war on Japan
- 1942-05-04: Battle of the Coral Sea
- 1942-06-04: Battle of Midway; turning point in the Pacific

Crisis 102 — Communism in Romania (1945)

ACTION: From 24 February to 6 March 1945, unrest in Bucharest led to a Soviet demand that Premier Radescu be replaced by Petru Groza, accompanied by the movement of Romanian troops out of the capital. The United States took no action and did not actively weigh military measures, consistent with its escalation level of No Response.

TIMELINE:
- 1945-02: Unrest mounted in Romania, including disturbances in factories and violent criticism in Soviet media.
- 1945-02-24: Rioting erupted in Bucharest; Radescu called for resistance to foreign intervention.
- 1945-02-27: Soviet Deputy Foreign Minister Vyshinsky arrived in Bucharest and demanded Radescu’s replacement.
- 1945-02-28: Vyshinsky issued an ultimatum naming Petru Groza as the proposed premier; Soviet forces moved Romanian troops from Bucharest to the front.
- 1945-03-01: Romanian authorities complied with the Soviet demands.
- 1945-03-06: Groza was installed as prime minister after King Michael overcame domestic opposition.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced no reported decision concerning U.S. military action in the crisis. The United States maintained a No Response posture throughout the period.

Crisis 104 — Trieste I

ACTION: As the Italian campaign ended, Yugoslav Partisan forces occupied Trieste on 1 May 1945, triggering a tense standoff with the Western Allies over Venezia Giulia. U.S. and British forces already in Italy were massed and reinforced while Truman and Churchill pressed Tito to withdraw, but the confrontation was resolved diplomatically without new U.S. combat.

TIMELINE:
- 1945-05-01: Yugoslav Partisans enter Trieste; contested joint occupation
- 1945-05-02: German forces in Italy surrender; Allied troops enter the city
- 1945-05: Truman and Churchill protest; Allied forces in Venezia Giulia reinforced
- 1945-05-20: Yugoslav General Staff orders a partial pullback
- 1945-06-09: Belgrade agreement signed, dividing Venezia Giulia along the Morgan Line
- 1945-06-12: Yugoslav military occupation of Trieste officially ends

Crisis 106 — Kars-Ardahan (1945)

ACTION: The USSR demanded revision of the Montreux Convention governing the Turkish Straits and the return of Kars and Ardahan, while Turkey sought U.S. and U.K. assistance. The United States considered diplomatic action and a revised, three-power-guaranteed Straits arrangement, and ultimately conducted a noncombat show of force by sending the battleship Missouri with an escort to return the deceased Turkish ambassador’s body to Istanbul.

TIMELINE:
- 1945-06: The USSR presented demands to revise the Montreux Convention, including the return of Kars and Ardahan.
- 1945-06: Turkey informed the U.K.; a proposed joint U.S.-U.K. démarche was rejected by Washington at that time.
- 1945-07: Turkey requested U.S. and U.K. assistance, leading Washington to agree to raise the issue with Stalin at Potsdam.
- 1945-07: Truman proposed revising the Montreux Convention under a three-power guarantee; discussion was postponed.
- 1946-04: The U.S. announced that the battleship Missouri and a full escort would return the deceased Turkish ambassador’s body to Istanbul, constituting a show of force without combat.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify a specific decision placed before Congress. U.S. decisions described in the crisis were executive actions and diplomatic proposals, shifting from consideration of a joint démarche and discussion at Potsdam to a noncombat naval show of force.

Crisis 108 — Azerbaijan (1945)

ACTION: From March 1946, Soviet troop movements in Iran and related pressure on Turkey created a crisis for the United States. The United States did not deploy forces or take military action; its response was limited to diplomatic protest and a firm position at the UN Security Council, consistent with a “No Response” escalation level.

TIMELINE:
- 1945-08: Soviet-supported Tudeh forces occupied government buildings in Tabriz and demanded autonomy; Iranian gendarmes were denied entry to the Soviet occupation zone.
- 1945-11: A Soviet-supported rebellion for Azerbaijani autonomy began; Iranian forces sent to Tabriz were stopped near Qasvin by Soviet forces.
- 1945-12: U.S. Secretary of State Byrnes and British Foreign Secretary Bevin sought a settlement with Stalin at the Moscow Conference; a proposed Big Three commission was rejected by the USSR.
- 1946-01: Iran requested a UN Security Council meeting over Soviet interference; the Council later called for bilateral Iranian-Soviet negotiations.
- 1946-03: Soviet forces moved outward from Tabriz; the United States decided to send a sharp protest to Moscow and instructed its UN delegation to take a firm position.
- 1946-05: Soviet troops left Iran, terminating the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary identifies no concrete decision placed before the U.S. Congress. U.S. decision-making described in the crisis occurred through executive diplomatic measures: a protest to Moscow and instructions to the U.S. delegation at the UN Security Council.

Crisis 110 — Communism in Poland (1946)

ACTION: The crisis concerned communism in Poland in 1946. The United States took no military action and did not reach an escalation level involving an active military option; its recorded response was “No Response.”

TIMELINE:
- 1946-01: Crisis period identified as communism in Poland.
- 1946-XX: U.S. escalation level actually reached: No Response.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The provided summary does not specify any concrete decision that Congress faced or any change in congressional consideration over the period.

Crisis 111 — Turkish Straits (1946)

ACTION: The USSR sought a revised international regime for the Turkish Straits while conducting naval maneuvers in the Black Sea and concentrating forces in the Caucasus. The United States reached a show-of-force level without combat, sending army and naval forces into the area on 20 August and increasing naval activity; it also reaffirmed diplomatic support for Turkey.

TIMELINE:
- 1946-08: The USSR requested a revised international regime for the Straits, while beginning naval maneuvers and concentrating forces in the Caucasus.
- 1946-08: The United States decided to send army and naval forces into the area as a show of force.
- 1946-08: Turkey resisted the Soviet demands while expressing willingness to attend a conference with the original signatories to revise the Montreux Convention.
- 1946-09: The USSR issued another Note, milder in tone than its initial demand.
- 1946-10: The United States reaffirmed support for Turkey in a Note to the USSR; increased U.S. and British naval activity preceded Turkey’s total rejection of the Soviet demands.
- 1946-10: The USSR indicated that a conference on the Straits was premature, terminating the crisis for the United States and Turkey.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions related to the U.S. response to the Soviet demands, including the use or deployment of military forces for a show of force in support of Turkey. Over the period, the U.S. response included both the deployment of army and naval forces and continued diplomatic backing for Turkey.

Crisis 113 — Communism in Hungary (1947)

ACTION: The Soviet Union’s arrest of Bela Kovács and subsequent pressure on Hungary’s Smallholders Party and Prime Minister Nagy created a crisis over political control following the Peace Treaty’s provision for withdrawing occupation forces. The United States took no military action and, consistent with the “No Response” escalation level, the summary identifies only diplomatic measures: requesting a three-power inquiry with the United Kingdom and exchanging notes with the USSR.

TIMELINE:
- 1947-02: The Peace Treaty entered into force, providing for the withdrawal of occupation forces from Hungary within six months.
- 1947-02: Soviet authorities arrested Bela Kovács on charges of undermining the security of Soviet occupation forces.
- 1947-03: A purge of the Smallholders Party extended to members of parliament and government ministers.
- 1947-05: While Prime Minister Nagy was in Switzerland, Soviet authorities presented Hungary with an interrogation record naming him as a fellow conspirator; Nagy was pressured to resign and threatened over his son’s safety.
- 1947-05: Nagy resigned on 30 May.
- 1947-06: Nagy’s formal resignation on 1 June terminated the crisis; the United States and United Kingdom had requested a three-power inquiry and exchanged notes with the USSR.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify a concrete decision placed before Congress. U.S. activity remained at the diplomatic level, involving a proposed three-power inquiry with the United Kingdom and exchanges of notes with the USSR, with no military action reported or actively weighed.

Crisis 114 — Truman Doctrine (1947)

ACTION: The United Kingdom’s decision to end aid to Greece and Turkey created a crisis involving concerns about Soviet influence in the eastern Mediterranean and threats perceived by Greece and Turkey. The United States requested and authorized military and economic assistance, but did not use combat forces; the aid bill was signed on 22 May, formalizing the Truman Doctrine.

TIMELINE:
- 1947-02: The United Kingdom announced that it would discontinue aid to Greece and Turkey by 31 March; Turkey requested U.S. aid.
- 1947-02: Greece sent a formal note to Washington requesting U.S. aid, following President Truman’s advice.
- 1947-03: President Truman asked Congress to grant military and economic assistance to Greece and Turkey.
- 1947-05: A United Nations Security Council commission reported on Greek border violations and called for an end to external assistance to Greek Communist guerrillas; the Soviet Union vetoed a related resolution.
- 1947-05: The aid bill was signed on 22 May, ending the crisis and proclaiming the Truman Doctrine.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the decision whether to authorize military and economic assistance for Greece and Turkey after the United Kingdom announced it would end its aid. The decision culminated in enactment of an aid bill signed on 22 May.

Crisis 120 — Palestine Partition/Israel Independence (1947)

ACTION: The UN’s 29 November 1947 partition resolution precipitated a crisis that escalated after Israel proclaimed independence on 14–15 May 1948 and Arab states invaded on 15 May. The United States reached no response level in the crisis; the summary does not identify U.S. military action or an actively weighed U.S. military option.

TIMELINE:
- 1947-11: The UN General Assembly adopted a resolution calling for partition of Palestine into independent Arab and predominantly Jewish states.
- 1947-12: Arab states held a summit, and the Arab League reaffirmed its determination to maintain Palestine as an Arab state while announcing plans to recruit volunteer forces.
- 1948-05: Britain’s Mandate ended on 14 May; Israel proclaimed independence on 14–15 May.
- 1948-05: Egypt, Syria, Lebanon, Jordan, and Iraq dispatched regular forces into Palestine and invaded Israel on 15 May; Israel sent its newly formed regular army to fight.
- 1948-12: The UN General Assembly created the Palestine Conciliation Commission on 11 December.
- 1949-07: Syria and Israel signed an armistice on 20 July, marking the final termination of the crisis for the participants.

BEFORE CONGRESS: With the U.S. escalation level at no response, the summary identifies no concrete U.S. military decision before Congress. The crisis involved U.S. political activity within and outside the UN, but the summary does not specify a congressional decision or a shift in one over the period.

Crisis 121 — Communism in Czech. (1948)
