ACTION: From 13 to 25 February 1948, a cabinet crisis in Czechoslovakia developed after non-Communist ministers resigned over the Communist interior minister’s failure to reinstate transferred or retired police commanders. The United States took no military action and, at the stated escalation level of “No Response,” was not recorded as actively weighing military measures; Soviet pressure contributed to President Beneš accepting the resignations on 25 February.

TIMELINE:
- 1946-05: Elections produced a coalition government in which the Communists were the largest single party; Klement Gottwald became Prime Minister and Jan Masaryk became Foreign Minister.
- 1947-10: A campaign of intimidation against non-Communist cabinet ministers began.
- 1948-01: The coalition government accused the Czechoslovak secret police of cooperating directly with the Soviet NKVD.
- 1948-02-13: Non-Communist regional police commanders in Prague were retired or transferred; after the Communist interior minister did not implement the cabinet’s reinstatement order, 12 ministers resigned.
- 1948-02-19: Soviet Deputy Foreign Minister Zorin arrived in Prague and told President Beneš that the USSR wanted Gottwald to resolve the cabinet crisis.
- 1948-02-25: Beneš accepted the resignations of the non-Communist ministers, ending the crisis and enabling the Communists to retain control.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced no recorded concrete decision on military action during the crisis because the United States took no military response. The summary records U.S. criticism of Soviet behavior, but does not identify a congressional authorization, appropriation, or other congressional decision that shifted over the period.

Crisis 123 — Berlin Blockade (1948)

ACTION: The Soviet Union imposed a blockade restricting ground access to West Berlin, creating a crisis over the city’s status and supply. The United States carried out an uncontested military airlift to sustain West Berlin and was not recorded here as undertaking broader military action.

TIMELINE:
- 1948-06: The Soviet Union imposed the Berlin blockade, restricting ground access to West Berlin.
- 1948-06: The United States initiated an airlift to supply West Berlin.
- 1948-06–1949-05: The U.S. continued the airlift while the blockade remained in effect.
- 1949-05: The Soviet Union ended the Berlin blockade.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning authorization or support for the U.S. military airlift and the resources required to sustain West Berlin. Over the period, the practical congressional issue shifted from responding to the blockade to maintaining the ongoing airlift until the blockade ended.

Crisis 125 — China Civil War (1948)

ACTION: Communist forces captured Tsinan on 1948-09-23 after an entire Nationalist division defected, and by autumn 1948 had taken control of northeast China. The United States conducted a policy review and decided not to provide military aid or commit U.S. combat forces to Nationalist China; the escalation level reached was aid or sanctions, with no U.S. combat force used.

TIMELINE:
- 1948-09: Tsinan fell to Chinese Communist forces, triggering the U.S. crisis.
- 1948-10: U.S. decision makers conducted an extensive policy review as Nationalist military setbacks continued.
- 1948-10: The United States decided not to commit military aid to Nationalist China.
- 1949-01: Nationalist military strength was broken.
- 1949-10: The People’s Republic of China was proclaimed, expanding the crisis to international proportions.
- 1949-12: Nationalists formally established the Republic of China on Taiwan; the United Nations General Assembly passed a resolution concerning foreign control and special privileges in China.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of whether to provide large-scale military aid to Nationalist China, including the possible use of U.S. armed forces. The U.S. decision ultimately shifted toward not committing military aid or combat forces as Nationalist control of mainland China continued to collapse.

Crisis 127 — Indonesia Indep. III (1948)

ACTION: Dutch forces occupied Djakarta and other cities in Java and Sumatra on 19 December 1948 and captured Republican leaders, prompting Indonesia to establish an emergency government and seek international action. The United States used aid pressure rather than combat forces, shifting from support for the Netherlands to threatening suspension of U.S. aid to secure Dutch compliance with UN resolutions.

TIMELINE:
- 1948-12: Dutch forces occupied Djakarta and other cities in Java and Sumatra and captured Republican political leaders.
- 1948-12: The United States shifted toward pressing the Netherlands through diplomatic and aid-related measures.
- 1949-04: U.S. representative H. Merle Cochran proposed 17 points for consideration in a UNCI-sponsored round-table process.
- 1949-08: Round-table discussions between the UNCI and the belligerent parties began.
- 1949-11: Round-table discussions concluded after international and U.S. pressure on the Netherlands.
- 1949-12: The Netherlands transferred sovereignty over the Dutch East Indies to the Republic of Indonesia; the crisis ended.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. aid to the Netherlands, including whether to suspend or threaten suspension of that aid to press Dutch compliance with UN resolutions. Over the period, the U.S. position shifted from support for the Netherlands toward using aid suspension as leverage.

Crisis 131 — Sov. Bloc-Yugoslavia — 1949 (1949)

ACTION: On 19 August 1949, Yugoslavia received a Soviet ultimatum perceived as a possible prelude to invasion, accompanied by a show of force, while Yugoslavia placed its forces on alert and conducted border maneuvers. The United States provided economic aid to Yugoslavia; no U.S. combat forces were used, and the summary does not identify U.S. consideration of military action.

TIMELINE:
- 1948-01: The Soviet Union expressed displeasure with Yugoslavia’s increasingly independent domestic and foreign policy.
- 1948-07: Yugoslavia was expelled from the Soviet-dominated Communist Information Bureau (COMINFORM).
- 1949-08: The Soviet government delivered an ultimatum to Yugoslavia; Yugoslavia alerted its forces and conducted military maneuvers along the border.
- 1949-11: The crisis began to fade.
- 1951-11: Yugoslavia filed a complaint with the United Nations concerning hostile actions by the Soviet Union and its Eastern European allies; the General Assembly noted Yugoslavia’s readiness to seek a peaceful solution.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The concrete U.S. measure was economic aid to Yugoslavia, while COMINFORM and later COMECON imposed sanctions on Yugoslavia. Over the period, the U.S. response remained at the aid-or-sanctions level, with no U.S. combat-force decision identified.

Crisis 132 — Korea (1950)

ACTION: North Korean forces invaded South Korea across the 38th Parallel on 25 June 1950, prompting a U.S. crisis response. The United States authorized its forces to fight alongside South Korean troops, ordered the Seventh Fleet to the Taiwan Strait, and established a naval blockade; U.S. forces subsequently fought in the Korean War at the combat level, with more than 1,000 U.S. deaths.

TIMELINE:
- 1950-06: North Korean forces crossed the 38th Parallel and invaded South Korea; South Korea appealed to the United States for military aid.
- 1950-06: The UN Security Council condemned the attack and called for assistance to South Korea.
- 1950-06: President Truman authorized U.S. forces to fight alongside South Korean troops and ordered the Seventh Fleet to the Taiwan Strait with a naval blockade.
- 1950-07: After U.S. and South Korean forces were driven southward, the UN Security Council urged members to contribute forces to a Unified Command; General MacArthur was appointed to lead it.
- 1950-09: UN forces restored control of South Korea to the South Korean government, ending the crisis for South Korea and the United States.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. military assistance and participation in fighting alongside South Korean forces. Over the period, the U.S. role moved from weighing or authorizing assistance to conducting sustained combat operations under a UN Unified Command.

Crisis 133 — Korean War II (1950)

ACTION: ROK and U.S.-led UN forces crossed the 38th Parallel and advanced into North Korea, prompting Chinese intervention and major fighting with UN forces. The United States conducted combat operations involving more than 1,000 U.S. deaths; as Chinese forces attacked, President Truman stated on 30 November that the United States was prepared to use whatever weapons were in its arsenal, while the war later stabilized near the 38th Parallel and moved toward cease-fire negotiations.

TIMELINE:
- 1950-09: ROK forces crossed the 38th Parallel and advanced into North Korea.
- 1950-10: U.S. forces crossed the 38th Parallel; President Truman and U.S. forces faced the Chinese intervention and fighting at Unsan.
- 1950-11: Chinese forces launched major attacks; Truman stated that the United States was prepared to use whatever weapons were in its arsenal.
- 1951-01: UN and South Korean forces abandoned Seoul after PRC and North Korean forces recrossed the 38th Parallel.
- 1951-03: UN forces recaptured Seoul; differences over expanding the war contributed to General MacArthur’s dismissal.
- 1951-07: Cease-fire and armistice negotiations began, ending the crisis for the United States and other principal actors.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the continuation and scope of U.S. combat operations as the war expanded into North Korea and Chinese forces entered the conflict. Over the period, the immediate issue shifted from advancing against North Korea to responding to Chinese attacks, managing the reversal of the battlefield, and pursuing a cease-fire while the fighting stabilized near the 38th Parallel.

Crisis 139 — Invasion Of Laos I (1953)

ACTION: Vietminh forces advanced into Laos in April 1953, while French-Laotian forces defended positions, received an airlift from Saigon, and later recaptured abandoned areas. The United States reached the level of aid or sanctions: it took no combat action, and the summary does not specify a U.S. military option actively under consideration.

TIMELINE:
- 1953-03: Vietminh forces massed along the Lao frontier, reaching a peak by 24 March.
- 1953-04: Vietminh launched an offensive on 5 April; France evacuated a major border town on 13 April, and Laos ordered general mobilization and appealed to the UN.
- 1953-04: Vietminh forces surrounded positions on the Plaine des Jarres and advanced toward Vientiane and Luang Prabang; Prince Souphanouvong declared a “Free Laotian Government” on 27 April.
- 1953-05: Vietminh garrisons began withdrawing toward North Vietnam by mid-May, while French-Laotian forces moved to recapture abandoned positions.
- 1953-06: On 2 June, French-Laotian columns reestablished contact between Vientiane and Xiengkhouang provinces.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the level and form of U.S. assistance or sanctions in response to the invasion. Over the period summarized, the United States did not commit combat forces; the summary does not identify a further congressional shift in the decision at issue.

Crisis 140 — Korean War III (1953)

ACTION: From April to July 1953, PRC and North Korean forces launched an offensive against UN troops while armistice and POW negotiations remained stalled. The United States was engaged in combat, and on 22 May Secretary of State Dulles conveyed a threat to use tactical nuclear weapons if necessary to break the deadlock; no use of such weapons is stated in the summary.

TIMELINE:
- 1953-01: Eisenhower succeeded Truman; U.S. concerns included the release of POWs and ending the stalemate.
- 1953-04: PRC-North Korean forces began an offensive against UN troops at Pork Chop Hill and Old Baldy.
- 1953-05: Dulles conveyed to India’s Prime Minister Nehru a U.S. threat of possible tactical nuclear weapons use against the PRC.
- 1953-06: PRC and North Korea agreed at Panmunjom to voluntary POW repatriation.
- 1953-06: South Korea unauthorizedly released approximately 25,000 POWs who did not wish to return to the PRC or North Korea.
- 1953-07: The Armistice Agreement was signed, establishing a demarcation line, DMZ, and mechanisms for managing violations and troop withdrawals.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the ongoing U.S. combat involvement in Korea and the broader response to the battlefield and negotiating stalemate. As negotiations progressed, the immediate issue shifted toward the POW-repatriation arrangement and the terms for ending the conflict.

Crisis 141 — E. German Uprising (1953)

ACTION: On 17 June 1953, strikes and riots spread across East Berlin and roughly 400 other East German towns, prompting the declaration of a state of emergency and the introduction of martial law. The United States reached an escalation level of No Response and did not take military action or actively weigh military intervention; Western actions were limited to psychological warfare and diplomatic notes.

TIMELINE:
- 1953-05: East Germany introduced an increased production quota system, prompting worker resistance.
- 1953-06: Construction workers struck on 16 June, and a general strike was called for the following day.
- 1953-06: On 17 June, strikes, demonstrations, and riots spread across East Germany; Soviet troops and tanks moved into East Berlin, supported by East German paramilitary police.
- 1953-06: Soviet and East German forces suppressed the uprising; 125 people died and thousands were arrested.
- 1953-07: On 11 July, martial law was lifted in East Germany, ending the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced no reported decision on U.S. military action because the United States reached a No Response escalation level. The Western response consisted of psychological warfare, diplomatic notes urging an end to hostilities, and calls for German reunification.

Crisis 142 — Trieste II — 1953 (1953)

ACTION: On 8 October 1953, the United States and Britain announced plans to terminate the Allied Military Government in Trieste, withdraw their troops, and transfer administration to Italy. With the U.S. escalation level recorded as No Response, the United States did not take military action or actively weigh additional military measures; it instead participated in diplomatic mediation while the planned evacuation was postponed.

TIMELINE:
- 1953-10: The United States and Britain announced their intention to end the Allied Military Government in Trieste, withdraw their troops, and relinquish administration to Italy.
- 1953-10: Yugoslavia protested to London and Washington, informed the United Nations, closed its frontier, called up military reserves, and moved warships into the area.
- 1953-10: Italy placed three divisions on emergency posting at the Yugoslav frontier.
- 1953-10–1953-12: The United States and United Kingdom postponed the Trieste evacuation while their delegations conducted separate meetings and mediation efforts with Yugoslavia and Italy.
- 1953-12: An agreement was announced in Rome and Belgrade for the simultaneous withdrawal of Italian and Yugoslav forces from the border, ending the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the issue of whether to address or authorize the planned U.S. troop withdrawal and termination of Allied administration in Trieste. As diplomatic efforts continued, the immediate military decision shifted from implementing the announced evacuation to postponing it while negotiations proceeded; the summary does not identify a separate congressional action.

Crisis 144 — Guatemala (1953)

ACTION: Guatemala’s agrarian reforms, controls on foreign investment, and growing Communist Party influence led the United States to view the government as a threat to its regional influence. The United States used political and economic measures, including a complete arms embargo proclaimed on 18 June 1954, and supported an invasion led by Castillo Armas; it did not deploy combat forces.

TIMELINE:
- 1953-12: Guatemala received information that the United States would support an antigovernment “liberation” movement.
- 1954-01: Guatemala decided to obtain Soviet-bloc arms for a workers’ militia.
- 1954-02: The United States confirmed Guatemala’s Soviet-bloc arms decision, triggering a U.S. crisis.
- 1954-05: Soviet arms began reaching Guatemala; Honduras recalled its ambassador on 25 May amid concerns about Guatemalan infiltration and arms reaching Honduran strikers.
- 1954-06: On 18 June, the United States proclaimed a complete arms embargo and backed Castillo Armas’s invasion; Guatemala appealed to the Security Council and the Inter-American Peace Committee on 19 June.
- 1954-06: Arbenz resigned on 27 June after the Guatemalan army refused to engage Armas’s forces; a new junta was established on 29 June.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. economic and military-related measures toward Guatemala, including the arms embargo and support for the antigovernment movement. Over the crisis period, the U.S. response shifted from unsuccessful conferences, negotiations, and promises to review regional economic problems to an arms embargo and backing for the invasion, without U.S. combat-force involvement.

Crisis 145 — Dien Bien Phu (1954)

ACTION: Vietminh forces attacked and encircled the French position at Dien Bien Phu, prompting France to seek increased allied aid and direct U.S. involvement. The United States issued warnings emphasizing Indochina’s importance and explored united action, air strikes, and other military options, including limited tactical nuclear strikes and possible ground-force deployment, but actually reached the level of threats of force and did not take unilateral military action or send ground forces.

TIMELINE:
- 1954-03: Vietminh launched its first major offensive against Dien Bien Phu; France appealed to its allies for increased aid and threatened strong military action.
- 1954-03: U.S. officials discussed bombers, ground strategy, and possible assistance with French General Ely; Secretary of State Dulles proposed undefined allied “united action.”
- 1954-04: U.S. and French officials considered allied air strikes and a regional collective-defense arrangement; the United States declined to act without British cooperation.
- 1954-04: France formally requested U.S. aid; President Eisenhower stated that, absent a joint Allied response, the United States would not send ground forces or act unilaterally.
- 1954-05: Dien Bien Phu fell, and a formal cease-fire took effect the following day, ending the U.S. crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning whether to authorize or otherwise support possible U.S. participation in allied military action, including air operations or deployment of ground forces, while the administration sought allied cooperation. As the period progressed, the administration ruled out unilateral action and U.S. ground-force deployment in the absence of a joint Allied response.

Crisis 146 — First Taiwan Strait (1954)

ACTION: The PRC bombarded the Nationalist-held offshore islands of Quemoy and Matsu, prompting Taiwan to conduct air strikes and later return fire and fight on Tachen, Quemoy, and Matsu. The United States sent the Seventh Fleet to the area, appealed to the UN, helped fortify Quemoy and Matsu, and President Eisenhower requested congressional authority to control the situation; U.S. involvement remained a show of force without combat.

TIMELINE:
- 1954-08: The crisis began amid PRC concern over the impending creation of SEATO; SEATO was formally established on 8 September.
- 1954-09: PRC forces bombarded Quemoy and Matsu on 3 September; Taiwan conducted air strikes against the mainland on 7 September.
- 1954-09: On 12 September, the United States decided to send the Seventh Fleet to the area and appealed to the UN.
- 1954-12: The United States and Taiwan signed a defense treaty on 2 December; the PRC had ceased its bombardment by that time.
- 1955-01: Following heavy PRC bombardment of the Tachen Islands on 10 January, Taiwan returned fire and fought on Tachen, Quemoy, and Matsu, and subsequently evacuated Tachen.
- 1955-01: On 24 January, President Eisenhower asked Congress for a free hand in controlling the situation in the Taiwan area.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced a request from President Eisenhower on 24 January 1955 to grant him broad authority to control the situation in the Taiwan area. This followed the shift from earlier U.S. actions—sending the Seventh Fleet, appealing to the UN, and signing a defense treaty with Taiwan—to a request for expanded presidential authority after the PRC’s bombardment of Tachen and Taiwan’s subsequent military response.

Crisis 147 — Costa Rica/Nic. II (1955)

ACTION: From 8–20 January 1955, Costa Rica faced an armed incursion by rebels crossing from Nicaragua, with reported arms supplies and foreign aircraft activity. The United States provided four F-51 Mustang fighter planes under the OAS process; it did not deploy combat forces, while Costa Rica mobilized its own police and volunteers.

TIMELINE:
- 1955-01: Costa Rica appealed to the OAS, mobilized volunteer reserves, and placed its police on standby after receiving information about military transports sent to Nicaragua.
- 1955-01: Approximately 500 Costa Rican rebels crossed from Nicaragua and captured a town north of San José; Costa Rica declared a state of emergency and broke diplomatic relations with Nicaragua.
- 1955-01: The OAS Council sent a fact-finding mission and subsequently called on Nicaragua to halt arms flows to the rebels.
- 1955-01: The OAS authorized Costa Rica’s request for aircraft, and the United States delivered four F-51 Mustang fighter planes.
- 1955-01: Nicaragua reinforced its borders and increased military flights; Costa Rican forces drove the rebels back across the border.
- 1955-01: The rebels withdrew, and Costa Rica and Nicaragua agreed to an OAS plan for demilitarized border zones, ending the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the concrete issue of U.S. provision of four fighter aircraft to Costa Rica in response to the OAS decision. Over the crisis period, the U.S. action remained limited to aid rather than deployment of U.S. combat forces.

Crisis 148 — Baghdad Pact (1955)

ACTION: Egypt opposed the Baghdad Pact, established by Iran, Iraq, Pakistan, Turkey, and the United Kingdom on 24 February 1955, and sought counteralliances with other Arab states. The United States participated in negotiations on the Egypt-Syria Mutual Defense Pact and provided economic assistance, while never formally joining the Pact or using combat forces.

TIMELINE:
- 1955-02: Iran, Iraq, Pakistan, Turkey, and the United Kingdom created the Baghdad Pact, triggering Egypt’s crisis.
- 1955-02: Egypt and Syria announced plans to exclude Iraq from a proposed Arab alliance with Saudi Arabia and Yemen.
- 1955-03: Egypt denounced the Baghdad Pact and accused Western powers of dividing the Arab world and threatening Egypt with encirclement.
- 1955-03–10: Egypt pursued counteralliances with Jordan, Lebanon, and Syria; the United States participated in negotiations and provided economic assistance.
- 1955-10: Egypt and Syria concluded a Mutual Defense Pact, ending the crisis in mid-October.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. economic assistance and participation in negotiations related to the Egypt-Syria Mutual Defense Pact, rather than deployment of U.S. combat forces or formal U.S. entry into the Baghdad Pact. Over the period, the U.S. role remained assistance and negotiation rather than direct military action.

Crisis 152 — Suez Natn.-War (1956)

ACTION: Egypt nationalized the Suez Canal on 26 July 1956, after the United States and United Kingdom withdrew financial support for the Aswan Dam. Following Israel’s invasion of Sinai and the British-French intervention, the United States reached the escalation level of threats of force: on 6 November it declared a semialert and warned that it would not stand by if London and Paris were bombed, while also pressing them to withdraw.

TIMELINE:
- 1956-07: Egypt nationalized the Suez Canal; the United States, United Kingdom, France, and Australia began multilateral talks on a peaceful solution.
- 1956-08: France initiated bilateral talks with Israel concerning a military response; Britain later joined France and Israel in planning.
- 1956-10: At Sèvres, Britain, France, and Israel decided to launch a joint attack on Egypt’s Sinai Peninsula.
- 1956-10: Israel invaded Sinai on 29 October; British and French forces landed in the Canal Zone on 31 October.
- 1956-11: The Soviet Union issued a note threatening force against the invading powers; on 6 November the United States declared a semialert, warned Moscow regarding possible bombing of London and Paris, and pressed its allies to withdraw.
- 1957-03: Israel completed its withdrawal from Sinai on 12 March, ending the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the United States’ response to the conflict, including whether and how to address the threat posed by the Soviet warning and the possibility of attacks on U.S. allies. Over the period, the immediate issue shifted from the canal nationalization and the allied intervention to the U.S. response to the Soviet threat and the withdrawal of foreign forces.

Crisis 154 — Poland Liberalization (1956)

ACTION: Poland’s liberalization led to the return of Wladyslaw Gomulka and four allies to the Polish Politburo on 15 October 1956, prompting Soviet concern over its control of Poland. The United States took no action and was only marginally involved; no U.S. military action was taken or actively weighed.

TIMELINE:
- 1956-06: A workers’ demonstration in Poznan became a riot and was brutally suppressed by the Polish government.
- 1956-10: Gomulka and three allies were invited to rejoin the Polish United Workers Party Politburo.
- 1956-10: Polish leaders rejected a Khrushchev invitation to go to Moscow; the Soviet Union decided to send a delegation to the Polish Plenum.
- 1956-10: Soviet troop movements and heightened readiness accompanied the crisis; the Soviet Union later halted the movements.
- 1956-10: The Polish Plenum concluded, ending the crisis; Gomulka remained in power while the Soviet Union retained effective control over Poland.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced no identified concrete decision regarding U.S. military action, because the United States took no response and was only marginally involved. No shift in the U.S. decision posture is identified over the period.

Crisis 155 — Hungarian Uprising (1956)

ACTION: Beginning on 23 October 1956, demonstrations and armed resistance in Hungary challenged Soviet control, while the Soviet Union moved to remove the Nagy government by force. The United States issued a protest against Soviet interference but took no military action and did not actively weigh military intervention, consistent with the “No Response” escalation level.

TIMELINE:
- 1956-10: Demonstrations of approximately 200,000 people began in Budapest and other major Hungarian cities, demanding Soviet troop withdrawal, Imre Nagy’s return, democratization, and more equal Soviet-Hungarian relations.
- 1956-11: The Soviet Union decided to remove the new Hungarian government by military action; Hungary announced withdrawal from the Warsaw Pact and requested that the United Nations consider Hungarian neutrality.
- 1956-11: The United States issued a protest against Soviet interference in Hungary.
- 1956-11: Soviet forces removed the Nagy government and installed a more compliant regime; the UN General Assembly called for Soviet withdrawal, without effect.
- 1957-01: Organized resistance by Hungarian workers was crushed, ending the crisis for Hungary.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify a concrete decision that Congress faced. It records U.S. diplomatic protest and acquiescence in the Soviet reassertion of control, with no U.S. military action or military option described.

Crisis 157 — Jordan Regime (1957)

ACTION: On 4 April 1957, an attempted overthrow threatened King Hussein’s regime amid Jordan’s leftward political shift and suspected Egyptian and Syrian assistance to anti-Hussein elements. The United States provided military and economic aid and deployed units of the Sixth Fleet to the eastern Mediterranean as a show of force, without combat; the units departed on 3 May.

TIMELINE:
- 1956-10: Leftist nationalist parties won Jordan’s parliamentary elections, and Suleiman al-Nabulsi became prime minister.
- 1957-03: Jordan annulled its treaty with the United Kingdom, established diplomatic relations with the Soviet Union, and accepted an offer of Soviet aid.
- 1957-04: An attempt to overthrow King Hussein triggered the crisis.
- 1957-04: King Hussein dismissed the leftist cabinet, suppressed political parties after ensuing unrest, dismissed Chief of Staff Ali Abu Nuwar, and secured army officers’ support.
- 1957-04: The United States provided Jordan military and economic aid and sent units of the Sixth Fleet to the eastern Mediterranean.
- 1957-05: Sixth Fleet units departed on 3 May, indicating the crisis had ended.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. military and economic assistance to King Hussein’s government as the regime confronted an overthrow attempt. Over the crisis period, the U.S. action included assistance to the king and a show-of-force deployment, rather than combat operations.

Crisis 159 — Syria/Turkey Confrnt. (1957)

ACTION: Syria’s appointment of a pro-Soviet chief of staff and reported Soviet military support heightened tensions with Turkey, which deployed forces along the Syrian border. The United States mainly used political-diplomatic measures, reaffirmed the Eisenhower Doctrine, shipped arms to Jordan, and held Sixth Fleet maneuvers off Syria; it did not engage in combat, but was actively signaling support and deterrence.

TIMELINE:
- 1957-08: Syria formalized the appointment of pro-Soviet Colonel `Afif Bizri as chief of staff; Turkey responded with regional consultations and troop deployments along the Syrian border.
- 1957-09: Washington reaffirmed the Eisenhower Doctrine and its commitment to assist Middle Eastern states threatened by “international communism.”
- 1957-09: Following Soviet warnings to Turkey, Turkish Premier Menderes stated that Turkey had no aggressive intentions toward Syria.
- 1957-10: Soviet warships departed Latakia; the USSR and Syria continued alleging at the UN that Turkey, with U.S. advisers’ support, planned to attack Syria, while Turkey denied the charge.
- 1957-10-29: Khrushchev appeared at a reception at the Turkish embassy in Moscow, signaling the end of the crisis; Turkish forces later withdrew from the Syrian border.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the broader question of U.S. commitments and measures to assist states threatened by international communism, including the administration’s reaffirmation of the Eisenhower Doctrine. During the crisis, the concrete U.S. actions described were arms shipments to Jordan and Sixth Fleet maneuvers off Syria, alongside diplomatic signaling rather than combat operations.

Crisis 164 — Abort. Coup Indonesia (1958)

ACTION: From February to May 1958, an Indonesian regional rebellion challenged the central government, which responded with military operations in Sumatra. The United States reached a show-of-force level without combat: U.S. naval forces, including the Seventh Fleet, were offshore amid Indonesian concerns about possible U.S. intervention, while U.S. officials also pursued improved relations and stated that the United States was not involved in the internal affair.

TIMELINE:
- 1958-02: Sukarno rejected rebel demands and accused the rebels of acting as instruments of a foreign power; a revolutionary state had been proclaimed on 15 February.
- 1958-02-22: Indonesian government forces began air attacks against the rebels in central Sumatra.
- 1958-03: Government paratroops occupied Pakanbahru, where U.S. personnel were located at oil fields; the Seventh Fleet was offshore.
- 1958-04: Two key rebel towns fell, including Padang; the United States began efforts to improve relations with Indonesia.
- 1958-05-20: Secretary of State Dulles formally stated that the United States was not involved in the internal Indonesian affair, marking the crisis’s end.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the scope of any U.S. response to the Indonesian rebellion, including whether military involvement might be required as concerns arose about possible intervention. Over the period, the active U.S. posture shifted from a show of force without combat and consideration of possible intervention toward diplomatic efforts and a formal statement of noninvolvement.

Crisis 165 — Iraq/Leb. Upheaval (1958)

ACTION: Rioting in Lebanon and a coup in Iraq created a regional crisis involving Lebanon, Jordan, the United States, and the United Kingdom. After the Baghdad coup on 14 July, the United States ordered Marines to Lebanon to end the violence and stabilize the area, reaching the combat level with no U.S. deaths.

TIMELINE:
- 1958-05: Rioting began in Lebanon after the murder of a reformist editor; the Lebanese army assumed security duties in Beirut, Tripoli, and other cities.
- 1958-06: The UN Security Council considered the Lebanon crisis and approved a Swedish-sponsored Observer Group to investigate alleged cross-border infiltration.
- 1958-07: The Observer Group reported no border infiltration; on 14 July, a coup overthrew Iraq’s Hashemite monarchy, and the United States ordered Marines to Lebanon.
- 1958-07: Fu'ad Shihab was elected president of Lebanon on 31 July after the situation there had sufficiently calmed.
- 1958-08: The UN General Assembly adopted a resolution calling on all parties to follow the Arab League’s principle of nonintervention.
- 1958-10: On 14 October, all Lebanese political factions accepted the new government, marking the end of the crisis for Lebanon and the United States.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify a specific congressional decision or legislative action concerning the U.S. response. The U.S. posture shifted during the period from responding to Lebanon’s request for assistance and monitoring the crisis through international institutions to deploying Marines to Lebanon after the Iraqi coup.

Crisis 166 — Second Taiwan Strait (1958)

ACTION: The PRC massed forces opposite Quemoy and Matsu and began bombarding the islands on 1958-08-23, with Taiwan returning fire. The United States threatened to move into the area with a reinforced Seventh Fleet and implemented that decision with some restriction on 1958-08-27, constituting a show of force without combat; it also weighed intervention if the islands were invaded, potentially including nuclear weapons.

TIMELINE:
- 1958-07: Mainland Chinese military forces massed near Quemoy and Matsu, triggering a crisis for Taiwan.
- 1958-08: The PRC began bombarding Quemoy and Matsu on 23 August; Taiwan returned fire, triggering a crisis for the United States.
- 1958-08: The United States threatened to move into the area with a reinforced Seventh Fleet on 25 August and implemented the decision with some restriction on 27 August.
- 1958-09: The United States rejected the PRC’s announced 12-mile territorial-water extension and implied it would intervene if Quemoy were invaded; intervention might involve nuclear weapons.
- 1958-09: U.S.-PRC ambassadorial talks resumed in Warsaw on 14 September, ending the crisis for the United States.
- 1958-10: Secretary Dulles stated on 30 September that the United States favored evacuating Nationalist Chinese forces from the offshore islands if the PRC agreed to a cease-fire; the Taiwan crisis ended on 23 October with a joint communiqué by Chiang and Dulles.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of how to respond to the PRC’s bombardment and threat to blockade the offshore islands, including the implications of U.S. naval movement and possible intervention. Over the period, the U.S. position shifted from deploying a reinforced Seventh Fleet as a restricted show of force toward conditioning support for evacuation of Nationalist forces on a PRC cease-fire and clarifying that an invasion of the mainland would not be supported.

Crisis 168 — Berlin Deadline (1958)

ACTION: The Soviet Union issued a deadline concerning Berlin, creating a crisis over the city’s status and access. The United States reached the escalation level of threats of force; it threatened possible military action but did not actually use force.

TIMELINE:
- 1958-11: The Berlin crisis began with the Soviet deadline concerning Berlin.
- 1958-11: The United States responded to the deadline with threats of force.
- 1958-12: The crisis continued as the United States weighed possible military responses.
- 1959-01: The Berlin deadline crisis remained unresolved.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the United States’ response to the Soviet deadline, including whether to authorize or support possible military measures. Over the period, the immediate congressional issue shifted from considering a response to monitoring the continuing crisis while the United States maintained threats of force.

Crisis 170 — Cen. America/Cuba I (1959)

ACTION: A series of exile invasions, supported by Cuban revolutionary networks and in some cases Cuban forces, occurred in Panama, Nicaragua, the Dominican Republic, and Haiti between April and August 1959. The United States reached a Show of Force without combat by organizing a naval mission to patrol Caribbean waters and authorizing it to shoot to prevent a communist-led invasion; no U.S. combat action is reported.

TIMELINE:
- 1959-04: Exiles landed in Panama; Panama requested OAS assistance.
- 1959-05: The Panamanian invaders surrendered; an OAS naval patrol was authorized and later called off.
- 1959-06: Nicaraguan exiles invaded; the rebellion was defeated, and an OAS fact-finding mission was sent.
- 1959-06: An invasion of the Dominican Republic was repelled; the Dominican Republic later lodged a complaint against Cuba at the OAS.
- 1959-08: Haiti was invaded; Haiti charged Cuba before the OAS.
- 1959-09: Haiti announced that the invaders had surrendered.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of how to respond to repeated exile invasions and the potential for communist-led incursions in the Caribbean, including the U.S. patrol mission and its authorization to fire to bar such an invasion. Over the period, the immediate issue shifted from responding to individual invasions toward maintaining regional stability as the crises subsided.

Crisis 178 — Cen. America/Cuba II (1960)

ACTION: On 9 November 1960, exiles crossed from Costa Rica into Nicaragua and captured two towns; on 13 November, dissident Guatemalan army units seized a port, with the revolt collapsing on 16 November. After appeals from Guatemala and Nicaragua, President Eisenhower ordered U.S. Navy surface and air units to conduct Caribbean patrols to prevent further invasions—a show of force with no combat—and ordered their withdrawal after receiving assurances that coastal defense was no longer required.

TIMELINE:
- 1960-11: Exiles invaded Nicaragua from Costa Rica and captured two small towns; martial law was imposed and troops were deployed.
- 1960-11: Dissident Guatemalan army units seized a port northeast of Guatemala City.
- 1960-11: Guatemala appealed to the United States for naval and air patrols, citing alleged Cuban involvement.
- 1960-11: The Guatemalan revolt collapsed.
- 1960-11: Nicaragua appealed to the United States; President Eisenhower announced that U.S. naval surface and air units would act to prevent invasions by communist-directed elements.
- 1960-12: After assurances from Guatemala and Nicaragua that coastal defense was no longer required, Eisenhower ordered the Caribbean patrol withdrawn.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of whether and how to address the regional invasions and the requested U.S. military response, including the deployment of naval and air patrols. Over the period, the immediate issue shifted from potential U.S. action to the continuation or withdrawal of the patrol after the governments provided assurances that coastal defense was no longer required.

Crisis 180 — Pathet Lao Offensive (1961)

ACTION: On 9 March 1961, a Pathet Lao offensive supported by Vietminh forces broke through Laotian government defenses and threatened Vientiane, Luang Prabang, and the Mekong lowlands. The United States reached a show-of-force level without combat: it prepared troops for possible deployment, established and supplied bases in Thailand, alerted a task force, sent the Seventh Fleet to the Gulf of Siam, and ordered limited U.S. forces and uniformed military advisers into Laos on 28 April; it also actively weighed stronger action if peaceful efforts failed.

TIMELINE:
- 1961-03: Pathet Lao and Vietminh forces launched a major offensive, severing the road junction between Vientiane and Luang Prabang and threatening both cities.
- 1961-03: The United States prepared forces for possible action, established a helicopter base in Thailand, stocked forward bases, alerted the Okinawa task force, and sent the Seventh Fleet to the Gulf of Siam.
- 1961-03: President Kennedy warned that continued Soviet and North Vietnamese support increased the probability of war while backing proposals for a cease-fire and international conference.
- 1961-04: Pathet Lao advances toward the Mekong threatened to divide Laos and triggered a crisis for Thailand; Thai forces moved to the border, and U.S. forces and advisers were ordered into Laos on 28 April.
- 1961-05: A cease-fire took effect on 3 May, and the Geneva Conference on Laos opened on 16 May.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the possible authorization or support of U.S. military involvement if peaceful efforts failed, while U.S. actions remained at the show-of-force level without combat. As the crisis progressed, the immediate U.S. focus shifted toward cease-fire proposals, an international conference, and monitoring through the International Control Commission.

Crisis 181 — Bay Of Pigs (1961)

ACTION: Cuba and the United States faced a crisis from 15–24 April 1961 after exiles used U.S.-provided B-26 aircraft to bomb Cuban military and civilian centers. The United States provided support for an anti-Castro invasion force of Cuban exiles, but did not use U.S. combat forces; President Kennedy stated that the United States intended no military intervention in Cuba.

TIMELINE:
- 1961-01: U.S. diplomatic relations with Cuba were broken on 3 January.
- 1961-04: President Kennedy authorized an invasion by Cuban exiles, with support or nonobjection from the CIA, Defense Department, and Joint Chiefs of Staff.
- 1961-04: On 15 April, exiles flying U.S.-provided B-26 aircraft bombed Cuban military and civilian centers; Cuba charged the United States at the UN General Assembly with complicity.
- 1961-04: On 17 April, Cuba declared a state of “national alert” and appealed again to the General Assembly; the United States proceeded with support for the invasion on 18 April.
- 1961-04: On 18 April, Kennedy told the Soviet Union that the United States intended no military intervention in Cuba, while warning it would act if an outside force intervened militarily.
- 1961-04: Cuba defeated the invading force on 19 April; on 24 April, Kennedy assumed direct responsibility for the events leading to the Bay of Pigs fiasco.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary identifies executive authorization and support for the exile invasion, but does not specify a congressional vote or other concrete congressional decision. It therefore does not indicate a shift in the decisions before Congress during the crisis.

Crisis 185 — Berlin Wall (1961)

ACTION: East Germany, with Soviet authorization, erected a wall around West Berlin on 13 August 1961, ending the flow of refugees to the West and triggering a crisis for the Western powers. The United States responded by strengthening its Berlin garrison, rushing 1,500 troops to the city, patrolling west of the Wall, and later confronting Soviet tanks at Checkpoint Charlie; it did not engage in combat.

TIMELINE:
- 1961-06: Following the Kennedy–Khrushchev meeting, Kennedy stated that the United States would defend its rights in Berlin and the freedom of West Berliners, and called up military reserve units.
- 1961-08: Khrushchev authorized Walter Ulbricht to erect a wall around West Berlin at a Communist-bloc summit in Moscow.
- 1961-08: East Germany implemented the decision and began erecting the Wall on 13 August.
- 1961-08: On 17–18 August, the United States, United Kingdom, and France strengthened the Berlin garrison; 1,500 U.S. troops were rushed to the city.
- 1961-10: Khrushchev withdrew the year-end deadline for a German peace treaty on 17 October.
- 1961-10: U.S. and Soviet tanks confronted one another at Checkpoint Charlie from 25–28 October; the tanks withdrew in parallel on 28 October.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the U.S. military response to developments in Berlin, including the use of reserve call-ups and the reinforcement of the Berlin garrison. As the crisis progressed, the concrete U.S. military posture shifted from readiness and reinforcement to managing the tank confrontation without combat.

Crisis 186 — Viet Cong Attack (1961)

ACTION: Viet Cong forces captured Phuoc Vinh on 18 September 1961 and held it for a day, intensifying the deteriorating security situation in South Vietnam. The United States reached the Military Advisers level: it sent an assessment mission, then decided on a limited commitment of several hundred advisers and specialists, expanded aid, aircraft, and other equipment, while deferring a decision on committing ground forces.

TIMELINE:
- 1961-09: Viet Cong forces captured and held Phuoc Vinh for a day; South Vietnam’s President Diem requested a bilateral defense treaty with the United States on 29 September.
- 1961-10: President Kennedy and his advisers decided on 11 October to send Walt Rostow and General Maxwell Taylor to assess the feasibility of U.S. intervention; South Vietnam proclaimed a state of emergency on 19 October.
- 1961-11: Rostow and Taylor recommended on 3 November expanded U.S. aid and an 8,000-person logistical task force.
- 1961-11: Secretaries Rusk and McNamara recommended on 11 November that a decision to commit ground forces could be deferred.
- 1961-11: On 15 November, the National Security Council decided on limited aid and advisers, including several hundred specialists, plus aircraft and other equipment.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify a specific congressional decision during the crisis. The U.S. policy shifted from assessing the feasibility of intervention and considering expanded aid and a logistical task force to providing limited aid and military advisers while deferring commitment of ground forces.

Crisis 192 — Taiwan Strait III (1962) (1962)

ACTION: The crisis centered on PRC concerns that Chiang Kai-shek might prepare an invasion of the mainland, following Nationalist statements, additional conscription, and a new tax supporting a “return to the mainland.” The United States reached the level of threats of force: it stated that it would defend Taiwan against a PRC threat, while denying that it would actively support a Taiwanese invasion of the mainland; no U.S. military action is identified in the summary.

TIMELINE:
- 1962-04: Chiang Kai-shek’s Easter message threatened to invade the mainland, triggering the crisis.
- 1962-05: Taiwan conscripted additional manpower and imposed a special tax to support a “return to the mainland”; further invasion-related statements followed from 22 May.
- 1962-06: The PRC began a considerable troop buildup in Fukien Province on 10 June.
- 1962-06: At a 23 June meeting in Warsaw, U.S. Ambassador John Cabot denied that the United States would actively support a Taiwan invasion.
- 1962-06: On 27 June, President Kennedy stated that the United States would defend Taiwan against a PRC threat but that U.S. policy was peaceful and defensive only, bringing the crisis to a unilateral conclusion.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify a specific congressional decision or legislative action. It describes the U.S. executive position as weighing or communicating defense of Taiwan against a PRC threat while excluding active U.S. support for a Taiwanese invasion of the mainland.

Crisis 193 — Nam Tha (1962)

ACTION: On 6 May 1962, a heavy Pathet Lao attack captured Nam Tha, and Laotian government forces retreated into Thailand, creating concern about the movement of Pathet Lao forces toward Thailand’s northern border. The United States made a show of force without combat: President Kennedy ordered the Seventh Fleet to the Gulf of Siam, placed other U.S. forces on standby alert, moved 1,000 Marines already in Thailand to the Laotian border, and ordered 4,000 additional troops into Thailand to protect its territorial integrity.

TIMELINE:
- 1962-05: Pathet Lao forces launched a heavy attack on Nam Tha; Laotian government forces retreated across the Mekong River into Thailand.
- 1962-05: Thai Prime Minister Sarit ordered military units to reinforce defenses along the Laos border.
- 1962-05: President Kennedy ordered the Seventh Fleet to the Gulf of Siam and placed U.S. forces elsewhere in the Pacific and at home on standby alert.
- 1962-05: At Thailand’s request, 1,000 U.S. Marines participating in SEATO exercises moved to the Laotian border.
- 1962-05: Kennedy announced that 4,000 additional U.S. troops had been ordered into Thailand; a SEATO Council meeting also requested token military deployments.
- 1962-06: The three Laotian princes signed an agreement to participate in a Government of National Union, ending the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the issue of whether and how to respond to the threat posed by the Pathet Lao advance and the resulting deployment of U.S. forces to Thailand. Over the crisis period, the U.S. position developed from monitoring the fighting and considering the regional threat to using a noncombat military deployment and standby alert while negotiations were reopened.

Crisis 194 — China/India Bdr. II (1962)

ACTION: China and India entered full-scale war after Chinese attacks along the disputed frontier, while India sought military assistance and continued fighting. The United States reached the “Show of Force” level without combat: it offered military aid, and a U.S. aircraft carrier was ordered toward the Bay of Bengal; India also requested 15 U.S. bomber and fighter squadrons for air cover, but the summary does not state that they were deployed.

TIMELINE:
- 1962-09: Chinese forces crossed the Thag La Ridge, prompting India to plan a military operation to evict them.
- 1962-10: India began implementing Operation Leghorn; China launched simultaneous attacks in the western and eastern sectors on 20 October.
- 1962-10: On 29 October, the U.S. ambassador in New Delhi offered military aid to India.
- 1962-11: After a second Chinese offensive, Nehru requested U.S. bomber and fighter squadrons on 19 November; a U.S. aircraft carrier was ordered toward the Bay of Bengal.
- 1962-11: China announced a unilateral cease-fire on 21 November.
- 1962-12: China began withdrawing forces 20 kilometers behind the specified lines on 1 December; nonaligned states pursued mediation at the Colombo Conference from 10–12 December.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. military assistance to India as the conflict escalated, including support associated with aircraft and other military equipment. The summary does not specify particular congressional votes or describe a shift in congressional decisions over the period.

Crisis 195 — Yemen War I (1962)

ACTION: Following Yemen’s 26 September 1962 coup, Egypt aided the Republican government while Jordan and Saudi Arabia aided the Royalists, producing a risk of broader Egyptian-Saudi fighting. The United States sent a warship to Jiddah as a warning against further attacks and stated that it would withdraw U.S. military aircraft and adopt neutrality if Yemen or Egypt attacked Saudi Arabia; it did not engage in combat.

TIMELINE:
- 1962-09: A coup overthrew Yemen’s monarchy, triggering the crisis.
- 1962-10: Jordan and Saudi Arabia delivered arms to the Royalists; Yemen ordered general mobilization on 4 October.
- 1962-11: Egypt dispatched several thousand troops to Yemen; Egyptian attacks on Saudi villages were announced on 4 November, followed by a Saudi decision to conduct reprisal raids.
- 1962-12: The United States sent a warship to Jiddah, offered its good offices, and recognized Yemen’s Republican government on 19 December.
- 1963-03: UN Under-Secretary-General Ralph Bunche conducted a fact-finding mission in Yemen from 1 to 4 March.
- 1963-04: A Saudi-Egyptian disengagement agreement was secured, and Jordan recognized the YAR and withdrew on 15 April; a cease-fire and disengagement agreement marked the end of the crisis phase.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary identifies no specific congressional decision or change in congressional action. The U.S. measures described were executive actions: a naval show of force, a warning concerning U.S. military aircraft, an offer of good offices, and recognition of the Republican government.

Crisis 196 — Cuban Missiles (1962)

ACTION: From 16 October to 20 November 1962, the United States confronted Soviet missile deployments in Cuba. It imposed a naval quarantine on offensive military equipment, increased mobilization and aerial reconnaissance, and weighed continued interception and military force; the crisis reached combat, with 1–5 U.S. deaths.

TIMELINE:
- 1962-10: CIA photographic evidence presented to President Kennedy showing Soviet missiles in Cuba.
- 1962-10: The United States decided to blockade offensive military equipment en route to Cuba; the quarantine was announced on 22 October.
- 1962-10: The Soviet Union offered to remove the missiles in exchange for ending the quarantine and a U.S. assurance not to invade Cuba; a U.S. surveillance plane was shot down on 27 October.
- 1962-10: Kennedy accepted the terms of Khrushchev’s 26 October proposal; on 28 October, Khrushchev agreed to stop work on the missile sites, remove the missiles, and permit UN verification.
- 1962-11: The United States and Soviet Union reached an arrangement allowing U.S. inspection and interception of Soviet ships leaving Cuba and photographing of the missiles; the quarantine was lifted on 20 November after Cuba’s agreement was conveyed.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the U.S. response to the Soviet missiles, including the use of force and measures to stop offensive military equipment from reaching Cuba. As the crisis progressed, the immediate decision shifted toward maintaining the quarantine and surveillance while pursuing negotiated removal and verification.

Crisis 197 — Malaysia Federation (1963)

ACTION: Indonesia opposed the proposed Malaysia Federation and later refused to recognize it, leading to diplomatic and economic confrontation. The United States suspended economic aid to Indonesia and sent Attorney-General Robert Kennedy to mediate; it did not use combat forces.

TIMELINE:
- 1963-02: Indonesia declared strong opposition to a Malaysia Federation; Malaya announced an immediate expansion of its armed forces two days later.
- 1963-07: The United Kingdom and the participating entities agreed in London to form the Federation of Malaysia; Indonesia sharply rejected the agreement.
- 1963-08: Indonesian, Malayan, and Philippine leaders agreed that the UN would ascertain the views of the people involved.
- 1963-09: Publication of results indicating support for a Malaysia Federation was followed by Indonesia’s refusal to recognize it and recall of its ambassador; Malaya severed diplomatic ties with Indonesia and the Philippines.
- 1963-09: The United States suspended economic aid to Indonesia and sent Attorney-General Robert Kennedy to mediate; Kennedy brokered an ambiguous cease-fire that was not implemented and encouraged further negotiations.
- 1965-08: Singapore seceded from Malaysia, which Indonesia viewed as the beginning of the Federation’s breakup; the confrontation then eased.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary identifies a U.S. decision to suspend economic aid to Indonesia and an active mediation effort, but does not specify a congressional vote or other concrete decision Congress faced. It also does not describe a shift in congressional action over the period.

Crisis 198 — Dom. Rep./Haiti II (1963)

ACTION: Haitian police entered the Dominican Republic’s embassy in Port-au-Prince on 26 April 1963 and arrested opponents of Haiti’s regime, prompting a confrontation that included a Dominican ultimatum, border troop deployments, and consideration of military action against Haiti. The United States reached a show-of-force level without combat, sending a naval task force because of concern for foreign citizens’ safety; it withdrew after the OAS issued its final report.

TIMELINE:
- 1963-04: Haitian police forcibly entered the Dominican Republic embassy and arrested opponents of Haiti’s regime; President Bosch issued a 24-hour ultimatum and appealed to the OAS.
- 1963-04: Haiti severed diplomatic relations with the Dominican Republic, which began stationing troops along its border with Haiti.
- 1963-04: An OAS fact-finding mission arrived in Haiti on 30 April.
- 1963-05: The crisis peaked on 5–6 May; the UN Security Council discussed the issue on 8–9 May, while the OAS created a five-nation mediation committee on 8 May.
- 1963-05: The possibility of a Dominican invasion had lessened by 10 May, and Duvalier released the hostages on 14 May, after which Dominican forces were demobilized.
- 1963-06: The OAS committee issued its final report on 3 June without recommending further action; the U.S. naval task force was withdrawn and normal relations with Haiti were restored.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of how to respond to the crisis and the safety of foreign citizens in Haiti while the United States maintained a noncombat show of force. As the OAS mediation process progressed, the immediate possibility of a Dominican invasion diminished, the hostages were released, and the U.S. naval deployment was withdrawn.

Crisis 206 — Panama Flag (1964)

ACTION: On 9 January 1964, a U.S. student flag-raising at Balboa High School prompted rioting in the Canal Zone; U.S. troops fired on Panamanian demonstrators, killing 26 and wounding 100. With the U.S. escalation level recorded as No Response, the summary identifies no additional U.S. military action or actively weighed escalation after the shooting; President Johnson pursued diplomatic and investigative measures to restore calm.

TIMELINE:
- 1964-01: U.S. students raised the U.S. flag at Balboa High School; rioting followed, and U.S. troops fired on Panamanian demonstrators.
- 1964-01: Panama broke diplomatic relations with the United States and filed complaints with the OAS and UN Security Council.
- 1964-01: President Johnson phoned President Chiari, expressed regret for the casualties, and called for joint efforts to restore calm.
- 1964-01: Johnson requested an OAS Peace Committee investigation and stated that order in the Canal Zone had to be restored before treaty revision.
- 1964-01: Chiari agreed to Johnson’s proposal, ending the crisis for both countries.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify a concrete decision facing Congress during the crisis. It records no shift in congressional decisions over the period.

Crisis 208 — Ogaden I (1964)

ACTION: Somalia attacked an Ethiopian frontier post at Tog Wajale on 1964-02-07, prompting Ethiopian military resistance and retaliation on Somali territory. The United States, whose escalation level actually reached **No Response**, took no military action and was not described as actively weighing military action in the summary.

TIMELINE:
- 1964-02: Border clashes preceded the crisis.
- 1964-02-07: Somali forces launched a large-scale attack on Ethiopia’s frontier post at Tog Wajale.
- 1964-02-08: Ethiopia retaliated on Somali territory.
- 1964-02-16: Ethiopia and Somalia accepted a cease-fire, which did not hold.
- 1964-03-30: The parties concluded a cease-fire agreement in Khartoum along the original boundary and established a joint border commission.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced no identified decision on U.S. military action in the summary. The United States did not respond militarily during the crisis.

Crisis 210 — Vietnam (1964)

ACTION: South Vietnamese forces attacked two North Vietnamese islands on 30 July 1964, followed by North Vietnamese attacks on U.S. destroyers on 2 and 4 August. The United States ordered an air attack on North Vietnamese gunboats and supporting facilities, while Congress considered broad authorization for further action; the broader U.S. escalation ultimately reached combat with more than 1,000 U.S. deaths.

TIMELINE:
- 1964-07: South Vietnamese patrol boats conducted a U.S.-backed covert 34A attack on two North Vietnamese islands.
- 1964-08: North Vietnamese torpedo boats attacked the USS *Maddox*; no casualties or damage resulted.
- 1964-08: North Vietnam conducted a second attack against two U.S. destroyers; no casualties or damage resulted.
- 1964-08: President Johnson ordered an air attack against North Vietnamese gunboats and supporting facilities.
- 1964-08: President Johnson asked Congress to authorize all necessary action to protect U.S. forces and assist countries covered by the SEATO treaty.
- 1964-08: Congress passed the Gulf of Tonkin Resolution, granting the president broad authority concerning U.S. and allied interests in Southeast Asia.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced a request to authorize all necessary action to protect U.S. armed forces and assist SEATO-covered countries. The decision shifted from considering the request on 5 August to approving the broad Gulf of Tonkin Resolution on 7 August.

Crisis 211 — Congo II (1964)

ACTION: Rebel forces occupied Stanleyville and formed a Revolutionary Council, while the detention of about 1,500 foreign civilians created an international hostage crisis. The United States and Belgium dispatched paratroops to a British base in Congo to prepare for a hostage rescue; the paratroops occupied Stanleyville and rescued the hostages on 24 November, constituting a show of force without U.S. combat.

TIMELINE:
- 1964-08: Rebel forces occupied Stanleyville and formed a Revolutionary Council under Christophe Gbenye; Congo appealed to the United States and Belgium for direct military aid.
- 1964-09: Gbenye announced that approximately 1,500 white foreign civilians held in Stanleyville could not leave and threatened harm if specified conditions were not met.
- 1964-11: The United States and Belgium dispatched paratroops to a British base in Congo to prepare for a military hostage-rescue operation.
- 1964-11:24: Paratroops occupied Stanleyville and rescued the hostages within a few hours; the Congo government simultaneously attacked the rebels.
- 1964-11:29: Control of Stanleyville was handed back to the central government, ending the U.S. and Belgian crisis.
- 1964-12:30: The UN Security Council adopted a resolution calling for a cease-fire, removal of mercenaries, and an end to foreign intervention, terminating the broader crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. military involvement in responding to the hostage situation, including the option of preparing and carrying out a rescue operation. Over the period, the issue shifted from whether and how to provide direct military assistance to the operation’s execution and completion without U.S. combat.

Crisis 213 — Pleiku (1965)

ACTION: On 7 February 1965, Vietcong guerrillas attacked U.S. and South Vietnamese barracks at Pleiku, killing eight and wounding 126. The United States decided on measured, limited air action jointly with South Vietnam against selected military targets in North Vietnam; the first U.S. strikes began on 2 March, marking a sharp escalation to combat-level involvement.

TIMELINE:
- 1965-01: A military coup took over South Vietnam’s government on 27 January, amid debate in the United States over commitments to Saigon.
- 1965-02: Vietcong guerrillas raided U.S. and South Vietnamese barracks at Pleiku on 7 February, killing eight and wounding 126.
- 1965-02: President Johnson, following a National Security Council meeting on 13 February, decided in favor of limited joint U.S.–South Vietnamese air action against selected targets in North Vietnam.
- 1965-02: News of Operation Rolling Thunder became public on 19 February, triggering a crisis for North Vietnam.
- 1965-03: The first U.S. air strikes began on 2 March, ending the Pleiku crisis for the United States and South Vietnam and escalating U.S. involvement.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the broader question of U.S. commitments to the government in Saigon and the concrete choice of whether to authorize or sustain the escalating U.S. response to attacks on U.S. forces. Over the period, the response moved from minimal U.S. participation to limited joint air strikes against selected targets in North Vietnam.

Crisis 215 — Dominican Interventn. (1965)

ACTION: Civil war began in the Dominican Republic on 24 April 1965 after Bosch supporters overthrew the military junta, placing U.S. embassy personnel and other foreign citizens at risk. The United States reached the combat level by deploying 400 Army paratroops and Marines on 28 April, expanding the force to 14,000 by 2 May and 22,000 by 20 May; Johnson subsequently assigned U.S. units to restore order.

TIMELINE:
- 1965-04: Bosch supporters overthrow the junta; civil war begins, and U.S. embassy personnel report that American lives are in danger.
- 1965-04: The United States decides to send 400 Army paratroops and Marines; it also calls for an OAS meeting on 30 April.
- 1965-05: The U.S. force reaches 14,000 by 2 May; foreign citizens are evacuated, and on 3 May Johnson assigns U.S. military units to restore order.
- 1965-05: The U.S. force reaches 22,000 by 20 May; OAS and UN diplomatic efforts continue.
- 1965-06: Mediation produces a compromise among the military junta, constitutionalists, and the United States.
- 1965-08: The Act of Dominican Reconciliation is signed on 31 August, ending the civil war and international crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not specify a concrete congressional decision concerning the intervention. Over the period, the U.S. position shifted from deploying forces to protect endangered personnel and evacuate foreign citizens to assigning military units the task of restoring order, while diplomatic efforts pursued a political settlement.

Crisis 218 — Rhodesia's Udi (1965)

ACTION: Rhodesia declared UDI on 11 November 1965 after imposing emergency powers, creating a crisis for Zambia, which depended on Rhodesia for transport, fuel, and power. The United States did not deploy combat forces; it provided or actively weighed noncombat support through sanctions-related measures and participation in an Anglo-American-Canadian oil airlift to Zambia, which began in early January 1966.

TIMELINE:
- 1965-11: Rhodesia proclaimed a state of emergency on 5 November; Rhodesian troops moved to the Zambian border on 9 November, and Rhodesia declared independence on 11 November.
- 1965-11: Zambia dispatched troops to the Zambezi, imposed emergency regulations, and introduced limited economic sanctions against Rhodesia.
- 1965-11: The UN Security Council condemned UDI on 12 November and appealed to member-states not to recognize or assist Rhodesia; a UN oil embargo followed.
- 1965-12: Zambian ministerial missions visited Washington and Moscow on 28 December seeking stronger responses to UDI and increased assistance to Zambia.
- 1966-01: An Anglo-American-Canadian oil airlift to Zambia began in response to the effects of the UN oil embargo.
- 1966-04: On 27 April, Britain initiated “talks about talks” with Rhodesia, ending the crisis for Zambia.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. participation in sanctions-related measures against Rhodesia and assistance to Zambia, including support connected to the oil airlift. Over the period, the U.S. role centered on aid and sanctions rather than consideration or use of combat forces.

Crisis 221 — Che Guevara-Bolivia (1967)

ACTION: A pro-Cuban guerrilla movement led by Che Guevara operated in southeast Bolivia, prompting the Bolivian government to request U.S. arms and aid. The United States sent 40 military advisers on 11 April 1967 to train Bolivian antiguerrilla forces and provided modern weapons, including helicopters; the U.S. did not deploy combat forces.

TIMELINE:
- 1967-03: Guerrillas ambushed a large Bolivian army patrol on 23 March; Bolivia mobilized forces and clashes followed.
- 1967-03: Bolivia requested U.S. arms and aid on 31 March after moving troops to the southeast.
- 1967-04: The United States sent 40 advisers on 11 April to train antiguerrilla forces and provided modern weapons, including helicopters.
- 1967-06: Bolivia declared a state of siege on 2 June after demonstrations by students and miners in support of the guerrillas.
- 1967-09: At an OAS Foreign Ministers Conference on 21 September, the United States urged tighter diplomatic, economic, and political isolation of Cuba; the OAS adopted a resolution condemning Cuban subversive activities.
- 1967-10: President Barrientos announced on 10 October that Guevara had been killed by government troops on 8 October and that the guerrilla movement had ended.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify any specific decision faced by the U.S. Congress. The U.S. action described was executive provision of arms, helicopters, and military advisers to support Bolivia’s counterinsurgency effort.

Crisis 222 — Six Day War (1967)

ACTION: Egypt moved additional forces into Sinai, secured withdrawal of the United Nations Emergency Force, and closed the Strait of Tiran, while Israel launched attacks against Egypt, Jordan, and Syria. In response to a Soviet warning of possible intervention, President Johnson ordered the U.S. Sixth Fleet to change course and reduced its restriction from the Syrian coast from 100 miles to 50 miles, a show of force without combat; U.S. and U.K. efforts to form a naval flotilla had also been considered but failed.

TIMELINE:
- 1967-05: Egypt overflew Israel’s Dimona nuclear research center, moved additional divisions into Sinai, and later closed the Strait of Tiran; the U.S. Sixth Fleet moved toward the eastern Mediterranean.
- 1967-05: The United Nations Emergency Force withdrew from Sinai after Egypt’s demand, and the U.S. and U.K. efforts to form a naval flotilla failed.
- 1967-06: Israel began attacks against Egypt and Jordan, and fighting expanded after Jordan and Egypt responded.
- 1967-06: Premier Kosygin warned of possible Soviet intervention.
- 1967-06: President Johnson ordered the Sixth Fleet to change course and reduced its restriction from the Syrian coast from 100 miles to 50 miles.
- 1967-06: Syria accepted a cease-fire, followed by a cease-fire for the other actors the next day.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of whether and how the United States should respond to the fighting and the possibility of Soviet intervention, including the use or positioning of U.S. naval forces. The U.S. action ultimately remained a naval show of force rather than combat, while efforts to create a naval flotilla were not successful.

Crisis 224 — Pueblo (1968)

ACTION: North Korean forces seized the U.S.S. Pueblo on 22 January 1968, amid a related North Korean commando raid against South Korea. Consistent with the “No Response” escalation level, the summary records no U.S. military response as having actually been carried out; it does record reservist call-ups and the dispatch of the Enterprise task force as U.S. actions, followed by withdrawal after negotiations continued at Panmunjom.

TIMELINE:
- 1968-01: North Korean commando unit penetrated Seoul in an unsuccessful attempt to assassinate South Korean President Park Chung Hee.
- 1968-01: North Korean patrol and torpedo boats seized the U.S.S. Pueblo; the United States demanded return of the ship and crew.
- 1968-01: The United States called up Army and Air Force reservists and diverted the Enterprise and its task force to the Sea of Japan.
- 1968-02: The United States and North Korea agreed to continue negotiations through the Panmunjom talks; the United States withdrew the Enterprise task force.
- 1968-04: Presidents Johnson and Park issued a joint communiqué providing South Korea a de facto U.S. security guarantee.
- 1968-12: After ten months of negotiations, the U.S. representative issued an apology and acknowledgments before the crew was returned; the Pueblo was not recovered.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the U.S. response to the seizure, including the implications of calling up reservists and deploying naval forces. As negotiations continued, the immediate military posture was reduced after the Enterprise task force was withdrawn, while the Pueblo and crew remained subjects of negotiation.

Crisis 225 — Tet Offensive (1968)

ACTION: The Vietcong launched a major offensive across South Vietnam, including attacks on Saigon, Hué, provincial capitals, military installations, and district towns. The United States was already conducting combat operations and rushed infantry and tanks into Saigon, placed a U.S. general in control of the operation, and ordered additional combat troops into the area; on 31 March, it halted bombing of North Vietnam and renewed its willingness to negotiate.

TIMELINE:
- 1968-01: North Vietnamese forces attacked a U.S. Marine outpost near the Demilitarized Zone on 21 January.
- 1968-01: On 30 January, Vietcong forces attacked Saigon, Hué, 36 provincial capitals, military installations, and district towns.
- 1968-02: U.S. infantry and tanks entered Saigon on 1 February; the Vietcong main forces withdrew on 3 February, then launched another attack around the capital on 18 February.
- 1968-02: On 24 February, Vietcong forces were expelled from the capital area; the United States ordered more combat troops into the area.
- 1968-02: A pessimistic report by General Wheeler on 27 February triggered a U.S. crisis.
- 1968-03: On 31 March, President Johnson announced a halt to the bombing of North Vietnam and renewed U.S. willingness to negotiate.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. combat operations and the further commitment of U.S. forces during the offensive. Over the period, the executive policy shifted from continued combat operations and additional troop deployments toward halting the bombing of North Vietnam and reopening a willingness to negotiate.

Crisis 227 — Prague Spring (1968)

ACTION: The Prague Spring involved Czechoslovakia’s 1968 political and economic liberalization, which the Soviet Union and several Warsaw Pact states viewed as threatening to communist control and alliance cohesion. The United States took no military action and did not reach an escalation response level; the summary identifies no U.S. military option actively being weighed.

TIMELINE:
- 1968-01: Alexander Dubcek replaced Antonin Novotny as First Secretary of the Czechoslovak Communist Party.
- 1968-04: Czechoslovakia published its Action Program, including reduced censorship and restructured economic and political controls.
- 1968-06: Warsaw Pact military exercises were held in Czechoslovakia; troop withdrawal was delayed amid rising tensions.
- 1968-07: The Soviet Union, East Germany, Poland, Hungary, and Bulgaria issued an ultimatum to Czechoslovakia concerning the reform movement.
- 1968-08: Warsaw Pact forces invaded Czechoslovakia beginning 20 August and established control within 36 hours.
- 1968-10: The Moscow Protocol, signed on 18 October, legalized the presence of Soviet troops and terminated the crisis for the Soviet Union and Czechoslovakia.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced no concrete U.S. military decision identified in the summary. Because the United States reached a level of no response, the summary does not indicate that congressional choices shifted over the period.

Crisis 230 — Vietnam Spring Off. (1969)

ACTION: From 22 February to 8 June 1969, North Vietnam conducted a countrywide offensive in South Vietnam, while U.S. casualties mounted. The United States actively weighed and twice temporarily canceled bombing Cambodian sanctuaries before ordering and carrying out the strikes beginning 18 March (“Operation Menu”); the crisis occurred at the combat level, with more than 1,000 U.S. deaths.

TIMELINE:
- 1969-02: North Vietnam launched a countrywide offensive in South Vietnam on 22 February, triggering the U.S. crisis.
- 1969-02: On 23 February, Nixon ordered bombing of Cambodian sanctuaries; the order was canceled on 25 February.
- 1969-03: Bombing orders were issued again on 4 March and retracted on 7 March.
- 1969-03: On 15 March, Nixon ordered bombing of Cambodian sanctuaries; strikes began on 18 March, ending the U.S. crisis.
- 1969-05: North Vietnam launched a major attack throughout South Vietnam on 12 May; on 14 May, Nixon presented an eight-point peace plan including a withdrawal timetable and internationally supervised cease-fire.
- 1969-06: Nixon and South Vietnamese President Thieu met on 8 June; Nixon reaffirmed U.S. support and announced redeployment of 25,000 U.S. troops from Vietnam.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the continuation of U.S. combat operations amid the offensive and casualties, including the use of force against Cambodian sanctuaries. By May and June, the policy choices also included a proposed withdrawal timetable and the redeployment of U.S. troops from Vietnam.

Crisis 233 — EC-121 Spy Plane (1969)

ACTION: On 15 April 1969, North Korea shot down a U.S. Navy EC-121 reconnaissance plane over the Sea of Japan; the United States stated that the aircraft had remained in international airspace. The United States responded with a naval show of force, resumed reconnaissance flights with armed escorts, and repositioned a major naval force, while actively considering options ranging from diplomatic protests to stronger military retaliation; it did not undertake combat action.

TIMELINE:
- 1969-04: North Korea shot down a U.S. EC-121 reconnaissance plane with 31 men aboard; the United States dispatched Task Force 7 to the Sea of Japan and formed an ad hoc National Security Council group to assess response options.
- 1969-04: President Nixon ordered reconnaissance flights to resume with armed escorts and directed the repositioning of the U.S. naval force in the Sea of Japan.
- 1969-04-22: The Soviet Union issued a mild protest against U.S. activity in the Sea of Japan.
- 1969-04-26: The crisis ended when the U.S. naval task force moved from the Sea of Japan into the Yellow Sea after no further incidents.
