BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the U.S. response to North Korea’s shootdown, including the broader question of whether to authorize or support stronger military retaliation beyond the show-of-force measures taken. Over the period, the immediate U.S. response centered on diplomatic options, escorted reconnaissance, and naval deployment rather than combat.

Crisis 237 — Invasion Of Cambodia (1970)

ACTION: Communist forces expanded operations from bases in eastern Cambodia, while Cambodian forces lost border positions and requested foreign aid. The United States decided to send combat troops into Cambodia on 28 April 1970; U.S. troops remained there through 30 June, consistent with the recorded escalation level of combat and 251–500 U.S. deaths.

TIMELINE:
- 1970-03: Cambodia demanded that Vietcong and North Vietnamese forces withdraw within 48 hours.
- 1970-03: Cambodian authorities deposed Prince Sihanouk and declared a state of emergency; Sihanouk subsequently announced plans for a Government of National Union and National Liberation Army.
- 1970-03: North Vietnamese forces attacked a Cambodian regiment, beginning the combined Vietcong, North Vietnamese, and Sihanoukist invasion of Cambodia.
- 1970-04: Cambodian forces evacuated border positions in Parrot’s Beak, and Cambodia requested U.S. aid.
- 1970-04: Communist forces attacked Takeo and cut its road connection with Phnom Penh; on 28 April, President Nixon decided to send U.S. troops into Cambodia.
- 1970-06: U.S. troops remained in Cambodia until 30 June; South Vietnam began withdrawing from Cambodia on 22 July, ending the crisis for Cambodia and South Vietnam.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the U.S. deployment of combat troops into Cambodia after the Cambodian government requested assistance. Over the period, the issue shifted from whether to provide aid to whether and how to undertake direct U.S. military operations, followed by the withdrawal of U.S. troops by 30 June.

Crisis 238 — Black September (1970)

ACTION: Syria invaded Jordan on 19 September 1970, prompting Jordan to appeal to the United States for aid as Israel prepared to act if necessary. The United States reached a show-of-force level without combat: it provided for umbrella support to Israel if the USSR and Egypt became involved, approved a possible joint military plan, placed airborne divisions and forces on semi-alert, and moved naval assets toward the eastern Mediterranean.

TIMELINE:
- 1970-09: King Hussein changed his cabinet to include military personnel, signaling an effort to confront the PLO challenge.
- 1970-09: Syria invaded Jordan; Jordan engaged Syrian forces and appealed to the United States for aid.
- 1970-09: Israel decided to take military action if necessary to prevent a Palestinian victory in Jordan.
- 1970-09: The United States decided to provide umbrella support to Israel if the USSR and Egypt became involved; a possible joint U.S.-Israeli military plan was approved by President Nixon.
- 1970-09: Jordanian air forces entered the fighting, while Syrian tanks withdrew from Jordan; the United States maintained military forces on semi-alert and moved naval assets toward the eastern Mediterranean.
- 1970-09: Jordan and Syria signed a cease-fire agreement on 27 September, implemented on 29 September.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of whether and how the United States should support Jordan and Israel amid the Syrian invasion and the risk of broader involvement. Over the period, the U.S. position developed from considering possible military action and support contingencies to maintaining a show of force without entering combat.

Crisis 239 — Cienfuegos Sub. Base (1970)

ACTION: From September to October 1970, the United States responded to intelligence indicating Soviet construction of a possible submarine base at Cienfuegos, Cuba. At the escalation level of threats of force, U.S. actions consisted of monitoring, demanding explanations, and warning Soviet officials while actively weighing the implications of the 1962 agreement; the summary does not report U.S. military action.

TIMELINE:
- 1970-09: U.S. intelligence flights provided information on Soviet construction at Cienfuegos; Kissinger informed Nixon on 16 September.
- 1970-09: Nixon warned Foreign Minister Gromyko that the United States was monitoring developments.
- 1970-09: On 25 September, the issue became public in the U.S.; Kissinger warned Ambassador Dobrynin and demanded an explanation. Dobrynin replied on 27 September that the 1962 agreement had not been violated because no offensive weapons were installed.
- 1970-10: On 5 October, Kissinger again met Dobrynin and sought a definition of a “base”; on 13 October, Tass denied that the Soviet Union was building a base.
- 1970-10: On 22 October, Gromyko reaffirmed the 1962 agreement to Nixon; U.S. imagery showed construction slowing and later stopping.
- 1970-10: On 23 October, the crisis ended after Washington received Soviet assurances that construction had halted and the Soviet naval force had left Cienfuegos.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify a concrete congressional decision or legislative action during the crisis. The U.S. response described remained focused on executive monitoring, diplomatic warnings, and demands for clarification, with no reported shift to a congressional decision over military action.

Crisis 240 — Conakry Raid (1970)

ACTION: On 22 November 1970, Guinea reported an invasion by 350–400 mercenaries, allegedly supported by Portugal, and sought international assistance. The United States provided economic aid to Guinea; it did not deploy combat forces, and the summary does not identify a U.S. decision to actively weigh such deployment.

TIMELINE:
- 1970-11: Guinea announced the invasion and appealed for emergency UN and OAU assistance.
- 1970-11: The UN Security Council met from 22–24 November and called for an end to the attack and withdrawal of invading forces.
- 1970-11: Guinea appealed to friendly countries for military support; the United States sent economic aid.
- 1970-12: The UN fact-finding report, published 4 December, confirmed the invasion and alleged outside support appearing to be Portuguese.
- 1970-12: On 8 December, the Security Council warned Portugal against further attacks and called for self-determination and independence for Portuguese Guinea.
- 1970-12: On 11 December, the OAU condemned Portugal and demanded reparations, followed by a tacit agreement to end the invasion.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify a specific congressional decision concerning the crisis. The U.S. action described was economic aid rather than deployment of combat forces; no shift in congressional decisions is specified.

Crisis 241 — Invasion Of Laos II (1971)

ACTION: On 8 February 1971, South Vietnamese forces invaded Laos with U.S. air support to disrupt the Ho Chi Minh Trail. The United States reached the combat level, sustaining 6–25 deaths; it did not use ground forces, while actively providing air support and later airlifting South Vietnamese troops out of Laos.

TIMELINE:
- 1971-02: South Vietnamese and U.S. forces began the invasion of Laos, with U.S. air support.
- 1971-02: The International Control Commission convened to investigate the violation of Laotian neutrality.
- 1971-02: Laos declared a state of emergency, and North Vietnam began the first wave of a counteroffensive.
- 1971-03: South Vietnamese forces captured Tchepone on 7 March; North Vietnamese forces recaptured it after launching a counteroffensive on 12 March.
- 1971-03: The United States began airlifting South Vietnamese troops out of Laos; the withdrawal was completed on 25 March.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. air support for the South Vietnamese operation and the possible use of U.S. ground forces. The United States stated that it would not use ground forces and repeatedly denied support for a possible South Vietnamese invasion of North Vietnam.

Crisis 242 — Bangladesh (1971)

ACTION: Fighting began on 25 March 1971 when West Pakistani forces attacked in Dacca, followed by Bangladesh’s declaration of independence and Pakistan’s suppression of the revolt. The United States did not engage in combat; at the Show of Force level, it moved the Seventh Fleet into the Bay of Bengal on 13 December in support of Pakistan while the India-Pakistan war was underway.

TIMELINE:
- 1971-03: West Pakistani forces attacked Dacca University student dormitories; East Bengali leaders declared independence, and Pakistan outlawed the Awami League and launched a violent suppression campaign.
- 1971-08: The Soviet Union signed a Treaty of Friendship with India, providing superpower support in the event of war with Pakistan.
- 1971-10: Pakistan ordered troops concentrated on the Indian Punjab border, bringing the situation to crisis proportions for India.
- 1971-11: India crossed into West Pakistan on 21 November.
- 1971-12: Pakistan attacked Indian airfields on 3 December; the U.S. Seventh Fleet moved into the Bay of Bengal on 13 December, and Pakistan surrendered on 17 December.
- 1971-12: The United Nations General Assembly called for a cease-fire on 7 December, and the Security Council passed a resolution on 22 December calling for withdrawal of armed forces as soon as practicable.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the U.S. response to the conflict and the extent of support or pressure directed toward Pakistan, India, and the broader crisis. As the crisis expanded from Pakistan’s internal suppression of the Bangladesh revolt to war between India and Pakistan, the U.S. posture culminated in a noncombat show of force through the movement of the Seventh Fleet into the Bay of Bengal.

Crisis 246 — Vietnam Ports Mining (1972)

ACTION: North Vietnam launched a spring offensive in South Vietnam on 1972-03-30, prompting renewed U.S. B-52 bombing of the Hanoi–Haiphong industrial complex. After further North Vietnamese advances, President Nixon ordered the mining of all North Vietnamese ports on 1972-05-08 to impede military shipments by sea; U.S. involvement reached combat, with 26–50 U.S. deaths.

TIMELINE:
- 1972-03: North Vietnam launched a spring offensive in South Vietnam; South Vietnam began defensive fighting, and the United States renewed B-52 bombing.
- 1972-04: North Vietnamese forces attacked South Vietnamese troops in the Central Highlands, forcing their withdrawal.
- 1972-05: President Nixon ordered the mining of all North Vietnamese ports.
- 1972-05: North Vietnam denounced the mining at the Paris Peace Talks; U.S.–Soviet discussions, including a Moscow summit, contributed to Soviet pressure on North Vietnam.
- 1972-07: Talks in Paris resumed on 19 July with a serious exchange of views and a promise of compromise.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. military action in Vietnam as the conflict escalated from renewed bombing to the mining of North Vietnamese ports. Over the period, the concrete U.S. action at issue shifted from air operations to mining and preventing military shipments from reaching North Vietnam by sea.

Crisis 249 — Christmas Bombing (1972)

ACTION: U.S.–North Vietnamese negotiations stalled as Hanoi rejected U.S. proposals and withdrew earlier acceptance, while fighting continued in Vietnam. On 14 December, President Nixon ordered renewed aerial bombing of the Hanoi–Haiphong military complex and mining of North Vietnamese ports; massive bombing began on 17 December, reaching the stated combat level with 26–50 U.S. deaths.

TIMELINE:
- 1972-10: Peace talks remained deadlocked; South Vietnam publicly refused to comply with agreements reached by U.S. and North Vietnamese delegates.
- 1972-12: North Vietnam rejected U.S. proposals and withdrew its acceptance of earlier changes; meetings on 6, 11, and 13 December ended in stalemate.
- 1972-12: President Nixon ordered renewed bombing of the Hanoi–Haiphong military complex and mining of North Vietnamese ports.
- 1972-12: Massive bombing began on 17 December; on 26 December, the U.S. bombed the Hanoi railway station, killing 283 people and wounding 266.
- 1972-12: North Vietnam signaled on 26 December that it was willing to resume serious negotiations without preconditions.
- 1973-01: The Peace Accords were formally signed in Paris on 27 January, ending the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. military escalation, including renewed aerial bombing and the mining of North Vietnamese ports. Over the period, the immediate issue shifted from the stalled negotiations and proposals to the conduct of intensified bombing and mining operations, followed by the return to negotiations.

Crisis 255 — October-Yom Kippur War (1973)

ACTION: Egypt and Syria attacked Israel beginning 6 October 1973, while subsequent Israeli advances and Soviet support for the Arab states raised U.S. concerns about a possible U.S.-Soviet confrontation. The United States pressured Israel to stop fighting and permit nonmilitary supplies to reach Egypt’s encircled Third Army, while also issuing a warning to Moscow and placing most U.S. forces, including the Strategic Air Command, on Defensive Condition 3; the U.S. escalation level reached threats of force.

TIMELINE:
- 1973-10: Egyptian and Syrian forces attacked Israel; Israel raised its alert level and strengthened forces along its borders.
- 1973-10: Israel crossed the Suez Canal and threatened to surround Egypt’s Third Army.
- 1973-10-22: Israel and Egypt accepted a cease-fire agreement after Egypt sought Soviet help.
- 1973-10-24: The Soviet Union warned that it might intervene unilaterally unless Israel stopped its advance; the United States increased pressure on Israel and placed most U.S. forces on Defensive Condition 3.
- 1973-10-25: A Soviet freighter arrived in Alexandria reportedly carrying nuclear weapons.
- 1973-10-26: All parties accepted a U.S.-Soviet-sponsored Security Council cease-fire resolution.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the concrete issue of the U.S. response to the fighting, including the possibility of military readiness measures and pressure on Israel to halt operations and permit supplies to reach Egypt’s Third Army. As the crisis developed, the immediate decision shifted from responding to the Arab-Israeli war to managing the risk of possible Soviet intervention.

Crisis 256 — Oman/South Yemen (1973)

ACTION: South Yemen provided military assistance to rebels fighting Oman in Dhofar, including training and active participation in the conflict. The United States provided military assistance to Oman; at the recorded escalation level, it did not deploy combat forces or conduct combat operations, and no higher U.S. action was recorded as actively weighed.

TIMELINE:
- 1973-11: Oman announced that South Yemeni aircraft had attacked a military post and that South Yemeni forces were participating in the Dhofar fighting.
- 1973-12: Oman launched a successful campaign to retake the Muscat-Salala road from the rebels.
- 1974-05: The League of Arab States established a conciliation commission, which later failed to mediate the dispute.
- 1975-01: Reports indicated that guerrilla supply lines from South Yemen had been blocked.
- 1975-10: The rebels were completely cut off from supplies; by the end of the year they had been driven from their last Dhofar strongholds and crossed into South Yemen.
- 1976-03: Oman and South Yemen signed a cease-fire under Saudi mediation after a Saudi-South Yemeni agreement to halt South Yemeni assistance to the Omani rebels.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the concrete issue of U.S. military assistance to Oman during the conflict. The recorded U.S. response remained at aid or sanctions, with no U.S. combat force deployed and no shift to a higher level of military involvement specified.

Crisis 257 — Cyprus III (1974)

ACTION: A coup in Cyprus on 15 July 1974, followed by Turkey’s invasion on 20 July and subsequent territorial advances, produced fighting and negotiations over a cease-fire and Cyprus’s political status. The United States actively pursued mediation and, at the Aid or Sanctions level, stopped military aid to Turkey in December; it did not use combat forces.

TIMELINE:
- 1974-07: A coup overthrew parliamentary rule in Cyprus; U.S. and British diplomats began mediation, and the United States joined calls for restraint.
- 1974-07: Turkey invaded Cyprus after rejecting pleas from the United States, United Kingdom, and United Nations for restraint.
- 1974-07: A civilian government returned in Greece, and peace negotiations involving the United Kingdom, Greece, Turkey, and a Soviet observer opened.
- 1974-08: Talks broke down, Turkish forces advanced across the northern part of Cyprus, and a cease-fire took effect on 16 August under UN supervision.
- 1974-12: The United States stopped military aid to Turkey, while continuing diplomatic involvement in the crisis.
- 1975-02: A Turkish-Cypriot Federated State was proclaimed and a Constituent Assembly convened, ending the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the concrete question of whether U.S. military aid to Turkey should continue amid Turkey’s military action in Cyprus. The policy shifted over the period from active U.S. mediation and diplomacy to stopping military aid to Turkey in December.

Crisis 258 — Final N. Vietnam Off. (1974)

ACTION: North Vietnamese forces launched an offensive against South Vietnam on 14 December 1974, while the Khmer Rouge launched a major offensive in Cambodia on 1 January 1975. The United States provided economic and military aid, appealed to Congress for additional assistance, assisted Vietnamese refugees, and by the end of the crisis limited its activity to evacuating U.S. citizens; it did not use combat forces.

TIMELINE:
- 1974-12: North Vietnam began its offensive against South Vietnam.
- 1975-01: The Khmer Rouge launched a major offensive in Cambodia.
- 1975-01: President Ford appealed to Congress for aid; Secretary of State Kissinger subsequently made a further appeal.
- 1975-03: South Vietnamese President Thieu initiated a tactical retreat on 11 March.
- 1975-04: Phnom Penh fell to the rebels on 17 April, and the Cambodian government fled.
- 1975-04: Saigon fell on 30 April; U.S. activity by the end of the crisis was limited to evacuating U.S. citizens.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions on whether to continue or increase aid to Cambodia and South Vietnam after appeals from President Ford and Secretary of State Kissinger. Over the period, Congress cut aid to both governments, while U.S. assistance also went to Vietnamese refugees.

Crisis 259 — Mayaguez (1975)

ACTION: On 12 May 1975, Khmer Rouge forces seized the U.S.-registered cargo ship *Mayaguez* and its crew off Cambodian coastal waters, prompting U.S. demands for their return and a threat to use force if necessary. President Ford authorized military force on 14 May; U.S. Marines conducted combat operations, including clashes near Koh Tong, and the United States ceased operations on 15 May after the ship and crew were released.

TIMELINE:
- 1975-05: Khmer Rouge forces seize the *Mayaguez* and its crew off Cambodian coastal waters.
- 1975-05: The United States demands the return of the ship and crew.
- 1975-05: The United States threatens to use force if necessary.
- 1975-05: President Ford decides to use military force at the fourth National Security Council meeting on the crisis.
- 1975-05: Cambodia releases the ship and crew shortly after fighting begins; clashes continue near Koh Tong.
- 1975-05: The United States ceases military operations, ending the crisis for Cambodia.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the U.S. response to the seizure, including whether and how to authorize or support military action to secure the return of the ship and crew. Over the period, the issue shifted from demands and a threat of force to the conduct of combat operations after the president’s decision to use military force.

Crisis 260 — War In Angola (1975)

ACTION: Fighting over Angola’s post-independence government escalated as the MPLA received Soviet and Cuban support and the FNLA/UNITA alliance received external assistance. The United States reached a “No Response” escalation level: it did not take direct military action, while it increased covert aid to Zaire for funneling to the FNLA and UNITA and considered emergency aid that required congressional approval.

TIMELINE:
- 1975-07: The MPLA attacked FNLA headquarters in Luanda; Zambia appealed to the United States for aid on 27 July.
- 1975-09: After an MPLA offensive and the arrival of Cuban troops, the United States increased covert aid to Zaire on 25 September; President Ford requested emergency aid for Zaire from Congress.
- 1975-10: South Africa dispatched a strike force into Angola on 23 October to assist the FNLA/UNITA alliance.
- 1975-11: Portugal withdrew its remaining personnel on 10 November; the MPLA and FNLA/UNITA declared Angolan independence separately.
- 1975-12: On 19 December, Congress refused aid to Zaire and declined to support the FNLA/UNITA, ending the U.S. crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions on whether to provide emergency aid to Zaire and whether to support the FNLA/UNITA. The administration had increased covert assistance before Congress refused further aid and support on 19 December.

Crisis 261 — Moroccan March (1975)

ACTION: Morocco sought to assert sovereignty over Western Sahara through a planned civilian march after the International Court of Justice ruling, while Spain sought to prevent a military clash. The United States reached an aid-or-sanctions escalation level, involving no U.S. combat force; the summary provided does not specify a U.S. military deployment or a particular aid or sanctions measure.

TIMELINE:
- 1975-10: The ICJ ruled that Morocco and Mauritania had no valid claim to sovereignty over Western Sahara; King Hassan announced plans for a 350,000-person civilian march.
- 1975-10: Spain appealed to the UN Security Council and imposed emergency measures to prevent hostilities.
- 1975-11: Spain and Algeria announced that the march would be countered by force; Morocco moved 20,000 troops to the Western Sahara border.
- 1975-11: The march began on 6 November and was halted by King Hassan on 10 November.
- 1975-11: Morocco, Mauritania, and Spain signed an agreement on 14 November dividing Western Sahara and providing for Spain’s withdrawal.
- 1976-02: Spain withdrew from Western Sahara on 26 February; Morocco and Mauritania later fixed their borders in the territory on 14 March.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the level and form of U.S. involvement at the aid-or-sanctions level, without committing U.S. combat forces. The summary does not specify how those congressional decisions changed during the crisis.

Crisis 273 — Nagomia Raid (1976)

ACTION: On 9 August 1976, Rhodesian forces raided a guerrilla camp at Nagomia in Mozambique, prompting a Mozambican mortar attack on Umtali, Rhodesia, and further reported attacks on Mozambican territory. The United States did not use combat forces; it concluded agreements for $10 million in aid to Mozambique after Secretary of State Kissinger’s September visit.

TIMELINE:
- 1976-03: President Machel announced the closure of Mozambique’s borders with Rhodesia, and Mozambique was placed on a war footing.
- 1976-08: Rhodesian forces raided Nagomia on 9 August; Mozambique responded with a mortar attack on Umtali on 11 August, while the United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees investigated the camp.
- 1976-08: Rhodesia presented captured documents on 29 August as evidence that the Nagomia camp was a military base.
- 1976-09: The Front-Line States met in Dar es Salaam on 6 September to coordinate defense policies; Secretary of State Kissinger arrived in the area on 13 September.
- 1976-09: Agreements were concluded for $10 million in U.S. aid to Mozambique.
- 1976-11: The Front-Line States’ Inter-State Defense Commission decided to organize joint defensive action, ending the crisis for Mozambique.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary identifies U.S. aid to Mozambique as the concrete U.S. measure but does not specify a congressional vote, authorization, or other decision before Congress. It provides no account of a shift in congressional decisions during the crisis.

Crisis 274 — Poplar Tree (1976)

ACTION: From 17 August to 16 September 1976, a clash in the DMZ killed two U.S. soldiers and wounded nine during efforts to prune a poplar tree. The United States used a show of force without combat: it raised forces in South Korea to DEFCON 3, deployed F-111s, a naval task force, and B-52s, and then conducted Operation Paul Bunyan to cut down the tree under orders not to use force first.

TIMELINE:
- 1976-08-17: Two U.S. soldiers were killed and nine wounded in a clash with North Korean forces in the Joint Security Area; President Ford was informed.
- 1976-08-18: The WSAG began considering and directing visible U.S. military measures in response.
- 1976-08-19: The United States dispatched F-111s and a naval task force, conducted a B-52 training mission, and raised U.S. forces in South Korea to DEFCON 3; Kim Il-Sung ordered North Korean forces into a war posture.
- 1976-08-20: President Ford approved General Stilwell’s plan to cut down the poplar tree, with instructions that U.S. forces not use force first.
- 1976-08-21: Operation Paul Bunyan cut down the tree, with U.S. and South Korean forces protecting the operation and U.S. bombers, fighters, and helicopter-gunships supporting it; no violence occurred.
- 1976-09-06: The United States and North Korea signed a new Joint Security Area agreement, which took effect on 16 September.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the broader question of how to respond to the killing and wounding of U.S. personnel, including whether to authorize or support further military measures. Over the period, the U.S. approach moved from demanding North Korean responsibility, assurances, and punishment toward a completed noncombat show of force and a negotiated JSA agreement.

Crisis 277 — Shaba I (1977)

ACTION: On 8 March 1977, Katangan exiles based in Angola invaded Zaire’s Shaba province, while Zaire accused Angola, Cuba, and the USSR of involvement. The United States provided nonlethal aid to Zaire and did not use combat forces; Zaire also sought assistance from African states and Belgium, while Morocco and Egypt provided military personnel and France supplied the airlift or advisors.

TIMELINE:
- 1977-03: Katangan exiles invaded Shaba; Zaire appealed to African states, Belgium, and the United States for help, and the United States supplied nonlethal aid.
- 1977-03: Angola’s Defense Ministry disclaimed responsibility for the invasion; the FLNC stated that its objective was to overthrow Mobutu’s regime.
- 1977-04: Nigerian mediation efforts included a meeting that appeared potentially relevant to an inter-African observer group, but no sustainable agreement resulted.
- 1977-05: Moroccan troops and French advisors helped Zaire stop the rebel advance and retake towns and villages.
- 1977-05: Morocco completed its mission and withdrew on 22 May; Egyptian personnel withdrew on 25 May, reducing Angola’s concern about internationalization.
- 1977-05: Zairian troops recaptured the last rebel-held town on 26 May, ending the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of whether and how to provide assistance to Zaire in response to its appeal for help. During the crisis, the U.S. action remained limited to nonlethal aid, with no U.S. combat-force deployment or stated decision to undertake one.

Crisis 281 — Egypt/Libya Clashes (1977)

ACTION: Egypt and Libya entered a border crisis after Egyptian forces attacked a Libyan border police fort on 14 July 1977, followed by Libyan artillery fire and an Egyptian ground and air incursion. The United States provided arms and weapons to Egypt, reaching the stated aid-or-sanctions level without deploying combat forces or conducting combat operations.

TIMELINE:
- 1977-07: Egyptian armed forces attacked a Libyan border police fort on 14 July; Libya responded on 19 July with artillery fire on an Egyptian border post, killing nine soldiers and capturing 14.
- 1977-07: On 21 July, Egyptian forces supported by tanks and aircraft crossed into Libya.
- 1977-07: Mediators traveled to Egypt and Libya and called for a cease-fire on 24 July, which was upheld.
- 1977-08: Intermittent clashes continued, with both sides accusing the other of aggression; the United States provided arms and weapons to Egypt.
- 1977-09: Egyptian and Libyan troops withdrew from the border on 10 September, terminating the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of whether to authorize or condition U.S. arms assistance to Egypt during a regional border crisis. Over the period, the U.S. action remained aid to Egypt; the summary does not identify a shift to sanctions or deployment of U.S. combat forces.

Crisis 282 — Ogaden II (1977)

ACTION: Somalia-backed forces launched a full-scale attack and captured most of the Ogaden, prompting Ethiopia to seek Soviet and Cuban military assistance. The United States provided military aid and weapons to Somalia; it did not deploy combat forces, consistent with an escalation level of Aid or Sanctions.

TIMELINE:
- 1977-07: Somalia-backed Western Somalia Liberation Front forces launched a full-scale attack in the Ogaden.
- 1977-08: Ethiopia severed diplomatic relations with Somalia; OAU delegates met with Somali and Ethiopian leaders on 8–9 August, but mediation failed.
- 1977-10: Somali forces controlled approximately 90 percent of the Ogaden, while the Soviet Union began an airlift of weapons to Ethiopia.
- 1978-01: Ethiopian and Cuban forces began a counteroffensive on 21 January.
- 1978-03: Jijiga was retaken in early March; Somalia announced on 9 March that it would withdraw from the Ogaden.
- 1978-03: Somalia completed its withdrawal on 14 March, ending the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. military assistance and weapons provision to Somalia as Somali forces advanced and later faced an Ethiopian-Cuban counteroffensive. The U.S. response remained at military aid rather than deployment of combat forces.

Crisis 292 — Shaba II (1978)

ACTION: Katangan rebels invaded Shaba from Angola on 1978-05-11, prompting Zaire to seek foreign intervention and accuse Angola and other governments of supporting the rebels. The United States reached the level of a show of force without combat: it decided to mount an airlift carrying foreign troops to the battleground, and U.S. planes transported French and Belgian forces to Zaire; U.S. forces did not engage in combat.

TIMELINE:
- 1978-05: Katangan rebels invaded Shaba from Angola; Zaire called for U.S. and other foreign intervention.
- 1978-05: The United States decided to mount an airlift to carry foreign troops to the battleground.
- 1978-05: French and Belgian troops arrived in Zaire on U.S. aircraft.
- 1978-05: Belgian forces began withdrawing after evacuating civilians; the U.S. crisis ended.
- 1978-06: Foreign troops left the conflict area, while U.S. diplomat Donald McHenry began mediation efforts between Angola and Zaire.
- 1978-07: Angola and Zaire concluded the Brazzaville Agreement, pledging to end border incursions and disarm or halt support for forces operating across their borders.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of whether and how to address the U.S. response to the crisis, including the airlift of foreign troops to Zaire. Over the period, the concrete U.S. action remained logistical support at the show-of-force level, without U.S. combat involvement.

Crisis 294 — Nicaragua Civ War II (1978)

ACTION: The United States addressed the Nicaragua civil war through diplomacy, mediation, military-aid decisions, and regional initiatives, without deploying combat forces. It ended military aid on 1979-02-08, continued refusing arms sales during the renewed fighting, and proposed an OAS peacekeeping force that was rejected.

TIMELINE:
- 1978-09: Fighting and cross-border attacks involving Nicaragua, Costa Rica, and Sandinista forces led to OAS consideration of the crisis; the United States expressed concern about reported atrocities by Nicaragua’s National Guard.
- 1978-09: Nicaragua agreed to U.S. mediation, and on 1978-09-29 accepted an OAS mediation team sponsored by the Dominican Republic, Guatemala, and the United States.
- 1978-10: The mediation team held its first meeting.
- 1979-01: Mediation efforts collapsed by mid-month.
- 1979-02: The United States ended all military aid to Nicaragua.
- 1979-05: After renewed fighting and a guerrilla invasion from Costa Rica, the United States continued refusing to sell arms to Nicaragua and proposed dispatching a peacekeeping force through the OAS; the proposal was rejected.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. military assistance to Nicaragua as the crisis developed. The U.S. position shifted from mediation and existing assistance to ending military aid and continuing an arms refusal, while considering but not implementing a peacekeeping deployment.

Crisis 301 — North/South Yemen II (1979)

ACTION: Fighting between North and South Yemen escalated into open cross-border warfare on 24 February 1979, with each side accusing the other of initiating the attacks. The United States did not use combat forces; on 28 February it announced a speedup of Saudi-financed U.S. arms deliveries to North Yemen and had North Yemen’s appeal for additional arms under consideration.

TIMELINE:
- 1978-06: The assassination of the YAR president sharply increased tensions, and relations with the PDRY were severed.
- 1979-02-24: North Yemen reported a South Yemeni attack and appealed to the United States for arms; the PDRY said it was repulsing a North Yemeni attack.
- 1979-02-28: The United States announced a speedup of Saudi-financed U.S. arms deliveries to North Yemen; Saudi Arabia placed its armed forces on alert.
- 1979-03-02: League of Arab States mediation produced a cease-fire agreement scheduled to begin on 3 March, although fighting continued.
- 1979-03-16: North and South Yemeni chiefs of staff reached a second cease-fire agreement in San'a.
- 1979-03-30: Under Kuwaiti mediation, the two presidents announced a provisional agreement to unite their countries, ending the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. arms assistance to North Yemen, including the speed and provision of deliveries financed by Saudi Arabia. As mediation and cease-fire efforts progressed, the immediate issue shifted from responding to active fighting toward supporting or managing the arms-related response without deploying U.S. combat forces.

Crisis 303 — Afghanistan Invasion (1979)

ACTION: The Soviet invasion of Afghanistan on 24 December 1979 triggered a U.S. crisis response. The United States provided covert military support to the Mujahuddin and imposed aid, trade, diplomatic, and other sanctions on the Soviet Union; it did not deploy combat forces.

TIMELINE:
- 1979-03: The Herat Uprising and the Afghan government’s appeal for urgent assistance intensified the crisis.
- 1979-12: Soviet troops began landing at Kabul airport on 24 December; Hafizullah Amin was killed by 27 December and Babrak Karmal installed as leader.
- 1980-01: The United States announced measures including blocking grain exports, restricting technology and fishing access, delaying diplomatic and cultural arrangements, and boycotting the Moscow Olympics; it also expanded support for Pakistan and China and continued covert aid to Afghan guerrillas.
- 1980-02: The United States adopted a two-track policy of pursuing normal relations with the Soviet Union while continuing arms aid through Pakistan to anti-Soviet Afghan guerrillas.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. military assistance to Afghan anti-Soviet forces and measures restricting economic, technological, diplomatic, and other relations with the Soviet Union. By February 1980, the policy shifted toward maintaining arms aid while also pursuing a return to normal U.S.-Soviet relations.

Crisis 306 — Soviet Threat/Pak. (1979)

ACTION: Soviet statements accused Pakistan of allowing Afghan rebels to operate from its territory and indicated that the USSR would intervene if war broke out between Afghanistan and Pakistan. The United States provided political support to Pakistan and denied allegations that it was training Afghan rebels; no U.S. combat force was used or actively weighed, consistent with the escalation level of aid or sanctions.

TIMELINE:
- 1979-06: Pravda accused Pakistan of supporting attacks by Afghan rebels and indicated that the USSR would not remain indifferent; Pakistan denied the allegations and called for renewed normal relations with Afghanistan.
- 1979-06: Tass alleged that the United States was training Afghan rebel forces in Pakistan; the State Department denied the allegation and extended political support to Pakistan.
- 1979-07: Afghan Foreign Minister Shah Mohammad Dost arrived in Pakistan for talks with President Zia-ul-Haq.
- 1979-07: Afghanistan and Pakistan agreed to continue talks, prepare for a possible summit, and facilitate the voluntary return of refugees to Afghanistan.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of whether and how to provide U.S. political or other noncombat support to Pakistan amid Soviet warnings and Afghan-Pakistani tensions. The crisis resolution centered on renewed Afghanistan-Pakistan talks rather than a U.S. combat deployment.

Crisis 309 — US Hostages In Iran (1979)

ACTION: From 1979 to 1981, Iranian students held U.S. embassy personnel hostage, while the United States pursued diplomatic, economic, legal, and military options. The United States reached the combat level by launching an airborne rescue mission in April 1980; the mission was aborted after a helicopter collision killed eight U.S. soldiers.

TIMELINE:
- 1979-11: Iranian students stormed and occupied the U.S. embassy; the United States rejected their demand to return the shah.
- 1979-11: The United States discontinued oil purchases from Iran, froze Iranian assets, initiated action in the International Court of Justice, and conducted naval maneuvers in the Arabian Sea.
- 1980-03: President Carter authorized a plan to rescue the hostages.
- 1980-04: The United States broke diplomatic relations with Iran and decided to send an airborne rescue mission.
- 1980-04: U.S. helicopters entered Iran; a collision killed eight U.S. soldiers, and President Carter aborted the mission.
- 1980-11: The Iranian Parliament adopted conditions for releasing the hostages, with Algeria designated as mediator.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the United States’ response to the hostage seizure, including measures involving economic pressure and the possibility of military action. Over the period, the response moved from diplomatic, legal, and economic measures to an authorized and executed rescue operation.

Crisis 311 — Raid On Gafsa (1980)

ACTION: Tunisia faced a raid by Tunisian insurgents trained in Libya, who attacked Gafsa on 26–27 January 1980; Tunisian forces retook the town on 27 January. The United States provided aid rather than using combat forces: it expedited military-equipment deliveries and dispatched two Air Force transport planes carrying armored vehicles for the Tunisian army on 7 February.

TIMELINE:
- 1980-01: Insurgents crossed from Algeria, attacked Gafsa, and were reported to have received training in Libya; Tunisia sought foreign assistance.
- 1980-01: France sent military transport planes and advisors to Tunisia; the United States announced that military-equipment deliveries to Tunisia would be expedited.
- 1980-02: The United States dispatched two Air Force transport planes carrying armored vehicles for the Tunisian army.
- 1980-02: Libya’s General People’s Congress called for France to withdraw from Tunisia and for support to Tunisia’s armed opposition; French military aircraft were withdrawn from Tunisia on 12 February.
- 1980-02: Qaddhafi declared the confrontation with Tunisia over on 27 February, and the next day said the possibility of a serious confrontation with France had also ended.
- 1980-04: After continued tensions, including Tunisia’s execution of 15 participants convicted in the raid on 12 April, the crisis ended by late April.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. military assistance to Tunisia, including expedited equipment deliveries and the provision of armored vehicles. The U.S. response remained at the aid level; no U.S. combat force was used or actively weighed in the summary.

Crisis 317 — Onset Iran/Iraq War (1980)

ACTION: Iraq abrogated its 1975 treaty with Iran on 17 September 1980 and launched a major offensive four days later, beginning the Iran-Iraq War. The United States maintained strict neutrality and reached a show-of-force posture without combat; by mid-October, U.S. warships were among an international force patrolling the Strait of Hormuz over concerns about possible disruption of oil supplies to U.S. allies.

TIMELINE:
- 1980-03: Iraq began commando raids into Iranian territory.
- 1980-09: Iraq formally abrogated the 1975 treaty on 17 September and proclaimed sovereignty over the Shatt-al-Arab; Iraqi forces launched a major offensive on 21 September.
- 1980-09: President Carter proclaimed strict U.S. neutrality on 22 September; Iranian forces counterattacked Iraqi air bases on 23 September.
- 1980-09: The UN Security Council unanimously called for a cease-fire, scheduled to take effect on 25 September.
- 1980-10: By mid-month, approximately 60 U.S., U.K., French, and Australian warships were patrolling the Strait of Hormuz.
- 1980-11: Iranian forces reportedly drove Iraqi forces from Susangerd on 17 November; by 30 November Iraqi forces had dug in and a stalemate had followed.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the broader issue of whether and how the United States should address the war’s potential effects on oil supplies to U.S. allies while maintaining the proclaimed policy of strict neutrality. Over the period, the U.S. posture included a noncombat show of force through participation in Strait of Hormuz patrols, rather than military action against either belligerent.

Crisis 319 — Jordan/Syria Confrnt. (1980)

ACTION: Syria moved armored units toward the Jordanian border, producing a two-week military confrontation involving approximately 30,000 Syrian and 24,000 Jordanian troops and about 1,000 tanks. The United States took no action and did not actively weigh a military response; its escalation level was No Response.

TIMELINE:
- 1980-11: Damascus began moving armored units toward positions on the Jordanian border.
- 1980-12: The confrontation became increasingly serious, with Syrian and Jordanian forces facing each other.
- 1980-12: Saudi Arabia’s Second Deputy Premier Prince Abdullah mediated and facilitated de-escalation.
- 1980-12: Syrian forces began withdrawing on December 5, and Jordanian forces began withdrawing on December 10.
- 1980-12: Most forces had returned to pre-crisis positions by December 11; the crisis ended on December 14.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced no reported decision regarding a U.S. response because the United States was not involved in the crisis and took no action. No shift in congressional decisions is identified over the period.

Crisis 326 — Contras I (1981)

ACTION: Nicaragua attacked a Honduran border post at Guasale on 28 April 1981 in an effort to locate contras, prompting alerts and a border closure by both states. The United States had cut off aid to Nicaragua in early April; during the crisis, it did not deploy combat forces or directly intervene.

TIMELINE:
- 1981-04: The Reagan administration cut off U.S. aid to Nicaragua.
- 1981-04: Nicaragua attacked a Honduran border post at Guasale; troops of both states were placed on alert and the border was closed.
- 1981-05: Clashes occurred at el Tablazo on 3 May and again on 5 May.
- 1981-05: Nicaragua’s defense ministry denounced Honduras for alleged acts of aggression; Honduran and Nicaraguan officials also began negotiations.
- 1981-05: After six days of negotiations, the two sides issued a joint declaration emphasizing tension reduction, ending the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of whether to maintain or alter the U.S. aid cutoff to Nicaragua amid the Honduras–Nicaragua border crisis. The summary records no U.S. combat deployment or direct intervention and does not identify a further congressional decision during the crisis.

Crisis 330 — Gulf of Syrte I (1981)

ACTION: The United States conducted Sixth Fleet maneuvers in the Mediterranean, including naval exercises in the disputed Gulf of Syrte, to challenge Libya’s claim that the area was internal territorial water. On 19 August, two U.S. F-14s shot down two Libyan Su-22 fighters; the United States also warned that it would use military force if Libya attacked U.S. aircraft or ships involved in the exercise.

TIMELINE:
- 1981-08: The United States announced Sixth Fleet maneuvers in the Mediterranean; Libya responded by declaring a full military alert.
- 1981-08: Libya accused the United States of violating its territorial waters by conducting exercises in the Gulf of Syrte.
- 1981-08: Two U.S. F-14s shot down two Libyan Su-22 fighters within the Gulf of Syrte; the United States warned against retaliation.
- 1981-08: Qaddhafi said Libya was prepared to defend its claimed territorial waters even at the risk of war; President Reagan said he had deliberately ordered the maneuvers to challenge Libya’s claim.
- 1981-09: The crisis ended after Qaddhafi threatened to attack U.S. nuclear bases in the Mediterranean if the United States again entered the Gulf of Syrte.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of whether to authorize, constrain, or otherwise respond to U.S. military maneuvers and the use of force in the Gulf of Syrte. Over the period, the immediate decision shifted from the conduct of exercises in disputed waters to the consequences of the shootdown and the possibility of further military action if Libya retaliated.

Crisis 335 — Khorramshahr (1982)

ACTION: Iran’s March–May 1982 offensives drove Iraqi forces from much of their remaining territory in Iran and recaptured Khorramshahr. The United States responded at the Aid or Sanctions level, with no U.S. combat force; U.S. concern focused on a possible Iranian invasion of Iraq.

TIMELINE:
- 1982-03: Iran launched an offensive west of Dezful, forcing Iraq to withdraw from 350 square kilometers of Iranian territory over six days.
- 1982-04: Iran launched Operation Jerusalem against Iraqi positions in southern Khuzestan and Khorramshahr.
- 1982-05: Iranian forces recaptured Khorramshahr on 23–24 May; on 28 May, the UN Secretary-General renewed calls for an end to the war and offered mediation.
- 1982-06: Iraq initiated a unilateral cease-fire on 9 June and began withdrawing from 5,500 square kilometers of Iranian-occupied territory on 10 June; the GCC attempted mediation.
- 1982-07: Iran launched Operation Ramadan on 13 July to cut roads linking Basra to northern Iraq; the offensive lost momentum by 30 July.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the U.S. response at the Aid or Sanctions level, rather than the deployment of U.S. combat forces. Over the period, the immediate issue shifted from Iran’s advances and the recapture of Khorramshahr to the possibility of an Iranian offensive into Iraq and the continuation of the conflict.

Crisis 338 — Ogaden III (1982)

ACTION: Ethiopia attacked Somali army units in the Hiran region on 30 June 1982, later advancing 20 miles into Somali territory. The United States responded to Somalia’s request by providing arms and economic aid under a 1980 agreement; no U.S. combat forces were used or actively weighed.

TIMELINE:
- 1982-06: Ethiopian troops attacked Somali army units in the Hiran region, threatening communications between Somalia’s two halves.
- 1982-07: Somalia requested U.S. assistance; the United States began supplying arms and economic aid under the 1980 agreement.
- 1982-07: The OAU called on Ethiopia to desist from destabilizing East Africa.
- 1982-07-26: Ethiopia declared that U.S. aid to Somalia threatened its territorial integrity and denied invading Somalia.
- 1982-08: The crisis began to fade as neither side made further advances.
- 1982-09: Somalia declared a state of emergency in the war zone after Ethiopia’s incursion 20 miles into Somali territory.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. arms and economic assistance to Somalia under the 1980 agreement. Over the period, the concrete U.S. measure was the initiation and continuation of aid; no decision concerning deployment of U.S. combat forces is identified.

Crisis 340 — Libya Threat/Sudan (1983)

ACTION: Libya’s military buildup near Sudan’s northern border led Sudan and Egypt to perceive a possible Libyan effort to overthrow President Numeiri’s regime. The United States reached the “Show of Force” level without combat by dispatching four AWACS reconnaissance aircraft to Egypt and the aircraft carrier *Nimitz* to the Red Sea off Sudan and Egypt; no U.S. combat action occurred.

TIMELINE:
- 1983-02: Libya’s increased military presence near Sudan’s northern border, including MIG-23s and TU-22 bombers, intensified the crisis.
- 1983-02-11: Sudan and Egypt perceived a possible Libyan plan to overthrow the Numeiri regime.
- 1983-02-12: Sudan complained to the UN and placed its forces on alert; Egypt moved fighter aircraft toward its Sudan border and transport aircraft to Aswan, while President Mubarak warned the United States of an impending Libyan attack.
- 1983-02-17: The United States dispatched four AWACS aircraft to Egypt and the aircraft carrier *Nimitz* to the Red Sea off Sudan and Egypt.
- 1983-02-18: Libya concentrated additional air power near the Egyptian border.
- 1983-02-22: Numeiri and Mubarak met in Khartoum to consider implementing their 1982 integration agreement, reducing Sudan’s stress and ending the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify a specific congressional decision during the crisis. The U.S. action described was an executive military deployment at the show-of-force level, with no combat reported.

Crisis 342 — Chad/Libya VI (1983)

ACTION: Libya-supported Goukouni forces occupied Faya-Largeau, prompting fighting involving Chad, Libya, and France. The United States reached no response level; although Chad’s forces received weapons from the United States along with aid from France and Zaire, the summary does not identify a U.S. decision to deploy forces or actively weigh direct military action.

TIMELINE:
- 1983-06: Libya-supported Goukouni forces occupied Faya-Largeau, initiating the crisis.
- 1983-07: Chad’s forces, assisted by weapons from France, Zaire, and the United States, began a counteroffensive; Faya-Largeau was recaptured on 30 July.
- 1983-07: Libyan MIG fighter-bombers attacked Faya-Largeau on 31 July, marking direct Libyan intervention.
- 1983-08: France activated Operation Manta and dispatched several hundred troops to Chad.
- 1984-04: Qaddhafi proposed mutual withdrawal, which France accepted.
- 1984-09: France and Libya announced that mutual withdrawal would begin on 25 September and be completed by 10 November; tension eased and the crisis ended on 11 December.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced no identified decision on deploying U.S. forces or authorizing direct U.S. military action. The U.S. role described in the summary was the provision of weapons to Chad’s forces, while direct military involvement remained at the no-response level.

Crisis 343 — Invasion Of Grenada (1983)

ACTION: 19–28 October 1983, the United States responded to political upheaval and executions in Grenada, amid concerns about U.S. citizens on the island and regional security. At the combat level reached, the United States actively planned and carried out an invasion: 1,900 U.S. troops landed on 25 October, followed by 300 Caribbean troops; U.S. deaths were in the 6–25 range.

TIMELINE:
- 1983-10-19: Maurice Bishop and seven cabinet ministers and trade union leaders were executed after fighting; a 96-hour curfew was imposed.
- 1983-10-20: General Hudson Austin formed a Revolutionary Military Council.
- 1983-10-21: The OECS imposed diplomatic and economic sanctions, sought U.S. assistance, and a U.S. naval task force was diverted to the Caribbean for possible evacuation of U.S. citizens.
- 1983-10-22: CARICOM heads of government began an emergency meeting that produced broader diplomatic and economic sanctions against Grenada.
- 1983-10-23: President Reagan decided to respond to the OECS request; final military plans for an invasion were completed.
- 1983-10-25: Approximately 1,900 U.S. troops landed in Grenada, followed by 300 troops from six Caribbean states.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the U.S. military response to the Grenadian crisis, including the possible evacuation of U.S. citizens and the use of military force. Over the period, the contemplated response shifted from naval positioning for a possible evacuation to final planning and execution of an invasion.

Crisis 348 — Basra-Kharg Island (1984)

ACTION: Iran-Iraq fighting expanded from land offensives to attacks on oil installations and Gulf shipping, drawing Kuwait and Saudi Arabia into the crisis. The United States did not use combat forces; at the aid-or-sanctions level, it provided AWACS reconnaissance support to Saudi Arabia, secretly supplied Iraq with technological and intelligence assistance, and provided antiaircraft missiles and a naval escort for Saudi ships, while Kuwait requested Stinger missiles and a U.S. naval escort for its tankers.

TIMELINE:
- 1984-02: Iran launched four attacks on Iraqi forces, including Wal Fajr VI near Dehloran; Iran captured the Majnoon Island oil-drilling complex.
- 1984-03: Iraq attacked Iranian oil installations on Kharg Island on 1 March; Iran’s Operation Badr reached the Basra-Baghdad road, and Iraq recaptured its lost positions by 18 March.
- 1984-05: Iranian attacks damaged two Kuwaiti tankers on 13–14 May; Saudi Arabia became directly involved after an Iranian aircraft set a Saudi ship ablaze on 16 May.
- 1984-05: Kuwait requested U.S. Stinger antiaircraft missiles and a U.S. naval escort for its tankers on 30 May.
- 1984-06: Saudi F-15s, assisted by U.S. AWACS reconnaissance planes, shot down an Iranian F-4 on 5 June; Iran and Iraq agreed to a UN-brokered moratorium on shelling civilian areas on 10 June.
- 1984-07: Kuwait signed an arms deal with the USSR on 11 July, marking the end of the overall crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. provision of military assistance and support related to Gulf shipping and regional air defense, including antiaircraft missiles and naval escort arrangements. Over the period, the concrete U.S. measures included assistance and reconnaissance support rather than deployment of U.S. combat forces.

Crisis 350 — Omdurman Bombing (1984)

ACTION: On 16 March 1984, a Tupolev TU-22 bomber attacked Omdurman, prompting Sudan and Egypt to accuse Libya of responsibility, which Libya denied. At Sudanese and Egyptian request, the United States dispatched two AWACS surveillance aircraft on 18 March—a show of force involving surveillance support, with no U.S. combat action.

TIMELINE:
- 1984-03: A TU-22 bomber attacks Omdurman on 16 March, triggering the crisis.
- 1984-03: Sudan places its armed forces on alert on 17 March; Egypt sends senior army officers to Khartoum and both accuse Libya of responsibility.
- 1984-03: The United States dispatches two AWACS surveillance aircraft on 18 March at Sudanese and Egyptian request.
- 1984-03: Egyptian President Mubarak visits Khartoum on 25 March; thousands of Egyptian soldiers are deployed in Sudan.
- 1984-03: The UN Security Council meets on 27–28 March after Libya requests a meeting.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of how to respond to the crisis and to requests from Sudan and Egypt for U.S. support, including the dispatch of surveillance aircraft. Over the period, the U.S. action reached a show-of-force level through AWACS deployment, without U.S. combat.

Crisis 351 — Vietnam Incursion Into Thailand (1984)

ACTION: On 25 March 1984, Vietnamese troops entered Thai territory while pursuing Khmer Rouge Cambodian rebels, prompting Thai military and diplomatic responses after Thai military and civilian casualties. The United States reached the aid level without using combat forces: it offered military aid and reaffirmed the sale of 40 M-48 tanks, with further military sales available as needed.

TIMELINE:
- 1984-03: Vietnamese troops entered Thailand in pursuit of Khmer Rouge rebels; Thai military and civilian casualties followed.
- 1984-03: Thailand conducted military efforts to push Vietnamese forces back into Kampuchea and pursued diplomatic support in Washington and at the UN.
- 1984-04: Thailand’s military response included minor clashes near the Khmer Rouge camp at Chong Phra Palai Pass.
- 1984-04-15: Washington announced an offer of military aid to Thailand.
- 1984-04-17: After meeting Thai Premier General Prem Tinsulanonda, President Reagan reaffirmed the sale of 40 M-48 tanks and assurances of further military sales as needed; the crisis ended.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary identifies U.S. military aid and arms sales as the decisions at issue, specifically the offer of military aid and the sale of 40 M-48 tanks. It does not identify a separate congressional decision or describe a shift in congressional action over the period.

Crisis 354 — Nicaragua Mig-21s(1984)

ACTION: Reports indicated that a Soviet ship was carrying MIG-21 fighter planes to Nicaragua, prompting U.S. military exercises and a more visible military presence in and near Central America, including Honduras. Consistent with a show of force and no combat, the United States pursued a Soviet-flagged merchant vessel in Nicaraguan territorial waters and was reported to have violated Nicaraguan airspace; no invasion or other combat occurred, although Managua perceived an invasion as possible.

TIMELINE:
- 1984-11: Reports emerged that the Soviet cargo ship Bakuriani was en route to Nicaragua carrying MIG-21s.
- 1984-11-07: The United States increased the number and visibility of Pentagon military exercises in Honduras and expanded its military signaling on land and at sea in and near Central America.
- 1984-11-09: Two U.S. frigates pursued a Soviet-flagged merchant vessel in Nicaraguan territorial waters near Corinto; Nicaragua requested an emergency UN Security Council meeting.
- 1984-11-09: Moscow announced that the Bakuriani was not carrying MIG-21s, and U.S. officials quickly confirmed the statement; the crisis began to wind down.
- 1984-11-12: The State Department stated that no preparations were being made to invade Nicaragua; Nicaragua nevertheless placed its people on maximum alert.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify a specific congressional decision or legislative measure during the crisis. The U.S. executive-branch response shifted from visible military exercises and maritime signaling to confirming that the ship was not carrying MIG-21s and stating that no invasion preparations were under way.

Crisis 358 — Egypt Air Hijacking (1985)

ACTION: Egypt’s crisis followed the 23 November hijacking of an Egypt Air flight by members of an anti-Mubarak group, amid accusations of Libyan support and an Egyptian military buildup near Libya. The United States supported the Egyptian rescue mission and provided three officers who joined the Egyptian commando force; its escalation level reached Military Advisers, not direct U.S. combat action.

TIMELINE:
- 1985-11: Five hijackers seized an Egypt Air flight traveling from Athens to Cairo and diverted it to Malta; Egypt placed its armed forces on alert and began a military buildup near Libya.
- 1985-11: The hijackers killed one passenger and threatened others after separating passengers by nationality.
- 1985-11: Egypt secretly dispatched 75 commando troops to Malta, joined by three U.S. officers.
- 1985-11: Egyptian commandos stormed the aircraft; fighting and fire killed about 60 people.
- 1985-11: President Mubarak stated that the hijacking-group leader was living in a Tripoli hotel, and Egypt sought extradition of the surviving hijacker.
- 1985-12: Mubarak declared that Egypt could not fight an Arab or African “brother,” ending the crisis with Libya.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of whether to authorize or otherwise address a direct U.S. military role in the rescue operation and the broader Egypt-Libya tensions. Over the period, the U.S. role remained limited to supporting the mission and providing three military officers, rather than expanding to direct U.S. combat involvement.

Crisis 361 — Capture Of Al-Faw (1986)

ACTION: Iran launched offensives in southern Iraq on 9 February 1986 and captured al-Faw by mid-February, threatening Iraq’s access to the Gulf and potentially its Umm Qasr naval base. The United States reached a “No Response” escalation level; the summary identifies no U.S. military action taken or actively weighed.

TIMELINE:
- 1986-02: Iranian forces began offensives toward Qurna and Majnoon; those attacks failed.
- 1986-02: Iran captured al-Faw after advancing through Umm al-Rassas and toward the al-Faw Peninsula.
- 1986-02: Kuwait and Saudi Arabia joined an Arab request for a UN Security Council meeting and condemnation of Iran’s “aggression.”
- 1986-02: Iran assured Kuwait that it would respect Kuwaiti neutrality if Kuwait did not permit Iraq to use its territory for military operations.
- 1986-02: Iraq launched a counteroffensive to regain al-Faw and expanded its naval exclusion zone to include Kuwait’s coast.
- 1986-04: The crisis faded with Iran retaining al-Faw while Iraq prevented further Iranian advances in the south.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify any concrete decision Congress faced regarding the capture of al-Faw. With the U.S. escalation level at “No Response,” no shift in a congressional decision is specified.

Crisis 362 — Chad/Libya VII (1986)

ACTION: On 10 February 1986, Libya-backed GUNT forces renewed attacks in Chad and captured Koro Toro, while Libyan forces reportedly attacked Chadian troops. The United States provided Chad with $10 million in emergency military aid; it did not deploy combat forces, and the summary does not identify any U.S. combat action being actively weighed.

TIMELINE:
- 1986-02: GUNT forces captured Koro Toro near the 16th Parallel; Libyan forces reportedly attacked Chadian troops.
- 1986-02: Chad appealed for direct French military intervention; France dispatched commandos and fighter aircraft to N'Djamena.
- 1986-02: French aircraft bombed a rebel airstrip at Qadi-Doum; Libya subsequently bombed N'Djamena airport.
- 1986-03: The U.S. State Department announced $10 million in emergency military aid for Chad.
- 1986-03: Fighting continued, with the crisis fading at the end of the month while the conflict remained unresolved.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary identifies no specific congressional decision or legislative action. The U.S. measure described was emergency military aid to Chad, within an escalation level of aid or sanctions and without U.S. combat-force involvement.

Crisis 363 — Gulf Of Syrte II (1986)

ACTION: The United States entered the Gulf of Syrte with three naval vessels on 24 March, prompting Libyan missile fire and U.S. attacks on Libyan naval and radar targets; the U.S. sustained combat involvement with 1–5 deaths. After the 5 April West Berlin bombing, President Reagan approved air strikes, and on 15 April the United States conducted air raids on Tripoli and Benghazi.

TIMELINE:
- 1986-01: Washington announced naval exercises in the Gulf of Syrte, held from 23–30 January.
- 1986-02: A second U.S. naval exercise was held from 11–14 February; U.S. aircraft intercepted Libyan planes without violence.
- 1986-03: On 20 March, the Pentagon announced that U.S. Sixth Fleet ships would enter the Gulf south of latitude 32°30'; three vessels entered on 24 March, followed by Libyan SAM-5 fire and U.S. attacks on Libyan ships and radar installations.
- 1986-03: U.S. naval maneuvers ended on 27 March; on 28 March Secretary of State Shultz warned that further U.S. action against Libya remained possible.
- 1986-04: A 5 April bombing at a West Berlin discotheque killed one U.S. soldier and a Turkish woman and injured 155; on 9 April Reagan warned of retaliation and approved a proposal for air strikes.
- 1986-04: On 15 April the United States bombed Tripoli and Benghazi; a UN Security Council resolution debated on 21 April condemned the action and the United States and United Kingdom vetoed it.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the U.S. naval presence and exercises in the Gulf of Syrte and the possibility of further military action against Libya. After the West Berlin bombing, the active U.S. option shifted from naval operations and limited attacks in the Gulf to proposed and then conducted air strikes against Libyan targets

Crisis 369 — Contras III(1986)

ACTION: Sandinista forces crossed into Honduras on 4 December 1986 while pursuing contra rebels, overrunning a Honduran border post and triggering a Honduran military response. The United States granted Honduras’s request for military assistance and, on 7–8 December, used helicopters to transport more than 1,000 Honduran soldiers to the border area; its involvement remained a show of force with no U.S. combat.

TIMELINE:
- 1986-10: U.S.-Honduras talks led to a joint military commission to coordinate contra training.
- 1986-11: The United States made its first large shipment of arms; fighting near the Honduran border escalated.
- 1986-12-04: Sandinista forces overran the Honduran border post at Las Mieles, wounding three Honduran soldiers and capturing two.
- 1986-12-06: Honduran President Azcona del Hoyo requested U.S. military assistance, which President Reagan immediately granted; Honduran aircraft bombed Sandinista troops.
- 1986-12-07/08: U.S. helicopters transported more than 1,000 Honduran soldiers to the border area, about 40 kilometers from the fighting.
- 1986-12-12: Honduras confirmed that it had requested U.S. removal of contra rebels from its territory, marking the crisis’s termination.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify a specific congressional decision or congressional action during the crisis. The documented U.S. decisions were executive: granting Honduras’s request for military assistance and deploying helicopters for troop transport, without U.S. combat.

Crisis 370 — Chad/Libya VIII (1986)

ACTION: Libya attacked FAP forces in northern Chad, while Chadian government forces later fought alongside former GUNT forces and expanded operations north of the 16th Parallel. The United States provided emergency military aid to the Chadian government and welcomed Chad’s September 1987 raid into Libya; it did not use combat forces or directly intervene militarily.

TIMELINE:
- 1986-12: Libya attacked FAP forces in northern Chad; Habré appealed for U.S. intervention, and the State Department announced emergency military aid for Chad.
- 1987-01: Chadian forces recaptured Fada and Zouar; Libya bombed Arada, while France established air defenses and continued military aid to Chad.
- 1987-08: Chad captured Aozou; Libya retook it later in the month and continued bombing Chad.
- 1987-09: Chadian forces invaded Libya and occupied the Maaten-es-Sara air base; the United States welcomed the raid.
- 1987-09: Both sides accepted an OAU cease-fire call, ending the crisis on 11 September.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of providing or maintaining military aid and other assistance to the Chadian government as fighting with Libya continued. The U.S. response remained at the aid-or-sanctions level, with emergency military aid provided rather than deployment of U.S. combat forces.

Crisis 379 — Mecca Pilgrimage (1987)

ACTION: From 31 July through October 1987, Saudi Arabia and Iran were in crisis after violent clashes between Saudi security forces and Iranian pilgrims during the Mecca pilgrimage, killing several hundred and wounding many. The United States took no action and was not involved in the crisis; no U.S. military response was reached or actively weighed in the summary.

TIMELINE:
- 1987-07: Iran and Saudi Arabia reached an agreement that the Iranian demonstration would end half a kilometer from the Great Mosque, but the Iranian pilgrimage representative refused to cancel it.
- 1987-07-28: Ayatollah Khomeini called on Iranian pilgrims to participate in a “disavowal march” while urging them to avoid clashes, insults, and disputes.
- 1987-07-31: Clashes occurred between Iranian pilgrims and Saudi security forces during the demonstration; several hundred people were killed and many wounded, triggering the crisis.
- 1987-08-01: Iran called for the overthrow of the Saudi regime; 2 August was declared an official “Day of Hate” in Iran.
- 1987-08-25: Saudi official Prince Naif publicly called for the overthrow of Iran’s regime.
- 1987-10: The crisis faded, with the summary describing a Saudi victory and Iranian defeat.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary identifies no U.S. congressional decision concerning the crisis. Because the United States was not involved and reached a “No Response” escalation level, no U.S. military action or related option shifted before Congress over the period.

Crisis 380 — S. Africa Intervention In Angola (1987)

ACTION: South African forces attacked SWAPO bases in Angolan territory beginning in October 1987, and Angola launched a counterattack against South African units in December. The United States reached a “No Response” escalation level: it did not take military action and was not actively weighing military intervention; its role described here was diplomatic mediation and support for a policy that prevented UN sanctions against South Africa.

TIMELINE:
- 1987-10: South African Defence Forces attacked SWAPO bases in Angola; Angola appealed to the UN Security Council.
- 1987-10: The Security Council unanimously condemned South Africa’s actions and called for withdrawal, without imposing economic sanctions.
- 1987-12: Angola launched a counterattack against South African units.
- 1988-05: U.S. Assistant Secretary of State Chester Crocker chaired the first round of mediation among Angola, Cuba, and South Africa in London.
- 1988-07: Mediated negotiations in New York produced the 14-point “Principles for a Peaceful Settlement in Southwestern Africa.”
- 1988-08: Negotiations in Geneva produced the Geneva Protocol, followed by the 22 August Ruacana cease-fire agreement between Angola and South Africa.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress had faced the decision whether to maintain or repeal legal restrictions on U.S. assistance to UNITA; in July 1985, it repealed the Clark Amendment. During this crisis, the summary identifies congressional action through that prior repeal, while U.S. policy continued to prevent UN sanctions against South Africa.

Crisis 383 — Contras III (1988)

ACTION: Nicaragua launched an offensive against contra camps in Honduras on 6 March 1988, prompting Honduras to request U.S. assistance. The United States reached the “Aid or Sanctions” level without using combat forces: it ordered 3,200 troops to Honduras, while U.S.-supplied Honduran aircraft bombed Nicaraguan troops; U.S. troops began withdrawing on 28 March.

TIMELINE:
- 1988-03: Nicaraguan forces launched a major offensive against contra camps in Honduras.
- 1988-03: Honduras appealed to the United States for assistance.
- 1988-03: The United States ordered 3,200 troops to Honduras in response to Honduras’s appeal.
- 1988-03: U.S.-supplied Honduran fighter planes bombed Nicaraguan troops operating in the border zone.
- 1988-03: Nicaragua ended its military incursion and renewed its case against Honduras before the World Court.
- 1988-03: U.S. troops began withdrawing from Honduras, signaling crisis termination.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress had previously decided whether to provide assistance to the contras, approving $48 million in nonmilitary aid in March 1987. During this crisis, the summary identifies executive decisions involving assistance to Honduras and the dispatch of U.S. troops, but does not specify a separate congressional decision.

Crisis 385 — Iraq Recapture-Al-Faw (1988)

ACTION: Iraq recaptured al-Faw from Iran on 18 April 1988 amid renewed fighting in the Iran-Iraq War. The United States took no military action and did not actively weigh an escalation, corresponding to the recorded U.S. escalation level of No Response; the summary notes only that the United States and other major powers pressed for negotiations.

TIMELINE:
- 1988-04: Iraq recaptured the port of al-Faw after a fierce battle.
- 1988-05: Iraq captured Shalamchech; an Iranian counteroffensive failed.
- 1988-06: Iran declared there would be no compromise; Iraq launched further offensives and recaptured Mehran, Majnoon Island, and areas north of Basra.
- 1988-07: Iraq advanced into Iran and proposed peace talks; the United States, Soviet Union, and United Nations pressed for negotiations.
- 1988-07: Iran accepted a cease-fire under growing external pressure; Iraq continued to insist on direct negotiations.
- 1988-08: Fighting and the war ended on 8 August.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced no stated military decision concerning U.S. action in this crisis. The summary records no shift toward U.S. military involvement; the U.S. role described was diplomatic pressure for negotiations.

Crisis 386 — Libyan Jets (1988)

ACTION: After the 21 December 1988 Lockerbie crash and reports of Libyan chemical-weapons production, the United States imposed sanctions and conducted military maneuvers in the central Mediterranean near Libya. At combat escalation, U.S. fighters shot down two Libyan MIG-23s over international waters on 4 January 1989; no U.S. deaths occurred.

TIMELINE:
- 1988-12: A Pan Am plane crashed over Lockerbie, Scotland, killing 257 passengers; Libya became the prime suspect.
- 1988-12: U.S. intelligence reports indicated Libyan chemical-weapons production near Rabta; the United States imposed sanctions on 28 December.
- 1989-01: A U.S. task force led by the USS John F. Kennedy conducted maneuvers in the central Mediterranean near Libya.
- 1989-01: U.S. fighters shot down two Libyan MIG-23s over international waters on 4 January.
- 1989-01: The Soviet Union criticized the U.S. action on 5 January; a draft UN Security Council resolution on 11 January called for suspending U.S. maneuvers but was vetoed by France, the United Kingdom, and the United States.
- 1989-01: An emergency Arab League and Islamic Conference meeting condemned U.S. aggression on 12 January, marking a visible decline in tensions.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of U.S. sanctions in response to the Lockerbie crash and reported Libyan chemical-weapons production, as well as the broader question of military activity near Libya. Over the period, U.S. action progressed from sanctions and maneuvers to the actual use of force in the shootdown of two Libyan aircraft.

Crisis 391 — Invasion Of Panama (1989)

ACTION: Panama’s declaration of a state of war with the United States followed the killing of a U.S. marine by Panamanian soldiers on 16 December 1989. President Bush decided to intervene, and the United States conducted a combat invasion with 26,000 troops beginning 20 December; the campaign continued until Noriega surrendered on 3 January.

TIMELINE:
- 1989-04: President Bush called for free elections in Panama.
- 1989-05: Panama held elections on 7 May; the results were nullified on 10 May, when Bush announced further economic measures.
- 1989-12: On 15 December, Noriega was proclaimed head of government; on 16 December, a U.S. marine was killed and Panamanian officials declared a state of war with the United States.
- 1989-12: President Bush decided to intervene on 17 December; U.S. forces began Operation Just Cause with an invasion force of 26,000 troops on 20 December.
- 1989-12: On 21 December, Panama’s church leaders proposed proclaiming the winner of the May election, Endara, president; the UN General Assembly adopted a resolution deploring the intervention on 29 December.
- 1990-01: Noriega surrendered on 3 January after a 12-day U.S. military campaign, ending the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress had previously faced decisions concerning U.S.-Panama treaty ratification, including treaties governing the transfer and neutrality of the Panama Canal, and later faced the broader issue of U.S. economic and covert measures against Noriega. During the crisis period described, the summary identifies the intervention decision as made by President Bush rather than specifying a congressional authorization decision.

Crisis 393 — Gulf War (1990)

ACTION: Iraq invaded and occupied Kuwait on 1990-08-02, prompting U.S. condemnation and a military deployment to Saudi Arabia. The United States escalated to combat: it built up forces, weighed and approved plans for air, naval, and ground operations, and ultimately incurred 101–250 U.S. deaths.

TIMELINE:
- 1990-08: Iraq invaded and occupied Kuwait; the United States called for immediate Iraqi withdrawal and restoration of Kuwait’s government.
- 1990-08: The United States announced deployments to Saudi Arabia, including a possible force of up to 50,000 troops.
- 1990-10: President Bush secretly approved plans for an air and naval strike in January 1991 and a ground campaign in February.
- 1990-11: The United States announced the deployment of 150,000 additional troops to the Gulf region.
- 1990-11: At U.S. urging, the UN Security Council adopted Resolution 678 authorizing “all necessary means” after a 1991-01-15 deadline for Iraq to withdraw from Kuwait; Iraq rejected it.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the United States’ military deployment and the possible use of force to secure Iraq’s withdrawal from Kuwait. As the crisis progressed, the issue shifted from responding to the invasion and deploying forces to considering combat operations under the UN-authorized deadline.

Crisis 399 — Foreign Interv.-Zaire (1991)

ACTION: Rioting by Zaire’s regular troops began on 23 September 1991, including the occupation of Kinshasa Airport, prompting Belgium and France to send forces to protect and evacuate foreign residents. The United States reached a show-of-force level without combat: it provided transport planes for the Belgian-French evacuation project and, under pressure on Mobutu, was involved in efforts that led him to accept Etienne Tsisekedi as premier.

TIMELINE:
- 1991-09: Rioting by Zaire’s army began, and troops occupied Kinshasa Airport.
- 1991-09: Belgium and France announced plans to dispatch troops to protect and evacuate Western residents; the United States provided transport planes for the evacuation.
- 1991-09: Under pressure from the United States, France, and Belgium, Mobutu acquiesced to the appointment of Etienne Tsisekedi as premier.
- 1991-10: Violence and political tensions increased after Mobutu dismissed Tsisekedi; European evacuations were nearly complete by 25 October.
