- 1991-10: Mobutu announced he would remain in office, and opposition parties formed a parallel regime in Kinshasa.
- 1991-10 to 1991-11: French troops departed on 31 October and Belgian troops on 4 November, ending the international crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary identifies no specific congressional decision or vote. The U.S. role described was limited to providing transport aircraft for the evacuation and applying pressure in connection with Zaire’s political transition, rather than undertaking combat operations.

Crisis 403 — Bosnia (1995)

ACTION: 1995: Fighting over Bosnia’s status continued among Bosnian Muslim, Croat, and Serb forces, with Serbia supporting the Bosnian Serbs. At the uncontested military action level, the United States actually used military force; the summary does not specify the particular operation or additional military options actively under consideration.

TIMELINE:
- 1992-03: Bosnia declared independence after a referendum; fighting began between Bosnian Muslim and Croat forces and the Yugoslav National Army and Bosnian Serbs.
- 1992-04: The EU recognized Bosnia’s independence on 6 April, and the United States did so on 7 April; fighting intensified, including attacks and an artillery barrage against Sarajevo beginning on 21 April.
- 1995-11: The crisis ended on 21 November.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning whether and how the United States should undertake military action in response to the Bosnia crisis. Over the period, the U.S. position reached actual uncontested military action, although the summary does not identify specific congressional decisions or changes in them.

Crisis 406 — Iraq No-Fly Zone (1992)

ACTION: The United States, together with France and the United Kingdom, established an air exclusion zone south of Iraq’s 32nd Parallel to protect the Shia population amid reported repression and military activity. At the combat level, the United States actively enforced the zone with military aircraft; Iraqi aircraft and helicopters were barred from the area, and no U.S. deaths occurred.

TIMELINE:
- 1992-08: Reports of increased Iraqi military activity and human-rights violations among southern Shia preceded allied consideration of a no-fly zone.
- 1992-08-18: U.S., British, and French officials announced the decision to impose an air exclusion zone; the United Kingdom announced the deployment of six Tornado jets.
- 1992-08-20: France announced it would send 10 Mirage-2000 jets; Iraq denounced the plan.
- 1992-08-26: President Bush officially announced the exclusion zone, immediately banning Iraqi helicopters and aircraft south of the 32nd Parallel.
- 1992-09-01: Russia announced it would send two warships to the Gulf as the initiating powers strengthened their military presence.
- 1992-09-08: Iraq reassured U.S., British, and French pilots monitoring the zone that they would not face harm if forced to land in Iraq, marking a reduction in tensions.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the United States’ military participation in establishing and enforcing the air exclusion zone over southern Iraq. Over the period, the issue moved from consideration and allied announcement of the zone to its active combat-level enforcement, while Iraq’s subsequent compliance reduced the immediate crisis.

Crisis 408 — N. Korea Nuclear I (1993)

ACTION: The crisis centered on North Korea’s threatened withdrawal from the NPT, restricted IAEA inspections, and suspected nuclear-material activities. At the U.S. escalation level of **No Response**, the United States did not take military action or actively weigh a military operation; it pursued talks, sought international sanctions, and temporarily suspended the Team Spirit exercise.

TIMELINE:
- 1993-03: North Korea refused an IAEA request for special inspections and, on 12 March, announced its intention to withdraw from the NPT.
- 1993-06: The IAEA suspended technical assistance on 10 June; North Korea withdrew from the IAEA on 13 June; on 14 June, the United States called for a UN-authorized international sanctions coalition.
- 1993-06: U.S.–North Korean talks in New York from 2–11 June produced a suspension of North Korea’s NPT withdrawal.
- 1993-07: U.S.–North Korean talks in Geneva from 14–19 July produced North Korea’s proposal to replace its graphite-moderated reactors with light-water reactors.
- 1994-02: An interim agreement on 25 February provided for cancellation of the 1994 Team Spirit exercise and the resumption of IAEA inspections.
- 1994-03: IAEA-reported discrepancies led North Korea to deny permission for further sampling on 14 March and caused the suspension of bilateral U.S.–North Korean talks.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the U.S. response to North Korea’s nuclear activities, including the possible use of international economic sanctions. Over the period, the immediate U.S. approach shifted toward negotiations, inspection arrangements, and suspension of the Team Spirit exercise rather than military action.

Crisis 411 — Haiti Mil. Regime (1994)

ACTION: The United States responded to Haiti’s military junta, repression, and refugee outflow by preparing to intervene to remove the junta and restore President Aristide. After the Carter-brokered agreement, approximately 20,000 U.S. troops entered Haiti in September 1994 and took control with virtually no opposition; the operation reached the level of uncontested military action.

TIMELINE:
- 1994-07: A major refugee outflow, including an estimated 12,300 Haitian “boat people” rescued from 1–9 July, intensified the U.S. crisis response.
- 1994-07: UN Security Council Resolution 940 authorized member-states to use “all necessary means” to facilitate the departure of Haiti’s military leadership.
- 1994-09: President Clinton announced an impending U.S. military intervention while authorizing a mediation mission led by Carter, Powell, and Nunn.
- 1994-09: The Carter mission reached an agreement for junta leaders to leave Haiti, Aristide to return, and UN economic sanctions to be lifted.
- 1994-09: Approximately 20,000 U.S. troops entered Haiti with a mandate to remain up to six months if necessary and rapidly took control.
- 1994-10: Raoul Cedras resigned as commander in chief of Haiti’s armed forces.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of whether and how to address a potential U.S. military intervention to remove Haiti’s junta and restore Aristide, amid the administration’s effort to secure a broader political consensus. After the September agreement, the immediate decision shifted from authorizing or responding to a prospective invasion toward addressing the deployment and continued presence of U.S. troops in Haiti.

Crisis 412 — Iraq Deploy./Kuwait (1994)

ACTION: Iraq moved an estimated 40,000–50,000 troops toward Kuwait, prompting the United States to conduct a show of force without combat. The United States ordered the aircraft carrier *Eisenhower* and an Aegis cruiser to the Red Sea, sent 4,000 troops to Kuwait, later announced 36,000 additional troops and aircraft for the Persian Gulf, and placed 155,000 ground troops on alert while seeking support for possible military action if necessary.

TIMELINE:
- 1994-10: Iraqi forces began moving toward Kuwait on 2 October amid tensions over UN sanctions.
- 1994-10: Iraq deployed an estimated 40,000–50,000 troops near Kuwait on 7 October; the United States sent 4,000 troops to Kuwait and ordered naval forces to the Red Sea.
- 1994-10: On 8 October, President Clinton warned Iraq against repeating the mistakes of the past; on 9 October, he announced 36,000 additional troops and aircraft for the Persian Gulf.
- 1994-10: On 10 October, Iraq announced that its forces would withdraw from the Kuwaiti border; Clinton sought allied support for possible military action.
- 1994-10: On 11 October, Iraq began withdrawing forces, while the United States announced further deployments and placed 155,000 ground troops on alert.
- 1994-10: On 13 October, Iraq and Russia announced an agreement concerning recognition of Kuwait’s sovereignty and borders and possible sanctions relief; the United States and United Kingdom rejected negotiations on lifting sanctions.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the deployment and funding of U.S. forces sent to the Gulf, as well as the prospect of authorizing or otherwise addressing possible military action to defend Kuwait. As Iraq withdrew, the immediate issue shifted from responding to an active troop buildup toward sustaining the precautionary deployment and addressing future Iraqi threats.

Crisis 415 — Third Taiwan Strait (1995)

ACTION: The crisis began after the United States announced it would issue President Lee Teng-hui a visa for a private June 1995 visit, reversing restrictions on high-level Taiwanese contacts. As tensions escalated, the United States conducted a show of force without combat: the USS *Nimitz* was sent to the Taiwan Strait in December 1995, and carrier battle groups were deployed to the region in March 1996.

TIMELINE:
- 1995-04: Secretary of State Warren Christopher told China that the United States would not issue Lee Teng-hui a visa, while noting congressional pressure to permit the visit.
- 1995-05: The State Department announced that it would issue Lee a visa, triggering the crisis for China.
- 1995-06: Lee visited the United States for three days and met with members of the Senate and House; cross-strait discussions were postponed.
- 1995-07: China conducted military exercises and M-9 missile tests near Taiwan; Taiwan responded with a presidential address stating it could not be repressed by outside forces.
- 1995-12: The United States sent the USS *Nimitz* to the Taiwan Strait, the first such U.S. ship patrol there since 1976.
- 1996-03: U.S. carrier battle groups were sent to the region during China’s exercises near Taiwan; Taiwan held its presidential election on 23 March, after which China’s exercises stopped.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the issue of whether to press the administration to permit Lee Teng-hui to visit the United States, despite the administration’s initial position that such a visa would not be issued. During the crisis, Lee met with members of both the Senate and House during his visit.

Crisis 419 — Desert Strike (1996)

ACTION: Iraq intervened in the Kurdish civil war north of the 36th parallel and quickly captured Arbil, breaching the protected zone. The United States increased air sorties, conducted unilateral bombing of Iraqi military positions and air-defense systems from 3–4 September, extended the southern air exclusion zone, and later destroyed air-defense systems that fired on U.S. aircraft; it also threatened further air strikes but did not escalate beyond these combat actions.

TIMELINE:
- 1996-08: The KDP requested Iraqi assistance against advancing PUK forces.
- 1996-08-31: Approximately 40,000 Iraqi troops intervened, captured Arbil, and advanced north of the 36th parallel.
- 1996-09-01: President Clinton announced that the United States would respond aggressively; U.S. air sorties over Iraq increased.
- 1996-09-03: The United States began Operation Desert Strike, bombing Iraqi military positions and air-defense systems.
- 1996-09-04: The United States completed the reported Desert Strike attacks and extended the southern air exclusion zone from the 32nd to the 33rd parallel.
- 1996-09-11: After Iraqi forces fired on U.S. patrol aircraft, the United States destroyed the involved air-defense systems; on 14 September Iraq declared it would stop firing on patrols and observe the exclusion zones.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the United States’ military response to Iraq’s breach, including the use of air power and the possibility of additional strikes. Over the period, the response moved from increased patrols and consideration of further escalation to completed bombing, destruction of Iraqi air defenses after an attack on U.S. aircraft, and a decision not to escalate further after Iraq pledged to comply.

Crisis 421 — Zaire Civil War (1996)

ACTION: The 1996 rebellion in eastern Zaire escalated into civil war involving the ADFL, Banyamulenge forces, the Zairean government, and forces associated with Rwanda and other neighboring states. The United States approved and prepared to send 1,000 troops as part of a proposed multinational force to secure humanitarian assistance, but the force was later reconsidered after large numbers of refugees returned; the summary does not state that U.S. troops conducted combat operations.

TIMELINE:
- 1996-10: Fighting by Banyamulenge and ADFL forces triggered the crisis; Zaire imposed military rule in Kivu on 29 October, and Rwanda deployed troops to eastern Zaire on 30 October.
- 1996-11: The ADFL captured Goma and took control of North and South Kivu; France and Spain proposed a UN-sponsored force, and the United States agreed to send 1,000 troops.
- 1996-11: A multinational force was prepared by 14 November to protect aid workers, but representatives of 29 countries and six humanitarian agencies called for its cancellation on 22–23 November after refugee returns.
- 1996-12: The ADFL, in collaboration with Rwandan, Burundian, and Ugandan forces, launched an offensive along eastern Zaire’s border and expanded its territorial control.
- 1997-02: The ADFL launched another offensive, capturing Isiro and advancing toward Buta; the UN Security Council endorsed a five-point peace plan.
- 1997-03: As the situation deteriorated, an international force of more than 1,000 troops, mostly from the United States, France, and Belgium, was assembled.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. participation in a proposed multinational military force, including the planned deployment of 1,000 American troops to help secure humanitarian operations. As refugee returns reduced the perceived need for the force, the proposal shifted toward cancellation or reassessment.

Crisis 422 — Unscom I (1997)

ACTION: Iraq repeatedly restricted UNSCOM inspections and, on 13 November 1997, expelled all U.S. personnel working with the commission. The United States responded with a show of force, deploying naval and air assets to the Persian Gulf and weighing military strikes to compel Iraqi compliance, but did not conduct combat operations.

TIMELINE:
- 1997-10: UNSCOM reported continued Iraqi obstruction of inspections; Security Council Resolution 1134 threatened additional measures.
- 1997-11: Resolution 1137 demanded immediate, unconditional inspection access and imposed a travel ban on Iraqi officials who disrupted UNSCOM.
- 1997-11: Iraq expelled all U.S. inspectors; the United States sent the aircraft carrier *George Washington* to the region and accepted the United Kingdom’s offer to send another carrier to the Mediterranean.
- 1997-11: President Clinton ordered additional aircraft, including B-1 bombers, to the Gulf.
- 1998-01: Iraq demanded the stoppage of an UNSCOM team led by Scott Ritter; the United States worked to secure support for a military strike.
- 1998-02: On 23 February, Iraq and the UN signed a memorandum reaffirming UNSCOM’s authority and providing procedures for access to sites Iraq had deemed presidential or sovereign.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the use or preparation of U.S. military force to compel Iraq to comply with UNSCOM and Security Council requirements. Over the period, the immediate U.S. posture shifted from deploying forces as a precautionary show of force to actively weighing military strikes, while diplomatic negotiations ultimately produced the 23 February memorandum.

Crisis 425 — India/Pakistan Nuclear Tests (1998)

ACTION: India conducted nuclear tests on 11 and 13 May 1998, and Pakistan conducted five nuclear tests on 28 May, amid heightened tensions over security and Kashmir. The United States actively urged Pakistan not to test, including through President Clinton’s conversation with Prime Minister Nawaz Sharif, and imposed sanctions on Pakistan after the tests, in addition to sanctions already imposed on India; it used no combat force.

TIMELINE:
- 1998-04: Pakistan tested the Ghauri medium-range surface-to-surface missile, while reports indicated artillery exchanges with India.
- 1998-05: India conducted nuclear tests on 11 and 13 May, triggering the crisis.
- 1998-05: President Clinton urged Prime Minister Nawaz Sharif not to proceed with Pakistani nuclear tests.
- 1998-05: India announced a moratorium on further nuclear tests on 21 May and offered to negotiate a no-first-use pact with Pakistan.
- 1998-05: Pakistan announced five nuclear tests on 28 May; the United States imposed sanctions on Pakistan, similar to those already imposed on India.
- 1998-06: Pakistan announced a unilateral moratorium on nuclear tests on 11 June, ending the crisis.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. sanctions related to India’s and Pakistan’s nuclear tests. Over the period, the immediate U.S. policy issue shifted from pressure to prevent Pakistan’s tests to sanctions after Pakistan conducted them.

Crisis 426 — DRC Civil War (1998)

ACTION: The DRC civil war began after President Laurent-Desire Kabila ordered foreign troops to leave on 29 July 1998, followed by fighting in Kinshasa on 2 August and intervention by regional states on both sides. The United States reached no response: its involvement was primarily limited to political statements of approval or disapproval, with no U.S. military action described as taken or actively weighed in the summary.

TIMELINE:
- 1998-07: Kabila ordered foreign troops to leave the DRC and dismissed James Kabarehe, triggering the crisis with Rwanda.
- 1998-08: Fighting broke out in Kinshasa; Rwanda and Uganda backed a rebellion, while Angola, Namibia, Zimbabwe, and Chad supported Kabila.
- 1999-07: The governments involved signed the Lusaka cease-fire agreement.
- 2001-02: Joseph Kabila met Rwandan President Paul Kagame in Washington; Ugandan and Rwandan troops began a pullback as a goodwill gesture.
- 2002-04: The DRC and Uganda reached an agreement at Sun City, South Africa, though Rwanda rejected the proposed power-sharing arrangements.
- 2002-07: Rwanda and the DRC signed the Pretoria peace agreement, which called for Rwandan troop withdrawal and disarmament of Rwandan Hutu rebels in the DRC.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced no U.S. military intervention decision identified in the summary. U.S. involvement remained limited primarily to political statements, while the regional parties pursued cease-fire and peace agreements over the course of the conflict.

Crisis 427 — Us Embassy Bombings (1998)

ACTION: On 7 August 1998, bombings near U.S. embassies in Nairobi and Dar es Salaam killed more than 200 people and injured more than 5,000; the United States attributed responsibility to Osama bin Laden, who was based in Afghanistan. After the Taliban refused on 19 August to hand him over, the United States reached the combat level by launching air strikes on 20 August against an alleged terrorist training base in Afghanistan and a pharmaceutical factory in Sudan; no U.S. deaths occurred.

TIMELINE:
- 1998-08: Bombings near U.S. embassies in Nairobi and Dar es Salaam trigger a crisis for the United States, Afghanistan, and later Sudan.
- 1998-08: The U.S. government demands that the Afghan Taliban regime hand over Osama bin Laden.
- 1998-08: Taliban leader Mullah Mohamed Omar states that the Taliban will protect bin Laden and will not hand him over.
- 1998-08: The United States launches air strikes against targets in Afghanistan and Sudan, reportedly killing about 15 people in Afghanistan and seven in Sudan.
- 1998-08: The air strikes mark the end of the crisis for the three crisis actors.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary identifies a U.S. decision to demand bin Laden’s transfer and, after the Taliban’s refusal, to conduct air strikes against targets in Afghanistan and Sudan. It does not specify a separate congressional authorization decision or describe a shift in congressional action over the period.

Crisis 429 — Unscom II (1998)

ACTION: Iraq halted cooperation with UNSCOM and required sanctions review and the removal of UNSCOM chair Richard Butler before resuming activities. The United States increased military mobilization, weighed and prepared an attack, and then conducted combat air strikes with the United Kingdom against more than 90 Iraqi targets from 16–20 December 1998; no U.S. deaths occurred.

TIMELINE:
- 1998-08: Iraq’s National Assembly voted to suspend cooperation with UNSCOM after rejecting requested expanded inspections.
- 1998-09: The UN Security Council adopted Resolution 1194, condemning Iraq’s suspension of cooperation and indefinitely suspending periodic reviews of economic sanctions.
- 1998-10: Iraq announced that all UNSCOM activities would halt immediately, and on 31 October the crisis began for the United States and United Kingdom.
- 1998-11: The Security Council adopted Resolution 1205 demanding that Iraq rescind its anti-UNSCOM statements; on 9 November, the United States and United Kingdom increased Gulf military mobilization and urged the evacuation of UNSCOM personnel and aid workers.
- 1998-11: On 14 November, Saddam Hussein indicated that Iraq would cooperate fully with UNSCOM; U.S. aircraft were already en route, and the United States maintained that it would conduct an unannounced strike if Iraq violated the commitment.
- 1998-12: On 15 December, Butler reported that Iraq had failed to cooperate; U.S. and British forces conducted air strikes from 16–20 December in Operation Desert Fox.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the United States’ response to Iraq’s termination of UNSCOM cooperation, including the use or preparation of military force. As Iraq resumed and then again failed to provide cooperation, the immediate issue shifted from weighing a potential attack to the conduct of the December air campaign.

Crisis 430 — Kosovo (1999)

ACTION: From 20 February to 10 June 1999, NATO members pressured the Federal Republic of Yugoslavia (FRY) to accept a settlement for Kosovo autonomy after negotiations stalled. The United States reached the combat level by participating in NATO’s air attack on FRY military targets beginning 24 March; ground-force use was threatened and considered but never implemented, and the United States recorded no deaths.

TIMELINE:
- 1999-02: The 20 February deadline passed without an agreement; NATO threatened attacks if no settlement was reached by 23 February.
- 1999-02: A tentative Kosovo autonomy agreement was reached on 23 February after heightened NATO mobilization.
- 1999-03: NATO aircraft began attacking FRY military targets on 24 March under Operation Allied Force.
- 1999-05: A U.S. bomb struck the Chinese Embassy in Belgrade on 7 May, killing four Chinese nationals.
- 1999-06: NATO and the FRY signed a technical agreement on 9 June promising the withdrawal of FRY forces from Kosovo.
- 1999-06: The crisis ended on 10 June after the UN resolved to send a peacekeeping force to secure peace and monitor compliance.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify a specific congressional decision or a change in the decision facing Congress over the period. It identifies U.S. participation in NATO’s combat air campaign and the threatened but unused option of deploying ground forces.

Crisis 432 — East Timor II (1999)

ACTION: Violence followed East Timor’s 31 August 1999 referendum and the announcement of its independence vote on 4 September, prompting an Australian-led, UN-authorized peacekeeping mission. The United States remained at the “No Response” escalation level; the summary states that it was politically involved and contributed to the Interfet mission, but does not identify U.S. military action or a U.S. decision to weigh further military action.

TIMELINE:
- 1999-08: East Timor held a referendum on independence from Indonesia.
- 1999-09: The referendum result was announced, showing overwhelming support for independence; violence then escalated.
- 1999-09: Australia-led Interfet, authorized by the United Nations, was established with participation from 19 other countries.
- 1999-10: Interfet troops shot dead two pro-Indonesian militiamen on 6 October in the first fatal clash since deployment.
- 1999-10: Indonesia’s parliament endorsed East Timor’s independence vote on 19 October, signaling the end of the crisis and the planned transition to UN peacekeepers.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify a specific congressional decision regarding the crisis. It states that the United States was politically involved and contributed to Interfet, while its recorded escalation level remained “No Response”; no shift in congressional decisions is specified.

Crisis 434 — Afghanistan (2001)

ACTION: Following the 11 September 2001 attacks, the United States focused on the Taliban regime’s support for and harboring of al-Qaeda. After a regional military buildup, U.S. forces commenced combat operations on 7 October through cruise-missile attacks, bombing sorties, and special-forces operations, coordinated with Northern Alliance ground forces; the escalation level reached combat with more than 1,000 U.S. deaths.

TIMELINE:
- 2001-09: Terrorist attacks in the United States trigger the crisis; President Bush pledges action against states harboring or supporting terrorism.
- 2001-10: After the Taliban continued denying support for al-Qaeda and refused to assist in apprehending its leaders, U.S. and UK forces begin military operations on 7 October.
- 2001-10: U.S. and UK forces conduct air attacks and special-forces operations while coordinating with Northern Alliance infantry units.
- 2001-11: Northern Alliance forces capture Kabul on 23 November.
- 2001-12: An interim government headed by Hamid Karzai is formed on 5 December; the crisis ends on 7 December after the fall of the final Taliban stronghold in Kandahar.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify specific congressional decisions or a shift in congressional action over the period. The concrete U.S. decisions described were executive decisions to conduct military operations against the Taliban and al-Qaeda-related targets, including air attacks and special-forces operations.

Crisis 440 — Iraq (2003)

ACTION: From September 2002 to May 2003, the United States and its partners pressured Iraq over weapons inspections and compliance with UN Resolution 1441, while preparing for possible military action and pursuing diplomatic support. The United States ultimately conducted combat operations that escalated to total war and the fall of Saddam Hussein’s regime; the stated escalation level records combat with more than 1,000 U.S. deaths.

TIMELINE:
- 2002-09: President Bush addressed the UN General Assembly, calling for strong multilateral action if Iraq did not eliminate its weapons and weapons programs.
- 2002-11: The UN Security Council unanimously adopted Resolution 1441, establishing inspection conditions and warning of “the severest consequences” for noncompliance.
- 2002-11: UN weapons inspectors returned to Iraq.
- 2002-12: Iraq submitted a 12,000-page weapons declaration; the United States subsequently declared Iraq in material breach of Resolution 1441.
- 2003-05: The crisis ended with total war and the fall of Saddam Hussein’s regime.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. action against Iraq, including whether to authorize or permit military operations as the administration moved from inspections and diplomatic efforts toward combat. Over the period, the decision context shifted from pressure for Iraqi compliance and pursuit of a peaceful solution to active consideration and execution of war.

Crisis 441 — N. Korea Nuclear II (2002)

ACTION: The United States confronted North Korea in October 2002 over a secret nuclear-enrichment program that violated the Agreed Framework, publicized North Korea’s acknowledgment, and worked with South Korea and Japan to suspend KEDO oil shipments. With the U.S. escalation level at No Response, it did not take military action or actively weigh a military strike in the summary; instead, it initially pursued diplomatic and multilateral pressure, later supporting Six-Party Talks.

TIMELINE:
- 2002-06: U.S. intelligence assessed that North Korea had begun a secret nuclear-enrichment program.
- 2002-10: Assistant Secretary of State James Kelly confronted North Korean officials in Pyongyang; North Korea acknowledged the program, according to the summary.
- 2002-10: The United States publicly disclosed the acknowledgment and secured KEDO’s suspension of further oil shipments after consulting South Korea and Japan.
- 2002-12: North Korea ended the Agreed Framework, announced plans to restart its nuclear program, removed IAEA monitoring equipment, restarted plutonium facilities, and expelled IAEA inspectors.
- 2003-01: North Korea withdrew from the Nuclear Nonproliferation Treaty.
- 2003-08: The first round of Six-Party Talks began, with the United States, North Korea, China, Japan, South Korea, and Russia participating.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify a concrete congressional decision concerning military action or another specific U.S. policy measure. Over the period, the U.S. approach shifted from refusing direct negotiations and seeking complete dismantlement before talks to pursuing multilateral negotiations through the Six-Party Talks.

Crisis 448 — Iran Nuclear II (2006)

ACTION: Iran removed UN seals from its nuclear facilities, prompting U.S. condemnation and threats of UN Security Council action. The United States did not use military force; it pursued sanctions and diplomatic pressure, while military force was discussed as a possible option and the U.S. reached the escalation level of threats of force.

TIMELINE:
- 2006-01: Iran removed UN seals from its nuclear reactors; the United States condemned the action and threatened UNSC action.
- 2006-01: The U.S. pursued harsher and more frequent sanctions, while the EU3 called for UNSC action.
- 2006-03: The UNSC gave Iran 30 days to suspend enrichment.
- 2006-07: The UNSC adopted a resolution allowing tough sanctions if Iran did not stop its nuclear activities within one month.
- 2007-03: The UNSC imposed sanctions on Iran.
- 2007-12: The U.S. released an intelligence report stating that Iran was not seeking to produce nuclear weapons, prompting a U.S. policy shift.

BEFORE CONGRESS: The summary does not identify specific decisions faced by Congress. U.S. policy shifted over the crisis from threats of UNSC action, diplomatic pressure, and sanctions toward a reduced assessment of the Iranian nuclear threat after the December 2007 intelligence report.

Crisis 450 — N. Korea Nuclear III (2006)

ACTION: The United States faced North Korean preparations for a missile launch, followed by a missile test and announcement and conduct of a nuclear test. At the stated escalation level of **No Response**, the United States did not take military action and did not actively weigh a military operation in the summary; it pursued diplomatic efforts, maintained financial sanctions, and supported international sanctions.

TIMELINE:
- 2006-05: The United States obtained intelligence that North Korea was transporting a missile to a launch site; North Korea subsequently announced plans for a missile test.
- 2006-06: The United States formally terminated KEDO’s light-water-reactor project and continued efforts to restart the Six Party Talks while maintaining financial sanctions.
- 2006-07: North Korea test-fired a Taepodong II missile and six other missiles; the U.N. Security Council adopted Resolution 1695 on 15 July.
- 2006-10: North Korea announced on 3 October that it intended to conduct a nuclear weapons test.
- 2006-10: North Korea conducted its first nuclear test on 9 October; the United States and Japan supported U.N. Resolution 1718, which imposed additional measures concerning nuclear and missile-related activity.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the United States’ diplomatic and financial response to North Korea’s missile and nuclear activities, including whether to maintain financial sanctions and support international sanctions. Over the period, the focus shifted from the threatened missile test to responses to the missile launch and then the nuclear test; the summary does not identify a congressional decision to authorize U.S. military action.

Crisis 451 — Israel Lebanon War II (2006)

ACTION: Hezbollah’s 12 July 2006 cross-border attack and hostage-taking triggered an Israeli assault on Lebanon, while Hezbollah fired rockets into Israel. The United States did not deploy combat forces; it provided humanitarian aid and pursued ceasefire diplomacy, while participating in international efforts to authorize an expanded force, and Israel’s escalation—not a U.S. military operation—drove the fighting.

TIMELINE:
- 2006-07: Hezbollah attacked an Israeli patrol, killed three soldiers, took two hostage, and launched rockets; Israel imposed an air and sea blockade and began its assault on Lebanon.
- 2006-07-18: Secretary of State Condoleezza Rice visited Lebanon and Israel, sought a ceasefire, and offered humanitarian aid.
- 2006-07-26: The United States participated in the Rome crisis meeting, which called for immediate work toward a ceasefire and an international force to support UNIFIL.
- 2006-07-30: An Israeli attack on Qana killed almost 60 civilians; Israel agreed to suspend aerial bombardment for two days while its bombardment of southern Lebanon continued the next day.
- 2006-08-08: Israel approved a major escalation and authorized its army to advance farther into Lebanon.
- 2006-08-14: UN Security Council Resolution 1701’s cessation of hostilities took effect; Israel began slowly transferring control of the area to Lebanese troops.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. humanitarian assistance and the United States’ diplomatic and international role in efforts to secure a ceasefire and support an expanded UNIFIL force. Over the period, the focus shifted from ceasefire mediation and aid toward implementation of an internationally authorized stabilization arrangement; the summary does not specify a separate congressional vote or authorization.

Crisis 452 — Ethiopia Invasion Somalia (2006)

ACTION: Ethiopia’s crisis centered on the UIC’s expansion in Somalia and the resulting threat to Ethiopia and Somalia’s transitional government. The United States actively pursued aid or sanctions-level measures, including backing a UN Security Council resolution authorizing regional peacekeeping and partially lifting Somalia’s arms embargo; it did not use U.S. combat forces.

TIMELINE:
- 2006-06: The UIC took Mogadishu and consolidated control over much of southern Somalia.
- 2006-10: Ethiopian- and Somali-backed forces launched an offensive, while the UIC declared jihad against Ethiopia.
- 2006-10: Ethiopia acknowledged sending several hundred armed military advisers into Somalia and described itself as technically at war with the UIC.
- 2006-11: Ethiopia completed preparations for war, and its parliament approved military action against the UIC; Ethiopian troops and equipment moved toward Baidoa and the border.
- 2006-12: The United States backed a UN Security Council resolution authorizing IGAD and AU peacekeepers and partially lifting Somalia’s arms embargo.
- 2006-12: The UIC demanded Ethiopian withdrawal, mediation efforts failed, and heavy fighting resumed after Ethiopia rejected the demand.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the U.S. response through aid or sanctions-level measures rather than the deployment of U.S. combat forces. Over the period, the concrete U.S.-backed option involved authorizing and enabling a regional peacekeeping mission, including a partial arms-embargo exemption for those forces.

Crisis 454 — Ethiopia-Eritrea III (2007)

ACTION: Ethiopia and Eritrea escalated tensions over the unresolved border demarcation process, including troop movements, skirmishes, and accusations of possible renewed war. The United States took no action in the crisis and was not reported as actively weighing military action; it was identified as a witness to the Algiers Agreement and accused by Eritrea of supporting Ethiopia.

TIMELINE:
- 2007-09: The EEBC reported that it could not make further progress on the border impasse; Eritrea and Ethiopia subsequently moved troops into or near the Temporary Security Zone.
- 2007-09: Ethiopia threatened to terminate the Algiers Agreement unless Eritrea complied; Eritrea replied that the threat could precede renewed hostilities.
- 2007-10: Several skirmishes occurred, and the UN Secretary General called for restraint and implementation of the Algiers Agreement.
- 2007-10: Eritrea accused Ethiopia of planning an invasion and alleged U.S. support for the plan; both sides reinforced their forces near the buffer zone.
- 2007-11: The UN Secretary General demanded Eritrean withdrawal from the buffer zone, while the Security Council urged both sides to resolve the dispute.
- 2007-11: The EEBC finalized its 2002 border ruling and dissolved itself after the November 30 deadline; the crisis faded on December 1.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced no reported decision on U.S. military action in the summary. The United States remained identified as a witness to the Algiers Agreement, with no reported shift to a military response.

Crisis 10001 — Virginius Affair

ACTION: The Virginius Affair was a U.S.-Spain crisis touched off when Spanish authorities in Cuba captured the Virginius, a ship running arms to Cuban rebels while flying the U.S. flag, and executed 53 aboard including American citizens. Secretary of State Hamilton Fish pressed reparations amid war talk, and President Grant ordered the Navy to concentrate its Atlantic squadrons at Key West as a demonstration toward Spain. The United States never entered combat and settled the dispute diplomatically, so its escalation reached only a Show of Force.

TIMELINE:
- 1873-10-31: The Spanish gunboat Tornado captured the Virginius off Cuba
- 1873-11: Spanish authorities executed 53 of the crew and passengers, provoking outrage
- 1873-11-29: Fish and Spanish minister Polo signed a protocol for surrender of the ship and reparations
- 1873-12: Grant ordered the Navy to assemble a fleet at Key West as a show of force
- 1874-01: The fleet conducted maneuvers off Key West; no hostilities occurred
- 1875-02: Spain paid an indemnity to the families of the executed Americans

Crisis 10002 — War of the Pacific

ACTION: The War of the Pacific pitted Chile against Peru and Bolivia, and the United States involved itself as a would-be mediator protecting American lives and commercial interests. U.S. Navy vessels were kept in the war zone to safeguard citizens, and the USS Lackawanna hosted a failed peace conference at Arica. The United States made no military threats against the belligerents and never entered combat, so its posture amounted to a limited protective naval presence.

TIMELINE:
- 1879-04: Chile declared war on Peru and Bolivia
- 1879: U.S. warships remained off Peru and Chile to protect American nationals
- 1880-10-22: A U.S.-arranged conference aboard the USS Lackawanna at Arica collapsed
- 1881-01: Chilean forces occupied Lima; U.S. warships lay off Callao to protect residents
- 1881: Secretary Blaine advanced mediation proposals
- 1883-10: The Treaty of Ancon ended the war; Washington took no military measures

Crisis 10003 — 1885 Panama Crisis

ACTION: When Colombia stripped troops from Panama to fight rebels elsewhere, insurgents seized the isthmus and disrupted transit across the Panama Railroad, threatening U.S. rights under the 1846 Bidlack-Mallarino Treaty. The Navy and Marines assembled an expeditionary force of roughly 750 that landed at Colon and Panama City to restore and guard transit. The landings met no organized resistance, so the action was an Uncontested Military Action rather than combat.

TIMELINE:
- 1885-03: Colombia withdrew forces from Panama; insurgents rose across the isthmus
- 1885-03-29: Rebels under Pedro Prestan seized Colon
- 1885-03-31: Much of Colon was burned as fighting spread
- 1885-04-02: Navy ordered a Marine battalion assembled and dispatched
- 1885-04-11: The first Marine battalion landed at Colon; Cdr. McCalla secured the transit route
- 1885-04-27: Marines landed in Panama City to help restore order
- 1885-04-30: U.S. forces began withdrawing as Colombian control was reestablished

Crisis 10004 — Samoan Crisis

ACTION: The Samoan Crisis was a three-way standoff among the United States, Germany, and Britain over control of the Samoan Islands. To protect its Pago Pago coaling rights and check German expansion, the United States sent the warships Nipsic, Vandalia, and Trenton to confront the German squadron in Apia harbor. The vessels remained in a tense standoff without exchanging fire, so the U.S. posture was a Show of Force; no U.S. combat occurred before a cyclone wrecked the fleets in March 1889.

TIMELINE:
- 1888-06: German-backed Tamasese faced a revolt by Mata'afa; U.S. and British consuls opposed German control
- 1888-11: The United States dispatched the USS Nipsic to Apia
- 1888-12-18: Mata'afa's warriors defeated a German landing force at Fangalii
- 1889-01: Cleveland ordered the USS Trenton and Vandalia to reinforce Apia, producing a warship standoff
- 1889-02: The U.S., Germany, and Britain agreed to convene a Berlin conference while warships remained in confrontation

Crisis 10007 — Italy (New Orleans killings)

ACTION: After a New Orleans mob lynched eleven Italians in the murder case of the city's police chief, Italy recalled its minister and suspended relations, demanding punishment and indemnity. The rupture produced a press-driven war scare that highlighted U.S. naval weakness, but the U.S. government neither mobilized forces nor threatened Italy, handling the affair entirely through diplomacy and eventual reparations.

TIMELINE:
- 1890-10-15: New Orleans police chief David Hennessy was fatally shot; Italian immigrants blamed
- 1891-03-14: A mob lynched eleven Italians at the Orleans Parish Prison
- 1891-03-31: Italy recalled minister Fava and suspended relations, demanding prosecution and indemnity
- 1891: A press war scare underscored U.S. naval unpreparedness; Washington took no military steps
- 1891-12-09: Harrison condemned the lynching in his annual message
- 1892-04: The U.S. paid a 125,000-franc indemnity to Italy; relations restored

Crisis 10008 — Cuban War of Independence

ACTION: This crisis covers the pre-1898 U.S. debate over the Cuban insurrection against Spain, when U.S. posture was diplomatic rather than military. The U.S. proclaimed neutrality but pressed Spain with escalating diplomatic threats, Cleveland (Dec 1896) and McKinley (1897) both warning of intervention if Spain did not end the conflict, while Congress pushed belligerency resolutions. The highest U.S. action in this window was coercive diplomacy and threats of intervention; the actual combat entry against Spain in April 1898 is coded under the USS Maine crisis (10010).

TIMELINE:
- 1895-02: Cuban War of Independence begins (Grito de Baire, Feb 24)
- 1895-06: Cleveland proclaims U.S. neutrality (June 12)
- 1896-02: Spanish Gen. Weyler begins reconcentration policy, shifting U.S. opinion
- 1896-12: Cleveland warns the U.S. may intervene if Spain fails to resolve the crisis
- 1897-07: McKinley presses Spain to halt Weyler or face U.S. intervention
- 1897-10: New Spanish government recalls Weyler and offers Cuba limited autonomy
- 1898-04: Crisis merges into U.S.-Spanish war (combat coded under crisno 10010)

Crisis 10009 — Venezuela Border Crisis

ACTION: The U.S. intervened in the long-running Venezuela-British Guiana boundary dispute by invoking the Monroe Doctrine to demand British arbitration. Secretary of State Olney's July 1895 note and President Cleveland's December 17, 1895 message to Congress amounted to a threat of war, warning that the U.S. would resist by force any British boundary imposed beyond a U.S.-determined line. No fleet was dispatched and no forces mobilized; escalation stayed at the level of diplomatic coercion and verbal threats before Britain agreed to arbitrate.

TIMELINE:
- 1895-07: Olney note demands Britain submit the boundary to arbitration, asserting the Monroe Doctrine
- 1895-11: Lord Salisbury's reply rejects U.S. claims and the demand to arbitrate
- 1895-12-17: Cleveland's message to Congress threatens to enforce a U.S.-determined boundary line
- 1895-12: Congress appropriates funds for a U.S. boundary commission; war talk peaks in press
- 1896-11: Britain agrees to arbitration, defusing the crisis
- 1897-02: U.S.-British treaty establishing an arbitration tribunal signed

Crisis 10010 — USS Maine Explosion

ACTION: The USS Maine, sent to Havana as a show of force to protect U.S. interests, exploded on February 15, 1898, killing about 260 crew and inflaming U.S. opinion. After a Naval Court of Inquiry blamed an external mine, McKinley's war message and a congressional joint resolution led to a naval blockade of Cuba and a declaration of war. U.S. forces reached full combat: the blockade began April 22, 1898, with the first hostile naval action, followed by the formal declaration and the Battle of Manila Bay.

TIMELINE:
- 1898-01-25: USS Maine arrives in Havana harbor as a show of force
- 1898-02-15: Maine explodes, killing about 260 aboard
- 1898-03-28: U.S. Naval Court of Inquiry attributes the blast to an external mine
- 1898-04-11: McKinley's war message to Congress
- 1898-04-20: Joint resolution and ultimatum to Spain; McKinley signs
- 1898-04-22: U.S. begins naval blockade of Cuba; first combat action
- 1898-04-25: Congress declares war, retroactive to April 21
- 1898-05-01: Battle of Manila Bay

Crisis 10011 — Boxer Rebellion

ACTION: During the anti-foreign Boxer uprising in China, the U.S. committed Marines and Army troops to protect its legation and join the multinational relief of Peking. U.S. forces engaged in sustained combat: Marines fought with the Seymour column, defended the besieged legations, and the China Relief Expedition (about 2,000 Americans) battled Chinese forces at Tientsin and Peking. This was full combat by U.S. ground forces alongside the Eight-Nation Alliance.

TIMELINE:
- 1900-05-31: U.S. Marines reach Peking to guard the U.S. legation
- 1900-06-10: Seymour relief expedition departs Tientsin with a U.S. contingent and engages Boxers
- 1900-06-20: Siege of the legations begins; U.S. Marines in the defense
- 1900-07-13: Battle of Tientsin; U.S. 9th Infantry engaged, Col. Liscum killed
- 1900-08-04: China Relief Expedition advances on Peking with ~2,000 U.S. troops
- 1900-08-14: Allied forces relieve Peking; U.S. troops fight in the Battle of Peking

Crisis 10012 — Alaska Boundary Dispute

ACTION: The U.S. and Britain/Canada disputed the Alaska panhandle boundary, sharpened by Klondike gold-rush access to ports like Skagway. Roosevelt applied 'big stick' pressure, privately telling Secretary Hay he was willing to go to war and threatening to run the line unilaterally if arbitration failed. The U.S. also maintained and reinforced Army garrisons in the disputed district, choosing Haines in July 1902 for a permanent fort, a show of force short of any combat, before the 1903 tribunal resolved the boundary in the U.S. favor.

TIMELINE:
- 1898-06: Boundary tension rises as Klondike traffic makes panhandle ports strategically vital
- 1899: U.S. Army troops stationed at Skagway assert U.S. presence
- 1902-07: Haines chosen for a permanent fort to monitor the disputed passes
- 1903-01: Hay-Herbert Treaty establishes a six-member arbitration tribunal
- 1903-09: Tribunal convenes in London
- 1903-10-20: Tribunal rules largely for the U.S.
- 1903-12: Boundary settlement finalized

Crisis 10013 — Venezuelan Crisis of 1902-1903

ACTION: Britain, Germany, and Italy blockaded Venezuela from December 1902 to force debt repayment by President Cipriano Castro. President Theodore Roosevelt grew wary of German intentions but did not treat it as a Monroe Doctrine violation warranting war. The U.S. Navy under Admiral Dewey had concentrated a large fleet in the Caribbean for maneuvers, which functioned as a deterrent show of force. No U.S. combat occurred; the crisis was resolved through arbitration.

TIMELINE:
- 1902-12: European creditors deliver ultimatums to Venezuela; U.S. urges arbitration
- 1902-12-09: Britain and Germany begin naval blockade
- 1902-12: Admiral Dewey's assembled U.S. fleet remains in Caribbean waters
- 1903-01: German warships bombard Fort San Carlos, heightening U.S. concern
- 1903-02-13: Washington Protocols signed, referring debt claims to arbitration
- 1903-02-19: Blockade formally lifted; crisis ends without U.S. military engagement

Crisis 10014 — Panamanian Rebellion / Panama Independence 1903

ACTION: After Colombia's Senate rejected the Hay-Herran Treaty, the U.S. positioned the gunboat USS Nashville off Colon to support Panamanian secessionists. When Panama declared independence on November 3, 1903, U.S. naval presence and control of the Panama Railroad prevented Colombian troops from moving to suppress the revolt. This was a deliberate show of force rather than combat; the Colombians withdrew after negotiation.

TIMELINE:
- 1903-08-12: Colombian Senate rejects the Hay-Herran Treaty
- 1903-11-02: USS Nashville arrives at Colon
- 1903-11-03: Panama declares independence; U.S. presence blocks Colombian advance
- 1903-11-05: U.S. Marines land at Colon; Colombian troops re-embark and depart
- 1903-11-06: United States extends de facto recognition to Panama
- 1903-11-18: Hay-Bunau-Varilla Treaty signed

Crisis 10017 — Mexican Civil War I: Border

ACTION: As the Mexican Revolution erupted in 1910, the U.S. reinforced the border to protect American lives and property. In March 1911 President Taft dispatched a Maneuver Division of roughly 20,000 troops to the Texas border, a major show of force. U.S. troops occasionally returned fire when battles at border towns sent stray rounds into El Paso and Douglas, but these were incidental defensive exchanges, not a deliberate U.S. combat intervention.

TIMELINE:
- 1910-11: Mexican Revolution begins; U.S. Army units reinforce the border
- 1911-03: Taft orders the Maneuver Division (~20,000 troops) to the Texas border
- 1911-04: Battle of Ciudad Juarez; stray fire into El Paso prompts limited U.S. return fire
- 1912-03: U.S. imposes an arms embargo affecting Mexican factions
- 1913-02: Huerta seizes power; Wilson withholds recognition
- 1914-02: Wilson lifts the arms embargo while keeping troops on the border

Crisis 10018 — Mexican Civil War II: Veracruz

ACTION: The Tampico Affair of April 9, 1914 gave President Wilson grounds for armed action. Learning that a German steamer was bringing arms to Huerta, Wilson ordered the seizure of the customs house at Veracruz. On April 21, 1914, U.S. Marines and sailors landed and met armed resistance, producing several days of urban combat. U.S. warships shelled defended positions; by April 24 U.S. forces controlled the city, beginning a roughly six-month occupation.

TIMELINE:
- 1914-04-09: Tampico Affair; U.S. sailors detained then released
- 1914-04-20: Wilson asks Congress to authorize armed force against Huerta
- 1914-04-21: U.S. Marines and bluejackets land at Veracruz; fighting begins
- 1914-04-24: U.S. forces secure the city with casualties on both sides
- 1914-05: U.S. Army assumes administration of Veracruz
- 1914-11-23: U.S. forces withdraw, ending the occupation

Crisis 10019 — Mexican Civil War III: Villa

ACTION: After Pancho Villa's Villistas raided Columbus, New Mexico, on 9 March 1916 and killed at least 17 Americans, President Wilson ordered a Punitive Expedition under Brig. Gen. John J. Pershing. Roughly 6,600 U.S. troops crossed into Mexico on 15 March 1916 to pursue Villa, escalating to sustained cross-border combat. The expedition fought Villista forces and, at Carrizal, clashed with Mexican federal troops, but failed to capture Villa and withdrew.

TIMELINE:
- 1916-03-09: Villa raids Columbus, New Mexico, killing ~17 Americans
- 1916-03-15: Pershing's ~6,600-man Punitive Expedition crosses into Mexico
- 1916-03-29: U.S. cavalry engages Villistas at Guerrero
- 1916-06-21: Battle of Carrizal; U.S. 10th Cavalry fights Mexican federal troops
- 1917-01: Wilson orders the expedition's withdrawal
- 1917-02-05: Last U.S. troops recross into the United States

Crisis 10021 — Lusitania

ACTION: After Germany declared the waters around Britain a war zone in February 1915, the submarine U-20 sank the British liner Lusitania on May 7, 1915, killing 1,198 including 128 Americans. Despite public outrage, President Wilson chose a diplomatic rather than military response, taking no armed action. The U.S. protested through a series of formal notes demanding Germany abandon unrestricted submarine warfare.

TIMELINE:
- 1915-02-04: Germany declares a submarine war zone around the British Isles
- 1915-05-07: U-20 sinks the Lusitania, killing 128 Americans
- 1915-05-13: Wilson sends the first Lusitania note protesting the sinking
- 1915-06-08: Secretary of State Bryan resigns over the firmness of U.S. policy
- 1915-07-21: Third note warns further violations would be viewed as deliberately unfriendly; no military action taken

Crisis 10023 — Unrestricted Submarine Warfare / U.S. Entry into WWI

ACTION: Germany's 31 January 1917 announcement resuming unrestricted submarine warfare, plus the Zimmermann Telegram, pushed the United States from neutrality to war. Wilson severed relations in February and asked Congress for a declaration of war on 2 April 1917; Congress declared war on 6 April 1917. The first U.S. naval action followed as destroyers deployed to European waters for anti-submarine escort duty.

TIMELINE:
- 1917-01-31: Germany announces resumption of unrestricted submarine warfare
- 1917-02-03: U.S. severs diplomatic relations with Germany
- 1917-03-01: Zimmermann Telegram published in U.S. press
- 1917-04-02: Wilson asks Congress for a declaration of war
- 1917-04-06: United States declares war on Germany
- 1917-05-04: First U.S. Navy destroyer division arrives at Queenstown, Ireland

Crisis 10024 — Pearl Harbor / Germany

ACTION: This crisis covers the outbreak of open war between the United States and Germany. After Pearl Harbor, Germany and Italy declared war on the United States on 11 December 1941, and Congress reciprocated the same day. U.S. forces entered declared combat against Germany, having already fought an undeclared Atlantic naval war since September 1941.

TIMELINE:
- 1941-09-04: USS Greer clashes with a U-boat; undeclared Atlantic naval war underway
- 1941-10-31: USS Reuben James sunk by U-552
- 1941-12-07: Japan attacks Pearl Harbor; the U.S. enters the war
- 1941-12-11: Germany and Italy declare war on the U.S.; Congress declares war the same day
- 1941-12-22: Arcadia Conference confirms the 'Germany first' strategy
- 1942-01: German U-boat offensive begins off the U.S. East Coast

Crisis 10101 — Syria (2007)

ACTION: The crisis centered on U.S. concerns about Syria’s regional conduct, including its relationships with Iran, Hezbollah, and armed groups in Lebanon and Iraq. The United States took no military action against Syria and did not publicly advance an active plan for direct military strikes during this period.

TIMELINE:
- 2007-01: The United States continued diplomatic and economic pressure on Syria, including existing sanctions and efforts to isolate the Syrian government.
- 2007-02: U.S. officials continued to raise concerns about Syrian support for Hezbollah and Palestinian militant organizations, as well as alleged facilitation of foreign fighters into Iraq.
- 2007-05: Congress considered Syria-related legislation and oversight concerning sanctions, terrorism, proliferation, and Syria’s role in Lebanon and Iraq.
- 2007-08: U.S. policy remained focused on diplomatic pressure, sanctions, and regional security concerns rather than military action against Syria.
- 2007-09: Israeli forces conducted an operation against a suspected Syrian nuclear-related site; the United States did not respond with military action against Syria during the period.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning sanctions, oversight, and other nonmilitary measures directed at Syria’s conduct and alleged proliferation activities. No congressional decision authorizing U.S. military action against Syria was reached during the period.

Crisis 10102 — Somalia (1993)

ACTION: Amid Somalia’s civil war and famine, the United States provided humanitarian relief and security through Operation Provide Relief and the multinational Unified Task Force (UNITAF), including ground combat operations in late 1992 and early 1993. After the transition to the UN-led mission UNOSOM II, U.S. forces participated in combat operations against forces loyal to Mohamed Farrah Aidid; the October 1993 Battle of Mogadishu resulted in 18 U.S. military deaths.

TIMELINE:
- 1992-04: The United Nations established UNOSOM to support humanitarian relief and a ceasefire in Somalia.
- 1992-12: President George H. W. Bush authorized U.S.-led Operation Restore Hope, and U.S. Marines began deploying to Somalia to secure relief operations.
- 1993-05: Operational control of the international force passed from the U.S.-led UNITAF to the UNOSOM II mission.
- 1993-06: Fighting between UNOSOM II forces and Aidid’s Somali National Alliance intensified after the killing of Pakistani peacekeepers; U.S. forces began targeted operations in Mogadishu.
- 1993-10: A U.S. special-operations raid in Mogadishu led to a prolonged firefight; 18 U.S. servicemembers were killed and the United States subsequently began withdrawing its forces.
- 1993-12: The United States continued its drawdown from Somalia while supporting the remaining UNOSOM II mission.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions over funding and authorizing the deployment and continued presence of U.S. forces supporting humanitarian relief and UN operations in Somalia. After the October fighting, congressional consideration shifted toward limiting the mission and establishing a deadline for the withdrawal of U.S. combat forces.

Crisis 10103 — Rwandan Genocide (1994)

ACTION: Following the April 6 assassination of Rwanda’s president, extremist forces and militias carried out a genocide against Tutsi civilians and moderate Hutu. The United States did not deploy forces to stop the violence or undertake military intervention; its principal involvement was diplomatic, humanitarian, and the evacuation of U.S. citizens.

TIMELINE:
- 1994-04-06: President Juvénal Habyarimana was killed when his aircraft was shot down near Kigali; organized killings began shortly afterward.
- 1994-04-09: The United States evacuated its citizens and closed its embassy in Kigali; no U.S. combat force was sent to Rwanda.
- 1994-04-21: The UN Security Council reduced the United Nations Assistance Mission for Rwanda (UNAMIR) to a small residual force; the United States did not propose or undertake a unilateral military intervention.
- 1994-05-17: The UN Security Council authorized expanding UNAMIR, but the deployment was delayed and the United States did not provide a combat intervention.
- 1994-07-04: The Rwandan Patriotic Front captured Kigali, marking the collapse of the interim government in the capital.
- 1994-07-15: By the end of the specified period, the United States had not conducted military operations in Rwanda; U.S. policy remained focused on evacuation, diplomacy, and humanitarian assistance.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning funding and oversight for humanitarian assistance, support for refugee relief, and the broader U.S. response to the crisis. It did not authorize or direct a U.S. military intervention in Rwanda during this period.

Crisis 10104 — Lebanon (1983)

ACTION: During the Lebanese civil war, the United States deployed Marines to Beirut as part of a multinational force and used naval gunfire and aircraft to support Lebanese government forces. U.S. combat involvement included sustained exchanges of fire and air and naval strikes, culminating in the October 1983 barracks bombing and the subsequent withdrawal of the Marines in February 1984; U.S. military deaths in the crisis exceeded 100.

TIMELINE:
- 1983-07: Fighting around Beirut intensified, and U.S. forces in the multinational force came under fire while supporting a cease-fire and Lebanese government authority.
- 1983-08: Israeli forces withdrew from the Shuf region; renewed fighting followed, and the United States used naval gunfire and aircraft against positions threatening Lebanese government forces and U.S. personnel.
- 1983-09: U.S. naval and air forces conducted strikes in support of Lebanese government troops during fighting in the Shuf and around Beirut.
- 1983-10-23: A truck bomb destroyed the U.S. Marine barracks at Beirut airport, killing 241 U.S. service members.
- 1983-12: U.S. aircraft and naval forces struck Syrian and Druze positions after attacks on U.S. aircraft and continued threats to the multinational force.
- 1984-02: The United States withdrew its Marines from Beirut as the multinational force disbanded; naval forces remained offshore during the withdrawal.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions over funding and authorizing the continued presence of U.S. Marines in Lebanon under the War Powers Resolution, including the duration of that deployment. After the barracks bombing and worsening security conditions, the question shifted toward terminating the Marine mission and completing the withdrawal.

Crisis 10105 — ISIS (2014)

ACTION: ISIS captured large areas of Iraq and Syria in 2014, including Mosul, and threatened Iraqi and regional security. The United States conducted airstrikes against ISIS in Iraq beginning in August 2014 and expanded operations to Syria in September; U.S. forces also provided intelligence, training, advising, and logistical support, with reported U.S. military deaths during the period.

TIMELINE:
- 2014-06: ISIS seized Mosul and expanded across northern and western Iraq; the United States began assessing options to support the Iraqi government.
- 2014-08: President Obama authorized U.S. airstrikes in Iraq to protect U.S. personnel and prevent ISIS attacks on Erbil, and to support humanitarian operations near Mount Sinjar.
- 2014-09: The administration announced an expanded campaign against ISIS, including airstrikes in Syria and increased support for Iraqi and Kurdish forces.
- 2014-10: The United States established and expanded a multinational coalition campaign against ISIS, while U.S. aircraft continued strikes in Iraq and Syria.
- 2015-01: U.S. and coalition air operations, advising, training, and assistance to Iraqi and Kurdish forces continued as ISIS held territory in Iraq and Syria.
- 2015-06: U.S. forces continued combat operations against ISIS, including airstrikes and support activities, with the administration seeking congressional authorization for the use of military force.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions over funding and authorizing the U.S. campaign against ISIS, including air operations and support for partner forces. It also considered the administration’s request for a specific authorization while existing authorities remained part of the legal framework for operations.

Crisis 10106 — Darfur (2003)

ACTION: Fighting began in Darfur in February 2003 between Sudanese government forces and allied Janjaweed militias, and rebel movements. The United States did not use military force or undertake a direct military intervention in Darfur during this period; its response consisted of diplomatic, humanitarian, and sanctions-related measures.

TIMELINE:
- 2003-02: The Sudan Liberation Army and Justice and Equality Movement launched major attacks in Darfur, beginning the conflict’s escalation.
- 2003-07: The United States began publicly pressing Sudan to halt attacks and permit humanitarian access.
- 2004-07: The United States declared that the Sudanese government and allied militias were responsible for genocidal acts in Darfur; no U.S. military operation was authorized.
- 2004-09: The United States supported a United Nations Security Council resolution imposing measures related to Sudan’s conduct in Darfur and expanding international monitoring.
- 2005-12: Congress enacted the Darfur Peace and Accountability Act, authorizing sanctions and other nonmilitary measures.
- 2007-10: The United States imposed additional targeted sanctions on Sudanese entities and individuals connected to the Darfur conflict.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress considered sanctions, humanitarian assistance, support for peacekeeping and accountability mechanisms, and diplomatic pressure on Sudan. During the period, congressional measures focused on expanding these nonmilitary tools rather than authorizing U.S. military action.

Crisis 10107 — Syria (2018)

ACTION: On April 7, 2018, an alleged chemical-weapons attack occurred in Douma, Syria, during the Syrian government’s campaign to retake Eastern Ghouta. The United States, with the United Kingdom and France, carried out air and missile strikes against three Syrian chemical-weapons-related facilities on April 14; U.S. forces used naval and air-launched missiles, and no U.S. personnel were killed.

TIMELINE:
- 2018-04:07: A chemical-weapons attack was reported in Douma amid Syrian government operations in Eastern Ghouta.
- 2018-04:09–12: President Trump consulted with senior advisers and coordinated with Britain and France while U.S. military options were developed.
- 2018-04:13: Trump announced that the United States, Britain, and France were conducting strikes against Syrian chemical-weapons capabilities.
- 2018-04:14: U.S., British, and French forces struck three Syrian facilities associated with chemical-weapons production, storage, or research.
- 2018-04:16: The administration briefed congressional leaders and described the strikes as limited to deterring and degrading Syria’s chemical-weapons capability.
- 2018-05:14: The period closed with congressional debate over the administration’s legal authority and possible authorization or limitation of future U.S. military operations in Syria.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced questions about the administration’s authority for the April strikes and whether to enact a new authorization, restrict operations, or otherwise define the scope of U.S. military action in Syria. During the period, the immediate issue shifted from whether to respond to the Douma attack to how to govern possible future operations and the administration’s asserted legal basis.

Crisis 10108 — Venezuela (2019)

ACTION: The United States recognized Juan Guaidó as Venezuela’s interim president in January 2019 and imposed extensive sanctions while pursuing diplomatic and political pressure on Nicolás Maduro’s government. U.S. officials, including President Trump, publicly stated that military force remained an option, but the United States did not deploy forces to conduct combat operations or intervene militarily in Venezuela during this period.

TIMELINE:
- 2019-01: Juan Guaidó declared himself interim president; the United States recognized him and urged Maduro to leave office.
- 2019-01: The administration imposed sanctions on Venezuela’s state oil company, PDVSA, and transferred control of certain Venezuelan government assets in the United States to Guaidó’s representatives.
- 2019-02: The United States supported an effort to deliver humanitarian assistance into Venezuela; the attempted crossings at the Colombian and Brazilian borders produced clashes and did not lead to a U.S. military intervention.
- 2019-04: Guaidó launched an uprising with some Venezuelan military personnel; the effort failed, and U.S. officials continued diplomatic, economic, and public pressure on Maduro while retaining military force as a stated option.
- 2019-08: The administration issued an executive order blocking Venezuelan government property under U.S. jurisdiction and authorizing sanctions on persons assisting Maduro’s government.
- 2020-05: An armed maritime incursion involving Venezuelan opposition-linked actors was repelled by Venezuelan authorities; the United States denied directing the operation and did not undertake military action.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning sanctions, recognition and diplomatic support for the Venezuelan opposition, humanitarian assistance, and the scope of any potential authorization or limitation on U.S. military action. Over the period, the immediate policy focus remained on sanctions and diplomatic pressure, while possible military force was publicly retained as an option rather than carried out.

Crisis 10109 — Iran Tankers (2019)

ACTION: Tensions rose between the United States and Iran after Washington intensified sanctions and increased its military presence in the Gulf amid concerns about threats to shipping and U.S. personnel. The United States deployed additional naval, air, and troop assets, conducted surveillance and protective operations, and publicly considered options for protecting commercial shipping, but did not conduct combat strikes against Iran during this period.

TIMELINE:
- 2019-05: The United States announced deployments of additional military assets to the Middle East, including an aircraft carrier strike group, bomber aircraft, and air-defense capabilities, citing threats from Iran.
- 2019-05-12: Explosions damaged four commercial vessels near Fujairah, United Arab Emirates; the United States subsequently attributed the attacks to Iran.
- 2019-06-13: Two tankers were attacked in the Gulf of Oman; the United States released imagery and assessed that Iran was responsible.
- 2019-06-20: Iran shot down a U.S. RQ-4 Global Hawk over the Strait of Hormuz; President Trump stated that U.S. strikes had been considered and then canceled.
- 2019-06-24: The administration announced additional sanctions on Iranian leaders and institutions following the drone incident, while continuing military deployments and maritime security planning.
- 2019-07-18: The USS *Boxer* reported destroying an Iranian drone in the Strait of Hormuz after assessing that it had approached the ship; the United States described the action as defensive, and no broader combat operation followed.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning funding and oversight of the administration’s deployments, sanctions, and plans to protect shipping, as well as whether existing statutory authorities permitted or limited military action against Iran. Over the period, congressional consideration increasingly addressed constraints on any prospective hostilities and the administration’s reporting obligations, while the United States remained at a show-of-force level without initiating combat with Iran.

Crisis 10110 — Iran (2020)

ACTION: In December 2019–January 2020, tensions between the United States and Iran escalated through attacks by Iran-backed forces, U.S. retaliatory strikes, and the killing of Quds Force commander Qasem Soleimani. The United States conducted combat operations, including airstrikes in Iraq and Syria and the January 3 drone strike near Baghdad; no U.S. personnel were killed during the period.

TIMELINE:
- 2019-12: Iran-backed militia attacks in Iraq culminated in a December 27 rocket strike on a base near Kirkuk that killed a U.S. contractor and wounded U.S. personnel.
- 2019-12: On December 29, the United States conducted airstrikes against Kata’ib Hezbollah positions in Iraq and Syria.
- 2019-12: On December 31, protesters and militia supporters breached the U.S. Embassy compound in Baghdad; U.S. Marines reinforced the embassy.
- 2020-01: On January 3, a U.S. drone strike near Baghdad International Airport killed Qasem Soleimani and Iraqi militia commander Abu Mahdi al-Muhandis.
- 2020-01: On January 8, Iran launched ballistic missiles at bases in Iraq hosting U.S. forces; no U.S. personnel were killed, though some later reported traumatic brain injuries.
- 2020-01: On January 13, Congress transmitted the Iran War Powers Resolution to the president, directing termination of unauthorized hostilities against Iran subject to specified exceptions.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions over authorizing, restricting, or terminating U.S. hostilities involving Iran, including the president’s authority to conduct further military action without a new authorization. During January, the House and Senate moved toward measures invoking the War Powers Resolution to limit additional action, while the administration maintained that existing authorities supported its operations.

Crisis 10111 — Taiwan (2021)

ACTION: The United States maintained its longstanding policy of supporting Taiwan’s self-defense and treating any attempt to determine Taiwan’s future by nonpeaceful means as a matter of grave concern. During this period, U.S. officials publicly warned that force against Taiwan would meet a U.S. response, while the United States did not initiate direct combat operations against China or deploy forces to fight over Taiwan.

TIMELINE:
- 2020-09: The United States approved additional arms sales to Taiwan and continued official statements opposing unilateral changes to the Taiwan Strait status quo.
- 2021-04: A bipartisan congressional delegation visited Taiwan, prompting Chinese military activity near the island; U.S. officials continued to state that U.S. commitments under the Taiwan Relations Act supported Taiwan’s defense.
- 2021-10: President Joe Biden stated that the United States had a commitment to defend Taiwan if it were attacked; White House officials subsequently said U.S. policy had not changed.
- 2021-11: Biden and Chinese President Xi Jinping discussed Taiwan in a virtual summit, with the United States reiterating its opposition to unilateral changes in the status quo and China warning against support for Taiwan independence.
- 2022-03: During congressional testimony and public statements, U.S. officials described a Chinese attack on Taiwan as a serious contingency and emphasized continued military assistance and deterrence, without announcing a decision to use force.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions on authorizing and funding arms transfers, military assistance, and broader support for Taiwan, as well as oversight of executive-branch policy under the Taiwan Relations Act. Over the period, congressional attention increasingly included proposals to strengthen deterrence and clarify the consequences of an attack, but Congress did not authorize direct U.S. combat operations against China over Taiwan.

Crisis 10112 — Cuba (2021)

ACTION: Protests began across Cuba on 11 July 2021 amid economic shortages, electricity blackouts, and political grievances. The United States did not undertake military action or publicly place military intervention on the table during this period; its response consisted of diplomatic statements, public messaging, and consideration of targeted sanctions.

TIMELINE:
- 2021-07:11: Demonstrations began in multiple Cuban cities, including San Antonio de los Baños and Havana.
- 2021-07:12: The Biden administration called for the Cuban government to refrain from violence and respect protesters’ rights; President Biden stated that the United States stood with the Cuban people.
- 2021-07:13: The administration continued public calls for internet access and peaceful expression and assessed options for sanctions and humanitarian assistance.
- 2021-07:15: President Biden directed the administration to examine internet access for Cubans and established a Cuba task force addressing remittances, sanctions, and humanitarian support.
- 2021-07:16: The United States announced sanctions on Cuban officials under the Global Magnitsky Act in response to the government’s handling of the protests.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced no decision on authorizing or directing U.S. military action during this period. Its relevant policy questions concerned sanctions, humanitarian assistance, internet access, and the broader continuation or adjustment of U.S. policy toward Cuba.

Crisis 10113 — Ukraine (2022)

ACTION: Russia massed forces around Ukraine and launched a full-scale invasion on 2022-02-24. The United States did not deploy combat forces to fight in Ukraine; it provided military and economic assistance, imposed escalating sanctions and export controls, and considered but did not implement options such as a no-fly zone or direct U.S. military intervention.

TIMELINE:
- 2021-10: The United States began publicly warning that Russia was concentrating forces near Ukraine and shared intelligence about a possible invasion.
- 2021-12: The Biden administration engaged Russia and European allies diplomatically, while preparing sanctions and military-assistance measures in response to further Russian escalation.
- 2022-02: The United States announced additional security assistance for Ukraine and imposed sanctions after Russia recognized separatist entities in eastern Ukraine and deployed forces there.
- 2022-02-24: Russia launched its full-scale invasion; the United States condemned the attack, expanded sanctions and export controls, and continued providing assistance without entering the war directly.
- 2022-03: The United States and partners imposed further sanctions, and the United States announced additional military and humanitarian aid; proposals for direct military involvement, including a no-fly zone, were not implemented.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions on authorizing or appropriating military, economic, and humanitarian assistance for Ukraine and on strengthening sanctions and export controls against Russia. As the crisis progressed from threatened invasion to full-scale war, congressional consideration shifted toward larger aid packages and more extensive economic measures, without authorizing U.S. combat operations in Ukraine.

Crisis 10114 — Yemen (2018)

ACTION: The United States did not deploy combat forces or conduct direct military operations in Yemen during this period. U.S. policy involved continued security assistance and logistical support to the Saudi-led coalition, alongside diplomatic efforts and consideration of measures such as sanctions and restrictions related to the conflict.

TIMELINE:
- 2018-10: The killing of Saudi journalist Jamal Khashoggi intensified congressional scrutiny of U.S. support for Saudi Arabia’s role in the Yemen war.
- 2018-11: The Trump administration announced that it would end U.S. aerial refueling support for Saudi-led coalition aircraft operating in Yemen.
- 2018-12: The Senate passed a joint resolution invoking the War Powers Resolution and calling for an end to unauthorized U.S. military involvement in Yemen; the measure did not become law.
- 2018-12: The Senate also advanced legislation addressing U.S. support for the Saudi-led coalition and the Khashoggi killing, including possible sanctions-related measures.
- 2019-03: The House passed a War Powers Resolution measure directing an end to unauthorized U.S. military involvement in Yemen.
- 2019-04: President Trump vetoed the Yemen War Powers resolution after it passed both chambers of Congress.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress considered whether to terminate or restrict U.S. logistical and security support connected to the Saudi-led coalition’s campaign in Yemen, and whether to impose or require sanctions in response to Saudi conduct.
