Over the period, the issue shifted from Senate action in December 2018 to passage of a broader War Powers resolution by both chambers in early 2019, followed by the presidential veto.

Crisis 10115 — Suriname (1983)

ACTION: Suriname’s military government faced increasing U.S. concern over political repression, ties with Cuba, and reported Soviet-bloc influence after the December 1982 killings of opposition figures. The United States used diplomatic pressure and warnings that it might consider stronger measures, including force-related contingencies, but did not deploy U.S. forces or conduct military operations in Suriname during this period.

TIMELINE:
- 1982-12: Surinamese security forces killed fifteen prominent opponents of the military government in Paramaribo.
- 1983-01: The United States condemned the killings and intensified diplomatic pressure on Suriname.
- 1983-02: Suriname’s relations with the Netherlands deteriorated, while U.S. officials assessed the country’s political alignment and external military ties.
- 1983-03: The United States warned Suriname against further repression and considered possible responses if the situation worsened, including force-related options.
- 1983-05: U.S. policy remained focused on diplomatic pressure and contingency planning; no U.S. military intervention occurred.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced questions about whether and how to respond to Suriname’s repression and perceived alignment with Cuba and the Soviet bloc, including the use of diplomatic or economic measures. During the period, the immediate U.S. response remained executive-branch pressure and threats of possible force rather than a request for authorization of military action.

Crisis 10116 — El Salvador (1980s)

ACTION: El Salvador’s civil war continued between the government and the leftist Farabundo Martí National Liberation Front (FMLN). The United States provided military and economic assistance and deployed U.S. military advisers for training and advisory duties; U.S. personnel were not authorized to conduct combat operations, although Congress and the executive branch addressed the scale and conditions of further assistance.

TIMELINE:
- 1981-01: The Reagan administration entered office as the FMLN intensified its armed campaign; the United States expanded support for the Salvadoran government and maintained a limited advisory presence.
- 1981-12: The United States announced additional military assistance and training for El Salvador, while U.S. policy continued to distinguish advisory activities from direct combat involvement.
- 1982-03: El Salvador held constituent-assembly elections amid the civil war; U.S. assistance and military-advisory programs continued.
- 1983-12: The administration and Congress addressed continued military and economic aid, including reporting and human-rights conditions attached to assistance.
- 1984-05: José Napoleón Duarte became president after Salvadoran elections; U.S. military assistance and advisory support continued under the new government.
- 1984-12: Congress and the administration continued determining funding and conditions for aid to El Salvador, without authorizing U.S. combat deployment.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions over appropriating and conditioning military and economic assistance, requiring executive reporting, and defining limits on U.S. military involvement. Over the period, congressional attention remained focused on sustaining advisory and aid programs while restricting or scrutinizing any expansion into direct U.S. combat operations.

Crisis 10117 — Operation Peace Spring (2019)

ACTION: Turkey launched Operation Peace Spring against Kurdish-led Syrian Democratic Forces (SDF) positions in northeastern Syria after the United States moved personnel away from the anticipated area of operations. The United States did not conduct combat operations in the offensive; it imposed sanctions and pursued diplomatic measures, while some U.S. troops remained elsewhere in Syria to support counter-ISIS missions and protect oil facilities.

TIMELINE:
- 2019-10: President Trump announced the withdrawal of U.S. personnel from parts of northeastern Syria; Turkey began its cross-border operation on October 9.
- 2019-10: Congress considered and advanced measures addressing Turkey’s operation, including sanctions legislation and resolutions concerning the withdrawal of U.S. forces and Turkish actions.
- 2019-10: The United States imposed sanctions on Turkey, including measures against Turkish ministries and senior officials, and suspended negotiations on a trade agreement.
- 2019-10: The United States and Turkey announced a pause in Turkish operations under an October 17 arrangement; the United States subsequently lifted the sanctions after Turkey declared the operation concluded.
- 2019-11: President Trump stated that some U.S. troops would remain in eastern Syria to secure oil fields and continue counter-ISIS activities.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions over sanctions on Turkey, restrictions or conditions on U.S. military involvement, and the administration’s withdrawal of personnel from northeastern Syria. During the period, congressional action focused on sanctions and formal statements or resolutions concerning the withdrawal and Turkey’s operation; Congress did not authorize a U.S. combat intervention.

Crisis 10118 — Afghan Surge (2009)

ACTION: The Obama administration reviewed U.S. strategy in Afghanistan amid deteriorating security conditions and increased Taliban activity. The United States maintained and expanded combat operations, and on December 1 President Obama announced the deployment of 30,000 additional U.S. troops, bringing the planned U.S. force in Afghanistan to roughly 100,000; U.S. fatalities had already exceeded 1,000 by this period.

TIMELINE:
- 2009-09: General Stanley McChrystal submitted a classified assessment calling for additional resources and a revised counterinsurgency strategy.
- 2009-10: The administration continued interagency and military deliberations over troop levels, mission objectives, and the pace of any reinforcement.
- 2009-11: President Obama completed the Afghanistan strategy review and directed preparations for a troop increase.
- 2009-12: President Obama announced the deployment of 30,000 additional U.S. troops and set a July 2011 date to begin a conditions-based transition of responsibility.
- 2009-12: Congress considered and funded the administration’s Afghanistan and broader overseas war requirements, including resources for the expanded deployment.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions on authorizing and funding continued combat operations, including the additional personnel, procurement, and support required by the troop increase. Over the period, the central congressional question shifted from sustaining the existing war effort to providing resources for the expanded counterinsurgency campaign and its planned transition framework.

Crisis 10119 — Iraq Surge (2006)

ACTION: In late 2006, violence and sectarian conflict in Iraq continued while the United States maintained a large combat deployment and conducted counterinsurgency operations. During the period, President George W. Bush reviewed options for increasing U.S. forces; the resulting “surge” decision was announced in January 2007 and began deploying additional troops, rather than involving a new war or direct U.S. military action against another state.

TIMELINE:
- 2006-10: The Iraq Study Group began reviewing U.S. policy and military strategy in Iraq.
- 2006-11: The Baker-Hamilton Iraq Study Group completed its report, recommending changes in U.S. political and military policy.
- 2006-12: President Bush directed a review of Iraq strategy, including options for adding U.S. forces.
- 2007-01: Bush announced a new Iraq strategy involving approximately 21,500 additional U.S. troops, primarily to Baghdad and Anbar Province.
- 2007-02: Additional U.S. forces began arriving in Iraq as part of the surge deployment.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions over funding and authorizing the continuing U.S. military operation, including the deployment of additional forces. During the period, congressional consideration shifted from the existing deployment toward measures addressing or challenging the proposed troop increase, without immediately terminating the combat mission.

Crisis 10120 — Nicaragua (1980s)

ACTION: The United States treated Nicaragua’s Sandinista government as a regional security concern and used diplomatic pressure, economic measures, intelligence support, and assistance to the Contra opposition. U.S. forces did not conduct combat operations in Nicaragua; military options, including expanded regional deployments and possible direct action, were considered at various points but did not escalate to U.S. combat.

TIMELINE:
- 1981-01: The Reagan administration took office and reviewed policy toward Nicaragua, including allegations of Sandinista support for insurgencies in neighboring countries.
- 1981-12: The United States authorized covert assistance to Nicaraguan armed opposition groups, while maintaining pressure on the Sandinista government.
- 1983-03: U.S. forces participated in the multinational exercise Big Pine II in and around Honduras, demonstrating regional military capability without combat in Nicaragua.
- 1984-04: The mining of Nicaraguan ports by U.S.-supported covert operations became public, prompting congressional investigation and legal controversy.
- 1986-06: Congress enacted the Boland Amendment restrictions within the fiscal-year appropriations process, limiting specified U.S. support for Contra operations.
- 1987-08: The Central American peace process produced the Esquipulas II accord, shifting U.S. policy debate toward negotiations, elections, and regional compliance while Contra assistance remained a congressional issue.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions over appropriating, restricting, or conditioning funds and authorities for covert assistance to the Contras, as well as oversight of executive operations. By the later 1980s, congressional consideration increasingly included the relationship between further assistance, the Central American peace process, and negotiated political settlements.

Crisis 10121 — Vietnam Escalation (1965)

ACTION: In June–July 1965, the United States expanded its direct combat role in South Vietnam as Viet Cong and North Vietnamese pressure intensified. U.S. forces conducted combat operations, including major ground engagements, and Washington actively weighed further troop increases; U.S. deaths had surpassed 1,000 by the end of July.

TIMELINE:
- 1965-06: The United States approved deployment of additional combat forces to South Vietnam and expanded their authority to conduct offensive operations.
- 1965-06-28: U.S. Marines and South Vietnamese forces began Operation Starlite, a major search-and-destroy operation against Viet Cong forces near Chu Lai.
- 1965-07-01: President Lyndon B. Johnson authorized an increase in the U.S. troop ceiling in South Vietnam to 125,000.
- 1965-07-22: Johnson publicly announced the troop increase and stated that additional forces could be sent if required.
- 1965-07-28: Johnson ordered the deployment of additional U.S. ground troops, bringing the planned U.S. force level in Vietnam toward approximately 175,000.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions on appropriating funds and authorizing resources for the expanding U.S. military presence in Vietnam. During the period, the administration moved from requesting support for an existing advisory and limited-combat role toward funding and sustaining a substantially larger U.S. ground-combat deployment.

Crisis 10122 — Vietnam (1967)

ACTION: In February–March 1967, the United States continued large-scale combat operations in Vietnam, including ground operations and sustained air activity against North Vietnam and Viet Cong targets. U.S. policy centered on conducting and adjusting ongoing military operations rather than introducing a new level of intervention during this period.

TIMELINE:
- 1967-02: U.S. and South Vietnamese forces conducted Operation Junction City, a major ground operation against Viet Cong forces near the Cambodian border.
- 1967-02: The United States continued Operation Rolling Thunder air strikes against North Vietnam.
- 1967-02-14: Operation Junction City began in the III Corps area of South Vietnam.
- 1967-02-22: U.S. and South Vietnamese forces made a major airborne assault during Operation Junction City.
- 1967-03-04: Operation Junction City concluded after sustained U.S. and South Vietnamese ground operations.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning appropriations and oversight for continuing U.S. combat operations in Vietnam, including ground forces, air operations, and related military expenditures. During this period, the central congressional issue remained authorization and funding of ongoing combat, rather than a decision to initiate U.S. involvement.

Crisis 10123 — Post-Paris Enforcement (1973)

ACTION: After the January 1973 Paris Peace Accords, fighting continued in Vietnam, while U.S. forces conducted reconnaissance and maintained a military presence in the region. The United States did not resume combat operations in Vietnam; it carried out a limited show of force in Cambodia before ending U.S. air operations there on August 15.

TIMELINE:
- 1973-04: U.S. reconnaissance and logistical activities continued after the Paris cease-fire, while fighting persisted in South Vietnam.
- 1973-06: Congress enacted restrictions on U.S. military operations in Indochina, including a prohibition on funding combat activities in Cambodia after August 15.
- 1973-07: The Nixon administration continued air operations against communist positions in Cambodia while seeking additional time to negotiate an end to the fighting.
- 1973-08-15: The United States ended its bombing and other air operations in Cambodia, completing the post-Paris U.S. combat withdrawal from Indochina.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions over whether to continue funding or prohibit U.S. military operations in Cambodia and elsewhere in Indochina after the Paris Accords. During the period, legislation shifted U.S. policy toward terminating funding for combat activities in Cambodia, with August 15 set as the cutoff date.

Crisis 10124 — Tanker War (1987)

ACTION: During the Iran–Iraq Tanker War, attacks on commercial shipping in the Persian Gulf led Kuwait to request U.S. protection for its tankers. The United States reflagged selected Kuwaiti vessels and initiated Operation Earnest Will, using naval escorts and other forces to protect them; U.S. forces also engaged in limited combat, including the July 1987 Bridgeton incident involving an Iranian mine, without U.S. fatalities during this period.

TIMELINE:
- 1987-05: Kuwait formally requests U.S. protection for its oil tankers amid escalating attacks on Gulf shipping.
- 1987-06: The Reagan administration approves plans to reflag and escort Kuwaiti tankers under U.S. protection.
- 1987-07: The first reflagged Kuwaiti tanker convoy operates under U.S. Navy escort; the Bridgeton strikes an Iranian mine while leading the convoy.
- 1987-07: The United States begins expanded naval and air surveillance and mine-countermeasure preparations in the Gulf.
- 1987-09: The United States captures the Iranian vessel *Iran Ajr* after it is observed laying mines in international waters.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions over funding and authorizing the naval deployment, reflagging arrangements, and associated protection of Kuwaiti tankers. As the operation progressed, congressional attention shifted from whether to undertake the escort mission to oversight of the continuing U.S. military presence and limited combat operations in the Gulf.

Crisis 10125 — Cuba Ship Arms El Salvador (1981)

ACTION: In July 1981, the Reagan administration accused Cuba of supplying arms to leftist insurgents in El Salvador, while Havana denied the charge. The United States used diplomatic warnings and military signaling, including heightened regional surveillance and a show of force, but did not conduct combat operations or attack Cuba.

TIMELINE:
- 1981-07-17: The administration publicly focused attention on alleged Cuban support for arms shipments to Salvadoran insurgents.
- 1981-07-20: U.S. officials pursued diplomatic and intelligence measures concerning Cuba’s alleged role in the Salvadoran conflict.
- 1981-07-24: The United States increased military signaling in the Caribbean and Central America while continuing to assess alleged arms movements.
- 1981-07-30: The period ended with the United States at a show-of-force level, without combat against Cuba or direct intervention in the arms-shipment dispute.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced questions about the administration’s proposed response to alleged Cuban involvement, including the use of diplomatic pressure, intelligence resources, and regional military signaling. During the period, the operative response remained below combat and did not require Congress to authorize hostilities against Cuba.

Crisis 10126 — US Croatia Show Of Force (2000)

ACTION: The United States conducted a non-combat show of force in response to the September 2000 political crisis in Croatia, involving visible U.S. military presence and readiness measures. U.S. forces did not conduct strikes, enter hostilities, or undertake combat operations; the escalation remained at the show-of-force level.

TIMELINE:
- 2000-09-26: Political crisis and demonstrations intensified in Croatia following the disputed presidential election process.
- 2000-09-27: The United States increased the visibility of its military presence in and around Croatia as a deterrent measure.
- 2000-09-28: U.S. officials continued monitoring the crisis while maintaining non-combat force-readiness and signaling measures.
- 2000-09-29: The U.S. show of force concluded without combat action or direct U.S. military intervention.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced oversight of the administration’s deployment and readiness measures, rather than a decision to authorize combat. As the episode remained limited to a show of force, no congressional decision to authorize hostilities was required.

Crisis 10127 — Russian Alert During Kosovo Bombings (1999)

ACTION: During NATO’s air campaign against the Federal Republic of Yugoslavia, Russia placed some forces on heightened alert and objected diplomatically to the operation. The United States did not take military action against Russia and did not escalate the crisis beyond continuing the existing NATO campaign against Yugoslavia.

TIMELINE:
- 1999-03-24: NATO began air operations against the Federal Republic of Yugoslavia over Kosovo.
- 1999-04-08: Russia reported heightened readiness measures in response to the NATO campaign and intensified diplomatic objections.
- 1999-04-09: The United States and NATO continued the air campaign while maintaining diplomatic contacts with Moscow.
- 1999-04-14: NATO operations continued; U.S. policy remained focused on Yugoslavia and Kosovo rather than military action against Russia.
- 1999-04-19: The period ended without a U.S. military response to Russia’s alert measures.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning authorization, funding, and oversight of the ongoing NATO operation against Yugoslavia, while developments involving Russia raised questions about executive-branch policy and crisis management. No separate congressional authorization for military action against Russia was under consideration during this period.

Crisis 10128 — US-USSR Tensions During Iran Rev (1978)

ACTION: During the Iranian Revolution, escalating unrest and the collapse of the Shah’s authority raised U.S. concerns about Iran’s stability and possible Soviet advantage. The United States issued warnings and considered military contingencies, but during this period it did not carry out military action or deploy forces to intervene in Iran.

TIMELINE:
- 1978-11: Intensifying protests and strikes further weakened the Shah’s government, prompting U.S. consideration of options for protecting American personnel and interests.
- 1978-12: The United States assessed the possibility that instability in Iran could benefit the Soviet Union and examined stronger warnings and contingency plans.
- 1978-12-31: Shah Mohammad Reza Pahlavi appointed Shapour Bakhtiar as prime minister, while negotiations continued over the Shah’s departure and the political transition.
- 1979-01-02: The United States continued diplomatic pressure and contingency planning amid the unresolved succession crisis; no U.S. military operation had begun.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning authorization or support for possible protective or interventionary measures if the crisis threatened U.S. citizens, facilities, or regional security. During the period, the immediate issue remained contingency planning and the possible use of force rather than a request to approve an active military operation.

Crisis 10129 — Soviet Brigade In Cuba (1979)

ACTION: A Soviet military brigade in Cuba was publicly identified in 1979, prompting U.S. diplomatic pressure and demands for clarification and removal. The United States considered military-related responses, including possible measures to signal resolve, but did not use force or initiate military operations; its response remained at the level of threats of force.

TIMELINE:
- 1979-08: U.S. officials confirmed the presence of a Soviet combat brigade in Cuba.
- 1979-08-31: The administration publicly disclosed the brigade’s presence and began consultations with Congress and allies.
- 1979-09: The United States sought assurances concerning the brigade and examined possible responses, including military signaling.
- 1979-10: Diplomatic efforts continued over the brigade’s status and mission, while the administration maintained pressure on Cuba and the Soviet Union.
- 1979-11-20: The crisis period ended without U.S. military action; the brigade remained in Cuba, with the issue addressed through diplomatic understandings.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the question of how to respond to the Soviet brigade’s presence, including whether to support diplomatic demands and authorize or otherwise accommodate possible military measures. Over the period, the immediate decision shifted from assessing the disclosure and possible force-related options toward managing the issue through diplomatic assurances and continued monitoring.

Crisis 10130 — GHWB Assasination Attempt (1993)

ACTION: Following an attempted assassination of former President George H. W. Bush during his planned visit to Kuwait, the United States attributed the operation to Iraqi intelligence. On June 26, U.S. forces conducted cruise-missile strikes against the Iraqi Intelligence Service headquarters in Baghdad; the operation involved combat but no U.S. deaths.

TIMELINE:
- 1993-04: Iraqi operatives were arrested in Kuwait in connection with a plot to assassinate former President Bush.
- 1993-06-25: The United States publicly announced that Iraq was responsible for the assassination attempt.
- 1993-06-26: President Clinton ordered and U.S. forces carried out cruise-missile strikes against the Iraqi Intelligence Service headquarters in Baghdad.
- 1993-06-27: The administration briefed Congress on the strikes and their basis in the attempted assassination.
- 1993-06-28: The immediate U.S. military action had concluded; no further U.S. combat operation against Iraq occurred during this period.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the administration’s decision to use military force against Iraq in response to the attempted assassination, including receiving the constitutional and factual justification for the strikes. After the attack, congressional consideration centered on the operation’s scope, legal basis, and the administration’s continuing policy toward Iraq.

Crisis 10131 — Syria-Turkey Tensions (1996)

ACTION: Syria–Turkey tensions in June 1996 centered on Syrian support for the Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK), Turkish security concerns, and disputes involving water resources. The United States took no military action and did not actively weigh a military response during this period.

TIMELINE:
- 1996-06: Turkish officials continued raising concerns about Syrian-based PKK activity and Syria’s alleged support for the organization.
- 1996-06: Syria–Turkey tensions remained linked to disputes over the Euphrates waters and broader regional security issues.
- 1996-06: The United States monitored the bilateral tensions through normal diplomatic and regional-security channels.
- 1996-06: No U.S. military deployment, strike, coercive operation, or publicly identified military contingency was undertaken or actively considered.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced no identified decision on authorizing or directing U.S. military action in response to the Syria–Turkey tensions during June 1996. The U.S. posture remained nonmilitary throughout the period.

Crisis 10132 — 7th Fleet Taiwan Strait (1950)

ACTION: Following North Korea’s invasion of South Korea, President Harry Truman ordered the U.S. Seventh Fleet into the Taiwan Strait on June 27, 1950. The mission was to prevent attacks on Taiwan and to deter Chinese Nationalist and Communist forces from using the crisis to alter the island’s status; the United States did not conduct combat operations against China or attempt to occupy mainland territory.

TIMELINE:
- 1950-06-25: North Korea invaded South Korea, prompting an emergency U.S. and United Nations response.
- 1950-06-27: President Truman ordered the Seventh Fleet to the Taiwan Strait and directed that the status of Taiwan be left to later determination.
- 1950-06-28: Truman publicly announced the deployment and stated that the fleet would prevent attacks on Taiwan while also discouraging Chinese Nationalist attacks on the mainland.
- 1950-07: U.S. naval forces maintained patrols in the Taiwan Strait, while the administration pursued broader measures to support South Korea and contain the conflict.
- 1950-08-07: The period closed with the Seventh Fleet’s protective deployment continuing; no U.S. attack on Chinese forces or landing on Taiwan had occurred.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced the broader question of financing and sustaining the administration’s military response to the Korean War, including the naval deployment associated with Taiwan. It did not make a separate authorization decision for combat against China or for a U.S. occupation of Taiwan during this period.

Crisis 10133 — Taiwan Raids (1951)

ACTION: During 1951–1952, cross-Strait raids and related tensions involving Taiwan and the Chinese mainland occurred amid the Korean War, but the United States did not undertake a military response to the Taiwan raids. U.S. policy remained focused on maintaining the defense of Taiwan through existing arrangements rather than escalating directly in response to these incidents.

TIMELINE:
- 1951-02: The Taiwan Strait remained a potential flashpoint as the Korean War continued and the Nationalist government operated from Taiwan.
- 1951-06: U.S. officials continued assessing the security of Taiwan and the surrounding waters without initiating military action in response to raids.
- 1952-02: The United States maintained its existing regional military posture; no response to the Taiwan raids was ordered.
- 1952-10: The period ended without U.S. retaliatory strikes, new military operations, or other direct escalation specifically tied to the raids.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced oversight and authorization questions concerning broader U.S. defense commitments in East Asia, including Taiwan, rather than a specific request to authorize military action against the raiders. Over the period, the concrete U.S. decision remained whether to maintain existing policy and preparedness; no congressional decision produced a direct U.S. response to the raids.

Crisis 10134 — EP-3 Incident (2001)

ACTION: On 2001-04-01, a U.S. Navy EP-3E reconnaissance aircraft collided with a Chinese F-8 fighter over the South China Sea and made an emergency landing on Hainan Island. The United States did not undertake military retaliation or other coercive military action; its response consisted of diplomatic efforts to secure the crew’s release and return, while surveillance operations continued in the region.

TIMELINE:
- 2001-04-01: U.S. EP-3E aircraft collides with a Chinese fighter and lands on Hainan Island; the 24-person crew is detained and the aircraft is held by Chinese authorities.
- 2001-04-02: President George W. Bush demands the crew’s prompt return and directs diplomatic engagement with China; U.S. officials begin efforts to obtain access to the crew.
- 2001-04-03: China states that the aircraft entered its exclusive economic zone and requests a formal U.S. apology; the United States maintains that the EP-3 was operating in international airspace.
- 2001-04-11: Following diplomatic negotiations, China releases the U.S. crew, who depart Hainan for the United States.
- 2001-07-03: The United States receives the disassembled EP-3 aircraft from China and returns it to U.S. custody; no U.S. military retaliation has occurred.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning oversight of the administration’s handling of the detained crew and aircraft, U.S. reconnaissance operations near China, and broader U.S.-China security policy. After the crew’s release, the immediate congressional issue shifted from securing their return to assessing the aircraft’s recovery and the implications for future surveillance operations.

Crisis 10135 — 1999Taiwan (1999)

ACTION: Taiwan President Lee Teng-hui’s July 1999 description of cross-Strait relations as “special state-to-state” relations triggered heightened concern in Beijing and Washington. The United States took no military action and did not publicly commit forces beyond existing regional deployments; it continued to state that cross-Strait differences should be resolved peacefully.

TIMELINE:
- 1999-07: President Lee Teng-hui characterized Taiwan–mainland relations as “special state-to-state” relations, prompting a cross-Strait crisis.
- 1999-07: Beijing condemned the formulation and warned against steps toward Taiwan independence; Washington urged restraint and opposed unilateral changes to the status quo.
- 1999-08: The Clinton administration reaffirmed the U.S. “one China” policy and indicated that the Taiwan Relations Act remained the framework for unofficial U.S.–Taiwan relations.
- 1999-09: Taiwan and the United States continued discussions on Taiwan’s defensive requirements, without a U.S. decision to employ military force.
- 2000-03: Taiwan held its presidential election; Chen Shui-bian won, and Washington again called for peaceful handling of cross-Strait relations.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning the framework for unofficial U.S.–Taiwan relations and possible arms transfers under the Taiwan Relations Act, rather than authorization for a U.S. military response. During the period, the central congressional issue remained oversight of Taiwan policy and defense support; no authorization for direct U.S. intervention was enacted.

Crisis 10136 — Chinese Threats To Taiwan (2001)

ACTION: China issued warnings and military threats concerning Taiwan, while the United States addressed the issue through diplomatic statements, arms-sale decisions, and deterrence signaling. During this period, the United States did not use force against China or Taiwan and did not move beyond threats of force in the crisis.

TIMELINE:
- 2001-04: The United States approved a major package of defensive arms for Taiwan, including submarines, patrol aircraft, and other systems, while withholding a decision on certain additional weapons.
- 2001-04-25: President George W. Bush stated that the United States would do “whatever it took” to help Taiwan defend itself if attacked, while also reaffirming the U.S. “one China” policy.
- 2001-05: U.S. and Chinese officials continued exchanges over Taiwan, arms sales, and the implications of U.S. security commitments.
- 2001-07: China continued to warn that Taiwan-related developments could lead to conflict and pressed Washington to limit military support for Taipei.
- 2001-08-17: The administration announced that it would not approve Taiwan’s request for four Aegis-equipped destroyers at that time, while permitting or considering other defensive capabilities.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning authorization, funding, and oversight of arms transfers and broader U.S. security policy toward Taiwan. Over the period, the immediate issue shifted from responding to China’s threats to evaluating the scope and composition of U.S. defense assistance to Taiwan.

Appendix B — Quorum_crises_wide.csv (Quorum × crises)

The same crisis design applied to all public lawmaker output. The instrument is the CR one with three changes and nothing else: the OCR/masking conventions are removed (Quorum text is clean and names the country in plain text); the 58%-irrelevant prior is neutralised to a plain relevance test (this feed is already on-topic); and the input framing accepts any register from a committee transcript to a tweet, with an instruction to use lower confidence rather than force a strong label when a short post gives thin signal.

Context injected per statement: the same per-crisis ACTION/TIMELINE block as Appendix A, plus the statement's document type and the crisis date window.

B.1 Tripartite system prompt

You are a nonpartisan congressional analyst coding U.S. lawmakers' public statements for a political science project on war powers. Judge only the text; never guess partisanship.

You are shown ONE congressional statement (it may be a floor speech, press release, committee remark, newsletter, letter, or social-media post), the international crisis it concerns, and the dates that crisis spans. Assign EXACTLY ONE overall label for the speaker's position on the use of American military force IN THAT CRISIS: support, oppose, mixed, or irrelevant.

support = LEANS TOWARD SUPPORTING U.S. military force in this crisis
- Explicitly endorses strikes, intervention, or deployment; praises force taken or threatened
- Hawkish framing ("we must act," "all options on the table," "show strength") -- in an active crisis these imply force even without the word
- Frames the adversary as an urgent/intolerable threat requiring a forceful response
- Criticizes restraint, caution, or withdrawal as weakness or appeasement
- Emphasizes the adversary's repression/atrocities/proliferation to justify a harder U.S. posture
- Presses for a "strong"/"maximum pressure" deal, warns against a "weak deal," or urges keeping military options on the table

oppose = LEANS TOWARD OPPOSING U.S. military force in this crisis
- Explicitly opposes or warns against strikes, intervention, or deployment
- Calls for diplomacy, restraint, or de-escalation instead of force
- Raises the costs of force as a reason NOT to act: casualties, expense, quagmire, war-weariness, wider war
- Argues escalation would be counterproductive, or would wreck a negotiated settlement
- Invokes a cautionary precedent (Vietnam, Iraq, "another endless war") against force
- Argues the crisis is not a vital U.S. interest, or urges sanctions/aid/pressure INSTEAD OF force

mixed = genuinely BOTH support and opposition
- Backs one instrument but rejects another (e.g., supports air strikes but firmly opposes ground troops)
- Endorses the action but raises serious SUBSTANTIVE objections; or supports force only on conditions
- Cannot reasonably be placed in support or oppose

irrelevant = takes NO position on U.S. military force in this crisis
- Procedural, ceremonial, or a different topic; the crisis appears only incidentally

THE MOST COMMON ERROR IS CODING THE WRONG WAR. These statements were retrieved by keyword match, and crises in this project overlap in time and actors, so the crisis country turns up constantly inside statements really about a DIFFERENT conflict. A statement that argues for withdrawing from one conflict and mentions this crisis's country once expresses no position on American force in THIS crisis: it is irrelevant. Hawkishness or dovishness about any OTHER conflict is not a position on THIS crisis, however forcefully expressed. Use the crisis description and dates to stay anchored on the crisis named below.

RELEVANCE. Because statements were pulled by keyword match, many hits are incidental -- the crisis country or topic appears in a list, in passing, or inside a statement that is really about trade, human rights, domestic politics, or another conflict. Decide whether the speaker actually takes a position on U.S. military force in THIS crisis; if they do not, return irrelevant. Do not assume relevance, and do not manufacture a stance -- but do not force irrelevant when a genuine position is present.

DO NOT MISTAKE THESE FOR OPPOSITION:
- A demand for congressional authorization, or neutral procedural discussion of War Powers compliance, is NOT opposition unless the speaker voices a substantive objection to the force itself. Process talk alone is not opposition.
- An otherwise-supportive statement that merely acknowledges costs, risks, or difficulty is still support -- do not downgrade it for a passing caveat.
- Opposing a specific amendment, substitute, or procedural motion is not opposition to the underlying use of force.

TWO CARVE-OUTS:
- SANCTIONS HAWK: pressure or maximalist-deal rhetoric COMBINED WITH explicit opposition to military force is oppose, not support. Hawkishness about sanctions is not hawkishness about force.
- REGIME DELEGITIMIZATION: denouncing the adversary regime is support only where no explicit rejection of force accompanies it; denunciation plus "but war is not the answer" is oppose.

ATTRIBUTION: code the speaker's OWN position, not a view they quote or paraphrase in order to rebut it. SARCASM: read rhetorical questions for intended meaning ("Shall we send another generation to die in the desert?" is oppose).

REGISTER AND LENGTH: these statements range from brief social-media posts to long committee transcripts. Apply the same rubric regardless of length. When a short post gives only thin signal, use a LOWER confidence rather than forcing a strong label. The crisis country and actors are named in plain text -- there is no redaction.

Return ONLY a JSON object with: `classification` (exactly one of: support, oppose, mixed, irrelevant); `key_quote` (the shortest verbatim phrase justifying it, "" if irrelevant); `confidence` (integer 1-5: 5 = clear-cut, 3 = leaning but real ambiguity, 1 = genuinely borderline). No prose, no markdown.

B.2 8-item system prompt

You are a congressional analyst coding U.S. lawmakers' public statements for a political science project on war powers. You apply a fixed codebook exactly as written.

You will be shown one congressional statement (it may be a floor speech, press release, committee remark, newsletter, letter, or social-media post), the international crisis it concerns, and the dates that crisis spans. Record the speaker's expressed policy preferences about the use of American military force IN THAT CRISIS, as eight INDEPENDENT binary items.

The items are not mutually exclusive and do not sum to anything. A speaker who wants air strikes but no ground troops scores 1 on air support AND 1 on ground opposition. Code each item on its own; never force a single overall verdict.

THE MOST COMMON ERROR IS CODING THE WRONG WAR. These statements were retrieved by keyword match, and crises in this project overlap in time and actors, so the crisis country turns up constantly inside statements really about a DIFFERENT conflict. A 2006 floor speech that argues for 900 words about withdrawing from Iraq, and mentions Syria once as a country worth talking to, expresses no position whatever on American force against Syria: it is irrelevant. Hawkishness or dovishness about Iraq, Vietnam, Afghanistan, Korea, or any other conflict is NOT hawkishness or dovishness about this crisis, however forcefully expressed. Before scoring any item, ask: is the speaker expressing a view about American force directed at THIS crisis, or at some other one? Reserve every item for force committed to the named crisis.

RELEVANCE GATE -- READ THIS SECOND. A statement is relevant only if at least one of the eight items can be answered yes. Because statements were pulled by keyword match, many hits are incidental -- the crisis country or topic appears in a list, in passing, or inside a statement really about trade, human rights, domestic politics, or another conflict. Decide whether the speaker actually takes a position on U.S. military force in THIS crisis; if they do not, relevant is false. Do not assume relevance, and do not manufacture a stance to avoid returning one -- but do not force irrelevant when a genuine position is present. If relevant is false, set all eight items to 0.

The speaker's name and party are withheld from you deliberately. Code the text. Do not guess at partisanship and do not let an inferred party lean the classification.

WHAT COUNTS AS SUPPORTING FORCE (general support item):
- Endorses strikes, intervention, or deployment; praises a strike already carried out
- Hawkish framing: "all options on the table," "show strength," "we must act," "cannot
  stand by" -- in an active crisis these imply force even without the word
- Frames the adversary as an urgent or intolerable threat: "must be stopped," "cannot be
  tolerated"
- Criticizes restraint, caution, or withdrawal as weakness, appeasement, or dithering
- Urges the President to act more forcefully, or faults him for acting too slowly
- Emphasizes the adversary's repression, executions, terrorism, or proliferation to
  justify a harder American posture
- Frames the regime as fundamentally illegitimate or untrustworthy
- Presses for a "strong," "real," or "maximum pressure" deal on stringent terms
- Warns against a "weak deal" or premature sanctions relief
- Calls for keeping military options on the table during negotiations

WHAT COUNTS AS OPPOSING FORCE (general opposition item):
- Opposes or warns against strikes, intervention, or deployment
- Calls for diplomacy, negotiation, restraint, or de-escalation instead of force
- Raises costs: casualties, expense, quagmire, war-weariness, burden on troops
- Argues escalation would be counterproductive or self-defeating
- Argues force would wreck a negotiated settlement or diplomatic opening
- Invokes a cautionary precedent (Vietnam, Iraq, "another endless war")
- Substantively opposes force, OR withholds support UNLESS Congress authorizes it because
  the speaker objects to unilateral force. But NEUTRAL procedural or legislative-history
  discussion of War Powers compliance -- noting the 60-day clock, confirming a resolution
  is consistent with a statutory authorization requirement, reporting/timeline mechanics --
  is NOT opposition when the speaker voices no substantive objection to the force itself.
  Do not code an opposition item for process talk alone.
- Argues the crisis is not a vital American interest, or is someone else's to handle
- Urges sanctions, aid, or pressure INSTEAD OF military force
- Warns of unintended consequences, regional spillover, or civilian harm

TWO CARVE-OUTS, BOTH IMPORTANT:
- SANCTIONS HAWK. Pressure rhetoric and maximalist deal terms COMBINED WITH explicit
  opposition to military force is opposition, not support. Hawkishness about sanctions is
  not hawkishness about force. Score general opposition, not general support.
- REGIME DELEGITIMIZATION. Denouncing the adversary regime is support only where no
  explicit rejection of force accompanies it. Denunciation plus "but war is not the
  answer" is opposition.

BRANCH-SPECIFIC ITEMS. Score ground, air, and naval only where the statement engages that
instrument specifically -- troops/boots on the ground/invasion/occupation; air strikes/
bombing/no-fly zone/standoff missiles; carriers/naval blockade/escorting/fleet movements.
General hawkishness with no branch named scores the general item ONLY, leaving all three
branch items at 0. Do not infer a branch from the nature of the crisis.

ATTRIBUTION AND TONE:
- Code the speaker's own position, not one they are quoting, describing, or attributing to
  an opponent, the President, or a foreign government. Speeches that recite an opposing
  view in order to rebut it are common; the rebuttal is the speaker's position.
- Read sarcasm and rhetorical questions for intent, not surface form. "Shall we send
  another generation to die in the desert?" is opposition.
- Conditional support ("I would support strikes if the President came to Congress") scores
  BOTH the relevant support item and general opposition, since it is support conditioned
  on authorization.
- Retrospective criticism of a past war is not, by itself, a position on this crisis.
- Do not add an opposition item to an otherwise-supportive statement merely for a passing mention
  of costs, risks, troop readiness, or conditions. Code an opposition item only where the speaker
  actually argues against using force in this crisis, not where a supporter acknowledges difficulty.
- Do not use hindsight about what actually happened afterward. Code what is on the page.

REGISTER AND LENGTH. These statements range from brief social-media posts to long committee transcripts. Apply the same codebook regardless of length. When a short post gives only thin signal, use a LOWER confidence rather than forcing strong items. The crisis country and actors are named in plain text -- there is no redaction.

PARLIAMENTARY VEHICLES. Opposing a specific amendment, substitute, or procedural motion is not opposition to the underlying use of force. Code the member's stance on force itself, not on the vehicle.

WORKED EXAMPLES (apply the same logic):
1. "I confirm this resolution is consistent with our requirement that the President obtain statutory authorization for any expanded role of our forces in the crisis country; the 60-day War Powers clock is noted." -> Neutral War Powers procedure, no objection to the force. relevant=false; code NO opposition item.
2. "I strongly support the President's decision to strike the crisis country, though our forces are stretched thin and this will be costly." -> A supporter noting difficulty. GenSupp=1 only; do NOT add an opposition item for the passing cost/readiness caveat.
3. "I oppose the gentleman's amendment, which ties the President's hands. The underlying resolution authorizing air strikes deserves support." -> Opposes an amendment but backs the force. GenSupp=1, AirSupp=1; the amendment opposition is NOT opposition to force.
4. "We must not send another generation to die in the desert. Diplomacy, not bombs." -> Genuine opposition. GenOpp=1.

Return ONLY a JSON object matching the schema: `relevant`, the eight items, and `key_quote` -- the single shortest verbatim phrase from the statement that best justifies your non-zero items ("" if irrelevant or all 0). Also return `confidence` (integer 1-5): how confident you are that these labels correctly capture the speaker's stance on U.S. military force in THIS crisis (5 = clear-cut and unambiguous; 3 = leaning but with real ambiguity; 1 = genuinely borderline, could reasonably be coded otherwise). No prose, no markdown, no explanation.

B.3 Rendered example user message (tripartite, with injected context)

CRISIS: Iraq (2003)  (U.S. debate spanning 2002-09-12 to 2003-05-01).
This statement's date: 2002-10-02.  Document type: Floor Statement.

WHAT THE UNITED STATES WAS ACTUALLY CONSIDERING/DOING IN THIS CRISIS (use the statement's date together with the timeline below to determine what action was under debate AT THE TIME OF THIS STATEMENT, and judge the speaker's support for THAT action):
ACTION: From September 2002 to May 2003, the United States and its partners pressured Iraq over weapons inspections and compliance with UN Resolution 1441, while preparing for possible military action and pursuing diplomatic support. The United States ultimately conducted combat operations that escalated to total war and the fall of Saddam Hussein’s regime; the stated escalation level records combat with more than 1,000 U.S. deaths.

TIMELINE:
- 2002-09: President Bush addressed the UN General Assembly, calling for strong multilateral action if Iraq did not eliminate its weapons and weapons programs.
- 2002-11: The UN Security Council unanimously adopted Resolution 1441, establishing inspection conditions and warning of “the severest consequences” for noncompliance.
- 2002-11: UN weapons inspectors returned to Iraq.
- 2002-12: Iraq submitted a 12,000-page weapons declaration; the United States subsequently declared Iraq in material breach of Resolution 1441.
- 2003-05: The crisis ended with total war and the fall of Saddam Hussein’s regime.

BEFORE CONGRESS: Congress faced decisions concerning U.S. action against Iraq, including whether to authorize or permit military operations as the administration moved from inspections and diplomatic efforts toward combat. Over the period, the decision context shifted from pressure for Iraqi compliance and pursuit of a peaceful solution to active consideration and execution of war.

Classify this speaker's overall position on American military force in THIS crisis as exactly ONE of: support, oppose, mixed, irrelevant.

STATEMENT:
[STATEMENT TEXT]
...[truncated]

Appendix C — CR_wars_wide.csv (Congressional Record × wars)

Seven wars, each with its own instrument. All seven share the crisis codebook's core — the tripartite prompts derive from Appendix A's, and every 8-item prompt is quorum_8item.SYSTEM_8Q byte-for-byte with an additive war block appended — so the war and crisis labels remain comparable. Three things are war-specific:

  1. The referent. With no crisis window, support and opposition are defined relative to the policy in force on the statement's date. After 1969, opposing a withdrawal deadline is support; in 1954, urging aid to the French instead of intervention is opposition.
  2. The timeline. A dated chronology of U.S. action, injected into every user message.
  3. Per-war carve-outs for that conflict's characteristic traps, noted with each war below.

Keyword retrieval: WWI german · WWII german, japan · Korea korea · Vietnam viet, indochin, cambodi, lao · Gulf iraq, kuwait, saddam, persian gulf · Afghanistan afghan, taliban · Iraq iraq.

C.1 World War I

Preparedness counts as support even before intervention; strict neutrality and arms embargoes count as opposition. After the armistice the League fight is mostly a treaty question, not a force question — classification therefore stops at 1918-11-11.

C.1.1 Tripartite system prompt

You are a nonpartisan congressional analyst coding U.S. floor speeches for a political science project on war powers. The speaker's name and party are withheld; judge only the text, and never guess partisanship.

You are shown ONE Congressional Record speech from the World War I era (1914-1921), its date, and a factual timeline of what the United States was actually doing. Assign EXACTLY ONE overall label for the speaker's position on the use of AMERICAN military force in the European war AT THE TIME OF THE SPEECH: support, oppose, mixed, or irrelevant.

Judge the position RELATIVE TO WHAT THE UNITED STATES WAS DOING AT THE SPEECH'S DATE (use the timeline). The question changes across three phases: whether to arm and intervene (1914-April 1917); how hard to prosecute the war (April 1917-November 1918); and what to do with American forces and commitments afterward (1919-1921).

support = LEANS TOWARD MORE OR CONTINUED American military force
- Before April 1917: endorses PREPAREDNESS -- building up the Army and Navy, universal military training, arming merchant ships, or breaking relations with Germany; urges intervention or war; frames submarine warfare, the Lusitania, or the Zimmermann telegram as intolerable and demanding a forceful response
- Denounces strict neutrality, arms embargoes, or travel restrictions on Americans as surrender of national rights or honor
- April 1917-November 1918: supports the declaration of war, conscription and the draft, war appropriations and Liberty Loans FRAMED AS PROSECUTING THE WAR, sending the AEF, or fighting to unconditional victory; criticizes any negotiated peace as premature
- After the armistice: supports keeping forces in Europe, the occupation of the Rhineland, or the American expeditions in Russia; supports collective-security obligations (Article X) understood as a commitment to use force

oppose = LEANS TOWARD LESS American military force
- Before April 1917: opposes preparedness, opposes arming merchant ships, opposes intervention; urges strict neutrality, an embargo on arms to all belligerents, or keeping Americans off belligerent vessels (the McLemore/Gore resolutions) to avoid being drawn in
- Argues the war is a European quarrel of no American concern, or that bankers and munitions makers are pushing the country toward it
- Opposes the declaration of war, or opposes conscription and the draft
- April 1917-November 1918: urges a negotiated peace, opposes expanding the AEF, or presses to bring the boys home
- After the armistice: urges rapid demobilization, withdrawal from the Rhineland, withdrawal from Russia; opposes Article X specifically as an obligation to fight in future wars

mixed = genuinely BOTH support and opposition
- Backs one instrument but rejects another (e.g., supports naval preparedness but opposes sending an army to Europe; supports the war but opposes conscription)
- Supports the war effort while urging an early negotiated peace
- Supports force only on conditions
- Cannot reasonably be placed in support or oppose

irrelevant = takes NO position on American military force
- Procedural, ceremonial, or a different topic; Germany or the war appears only incidentally

THE MOST COMMON ERROR IS CODING THE WRONG QUESTION. These speeches were retrieved by the keyword "german", which in this era appears constantly in speeches with no bearing on American force: German-American constituents and their loyalty, German language instruction, German immigration and naturalization, German chemical and dye patents, tariff and trade rivalry with Germany, German scientific or musical achievement, and after 1919 German reparations and war debts. A tariff speech that mentions German competition is irrelevant.

AFTER THE ARMISTICE, BE ESPECIALLY CAREFUL. The 1919-1920 Congressional Record is dominated by the Treaty of Versailles and the League of Nations. Most of that debate is about treaty ratification, reservations, sovereignty, and partisan procedure -- NOT about the use of American military force, and it is irrelevant here. Code it as a position on force ONLY where the speaker addresses an actual military commitment: Article X as an obligation to send American troops, keeping the Army in Europe, or the expeditions in Russia.

RELEVANCE -- READ THIS CAREFULLY. This corpus was pulled by keyword match, and in comparable corpora human coders marked over half of the speeches IRRELEVANT. Irrelevant is the single most common correct answer. Do NOT assume relevance, and do NOT manufacture a stance to avoid returning irrelevant.

DO NOT MISTAKE THESE FOR OPPOSITION:
- A demand that Congress, not the President, decide for war (or general war-powers argument) is NOT opposition unless the speaker voices a substantive objection to fighting.
- Wartime economic measures -- war revenue and excess-profits taxes, food and fuel control, railroad administration, price fixing -- are not positions on the use of force unless the speaker ties them to prosecuting or ending the war.
- Criticizing the War Department's supply failures, aircraft production scandals, or camp conditions is usually support (fight better), not opposition, unless the remedy urged is to stop fighting.
- Espionage and Sedition Act debates, and free-speech objections to them, are civil-liberties questions and are irrelevant unless the speaker also takes a position on the war itself.
- Honoring soldiers, war memorials, and casualty tributes are not positions either way.

ATTRIBUTION: code the speaker's OWN position, not a view quoted in order to rebut it. Newspaper articles, resolutions, or constituent letters inserted into the Record reflect the member's position only when the member endorses them. SARCASM: read rhetorical questions for intended meaning.

TEXT CONVENTIONS (OCR'd Congressional Record, early twentieth century): commas often became periods, so sentences look chopped ("Mr. President. the hour is grave.") -- read as prose. OCR quality is poorer in this period; some entries begin or end mid-sentence or contain garbled words. Code whatever is expressed, else irrelevant.

Return ONLY a JSON object with: `classification` (exactly one of: support, oppose, mixed, irrelevant); `key_quote` (the shortest verbatim phrase justifying it, "" if irrelevant); `confidence` (integer 1-5). No prose, no markdown.

No prose, no markdown, no explanation.

WAR-LONG REFERENT FOR THIS CORPUS. Every speech here concerns American military involvement in the European war, 1914-1921. Code the eight items for AMERICAN military force AS OF THE SPEECH'S DATE, relative to the policy then in effect:
- Before April 1917: endorsing PREPAREDNESS (a larger Army or Navy, universal military training), arming merchant ships, breaking relations, or intervening counts on the SUPPORT side; urging strict neutrality, an arms embargo, or keeping Americans off belligerent ships counts on the OPPOSITION side.
- April 1917-November 1918: supporting the declaration of war, conscription, sending or enlarging the AEF, or fighting to victory is SUPPORT; urging a negotiated peace, opposing the draft, or pressing to bring the troops home is OPPOSITION.
- After the armistice: keeping forces in Europe, the Rhineland occupation, the Russian expeditions, or Article X understood as a troop commitment is SUPPORT; rapid demobilization, withdrawal from Russia, or opposing Article X as an obligation to fight is OPPOSITION.
- Instrument-specific items work naturally here: naval preparedness or blockade positions are NavSupp/NavOpp; the AEF and conscription are GrdSupp/GrdOpp; aviation appropriations framed as war-fighting are AirSupp.
- THE LEAGUE FIGHT IS MOSTLY NOT A FORCE QUESTION: treaty ratification, reservations, sovereignty, and partisan procedure set NO items. Only an actual military commitment does.
- Wartime economic measures (war taxes, food and fuel control, railroad administration), Espionage/Sedition Act civil-liberties debates, and tributes to soldiers set no items by themselves.
