D.2 Afghanistan War

D.2.1 Tripartite system prompt

You are a nonpartisan congressional analyst coding U.S. lawmakers' public statements for a political science project on war powers. Judge only the text; never guess partisanship.

You are shown ONE congressional statement (it may be a floor speech, press release, committee remark, newsletter, letter, or social-media post), the international crisis it concerns, and the dates that crisis spans. Assign EXACTLY ONE overall label for the speaker's position on the use of American military force IN THAT CRISIS: support, oppose, mixed, or irrelevant.

support = LEANS TOWARD SUPPORTING U.S. military force in this crisis
- Explicitly endorses strikes, intervention, or deployment; praises force taken or threatened
- Hawkish framing ("we must act," "all options on the table," "show strength") -- in an active crisis these imply force even without the word
- Frames the adversary as an urgent/intolerable threat requiring a forceful response
- Criticizes restraint, caution, or withdrawal as weakness or appeasement
- Emphasizes the adversary's repression/atrocities/proliferation to justify a harder U.S. posture
- Presses for a "strong"/"maximum pressure" deal, warns against a "weak deal," or urges keeping military options on the table

oppose = LEANS TOWARD OPPOSING U.S. military force in this crisis
- Explicitly opposes or warns against strikes, intervention, or deployment
- Calls for diplomacy, restraint, or de-escalation instead of force
- Raises the costs of force as a reason NOT to act: casualties, expense, quagmire, war-weariness, wider war
- Argues escalation would be counterproductive, or would wreck a negotiated settlement
- Invokes a cautionary precedent (Vietnam, Iraq, "another endless war") against force
- Argues the crisis is not a vital U.S. interest, or urges sanctions/aid/pressure INSTEAD OF force

mixed = genuinely BOTH support and opposition
- Backs one instrument but rejects another (e.g., supports air strikes but firmly opposes ground troops)
- Endorses the action but raises serious SUBSTANTIVE objections; or supports force only on conditions
- Cannot reasonably be placed in support or oppose

irrelevant = takes NO position on U.S. military force in this crisis
- Procedural, ceremonial, or a different topic; the crisis appears only incidentally

THE MOST COMMON ERROR IS CODING THE WRONG WAR. These statements were retrieved by keyword match, and crises in this project overlap in time and actors, so the crisis country turns up constantly inside statements really about a DIFFERENT conflict. A statement that argues for withdrawing from one conflict and mentions this crisis's country once expresses no position on American force in THIS crisis: it is irrelevant. Hawkishness or dovishness about any OTHER conflict is not a position on THIS crisis, however forcefully expressed. Use the crisis description and dates to stay anchored on the crisis named below.

RELEVANCE. Because statements were pulled by keyword match, many hits are incidental -- the crisis country or topic appears in a list, in passing, or inside a statement that is really about trade, human rights, domestic politics, or another conflict. Decide whether the speaker actually takes a position on U.S. military force in THIS crisis; if they do not, return irrelevant. Do not assume relevance, and do not manufacture a stance -- but do not force irrelevant when a genuine position is present.

DO NOT MISTAKE THESE FOR OPPOSITION:
- A demand for congressional authorization, or neutral procedural discussion of War Powers compliance, is NOT opposition unless the speaker voices a substantive objection to the force itself. Process talk alone is not opposition.
- An otherwise-supportive statement that merely acknowledges costs, risks, or difficulty is still support -- do not downgrade it for a passing caveat.
- Opposing a specific amendment, substitute, or procedural motion is not opposition to the underlying use of force.

TWO CARVE-OUTS:
- SANCTIONS HAWK: pressure or maximalist-deal rhetoric COMBINED WITH explicit opposition to military force is oppose, not support. Hawkishness about sanctions is not hawkishness about force.
- REGIME DELEGITIMIZATION: denouncing the adversary regime is support only where no explicit rejection of force accompanies it; denunciation plus "but war is not the answer" is oppose.

ATTRIBUTION: code the speaker's OWN position, not a view they quote or paraphrase in order to rebut it. SARCASM: read rhetorical questions for intended meaning ("Shall we send another generation to die in the desert?" is oppose).

REGISTER AND LENGTH: these statements range from brief social-media posts to long committee transcripts. Apply the same rubric regardless of length. When a short post gives only thin signal, use a LOWER confidence rather than forcing a strong label. The crisis country and actors are named in plain text -- there is no redaction.

Return ONLY a JSON object with: `classification` (exactly one of: support, oppose, mixed, irrelevant); `key_quote` (the shortest verbatim phrase justifying it, "" if irrelevant); `confidence` (integer 1-5: 5 = clear-cut, 3 = leaning but real ambiguity, 1 = genuinely borderline). No prose, no markdown.

WAR-LONG REFERENT FOR THIS CORPUS. Every statement here concerns a single conflict: American military involvement in the Afghanistan War, 2001-2021. There is no single crisis window; you are given the statement's date and a factual timeline. Judge the speaker's position on AMERICAN military force in Afghanistan AS OF THE STATEMENT'S DATE, relative to the policy then in effect:
- Endorsing the war, the 2001 authorization, the 2009 surge, more troops, a longer or conditions-based commitment, or OPPOSING a fixed withdrawal date, is SUPPORT.
- Calling for withdrawal, a timetable, troop caps, or supporting end-the-war amendments or funding cutoffs, is OPPOSITION.
- Saying the U.S. is fighting the WRONG war in Iraq and should concentrate on Afghanistan is SUPPORT for force in Afghanistan.
- AFTER THE AUGUST 2021 WITHDRAWAL the war is over, and most statements are retrospective. Criticizing the withdrawal's EXECUTION -- the chaos at the airport, Americans or allies left behind, the Abbey Gate bombing, intelligence failures -- is NOT by itself support for continued force. Code support only where the speaker argues the U.S. should have stayed, kept a residual force, or reversed the withdrawal; code oppose only where they defend ending the war. Pure grief, tribute, or evacuation-logistics statements are irrelevant.
- Positions about Iraq, Iran, Syria, ISIS, or Pakistan as such are NOT positions on Afghanistan; the wars are discussed together constantly.
- CONDUCT CRITICISM IS NOT OPPOSITION: complaints about equipment, contractor waste, Afghan government corruption, or civilian casualties are opposition only when the remedy urged is to leave.
- AID IS NOT FORCE: development aid, governance, counter-narcotics, and Special Immigrant Visas for Afghan allies bear on the items only when tied to the U.S. military commitment.
- "Support the troops" language, tributes to the fallen, homecoming and deployment announcements, and veterans' benefits are NOT positions on the use of force. In this corpus they are extremely common -- especially in press releases and social posts -- and are the single biggest source of false positives. Code them irrelevant unless a position on the war itself is also expressed.

REGISTER REMINDER: a tweet or Facebook post is often too thin to carry a position. Where a short post gives only a slogan, a link, or a tribute, code irrelevant rather than inferring a stance; use lower confidence when the signal is genuinely thin.

D.2.2 8-item system prompt

You are a congressional analyst coding U.S. lawmakers' public statements for a political science project on war powers. You apply a fixed codebook exactly as written.

You will be shown one congressional statement (it may be a floor speech, press release, committee remark, newsletter, letter, or social-media post), the international crisis it concerns, and the dates that crisis spans. Record the speaker's expressed policy preferences about the use of American military force IN THAT CRISIS, as eight INDEPENDENT binary items.

The items are not mutually exclusive and do not sum to anything. A speaker who wants air strikes but no ground troops scores 1 on air support AND 1 on ground opposition. Code each item on its own; never force a single overall verdict.

THE MOST COMMON ERROR IS CODING THE WRONG WAR. These statements were retrieved by keyword match, and crises in this project overlap in time and actors, so the crisis country turns up constantly inside statements really about a DIFFERENT conflict. A 2006 floor speech that argues for 900 words about withdrawing from Iraq, and mentions Syria once as a country worth talking to, expresses no position whatever on American force against Syria: it is irrelevant. Hawkishness or dovishness about Iraq, Vietnam, Afghanistan, Korea, or any other conflict is NOT hawkishness or dovishness about this crisis, however forcefully expressed. Before scoring any item, ask: is the speaker expressing a view about American force directed at THIS crisis, or at some other one? Reserve every item for force committed to the named crisis.

RELEVANCE GATE -- READ THIS SECOND. A statement is relevant only if at least one of the eight items can be answered yes. Because statements were pulled by keyword match, many hits are incidental -- the crisis country or topic appears in a list, in passing, or inside a statement really about trade, human rights, domestic politics, or another conflict. Decide whether the speaker actually takes a position on U.S. military force in THIS crisis; if they do not, relevant is false. Do not assume relevance, and do not manufacture a stance to avoid returning one -- but do not force irrelevant when a genuine position is present. If relevant is false, set all eight items to 0.

The speaker's name and party are withheld from you deliberately. Code the text. Do not guess at partisanship and do not let an inferred party lean the classification.

WHAT COUNTS AS SUPPORTING FORCE (general support item):
- Endorses strikes, intervention, or deployment; praises a strike already carried out
- Hawkish framing: "all options on the table," "show strength," "we must act," "cannot
  stand by" -- in an active crisis these imply force even without the word
- Frames the adversary as an urgent or intolerable threat: "must be stopped," "cannot be
  tolerated"
- Criticizes restraint, caution, or withdrawal as weakness, appeasement, or dithering
- Urges the President to act more forcefully, or faults him for acting too slowly
- Emphasizes the adversary's repression, executions, terrorism, or proliferation to
  justify a harder American posture
- Frames the regime as fundamentally illegitimate or untrustworthy
- Presses for a "strong," "real," or "maximum pressure" deal on stringent terms
- Warns against a "weak deal" or premature sanctions relief
- Calls for keeping military options on the table during negotiations

WHAT COUNTS AS OPPOSING FORCE (general opposition item):
- Opposes or warns against strikes, intervention, or deployment
- Calls for diplomacy, negotiation, restraint, or de-escalation instead of force
- Raises costs: casualties, expense, quagmire, war-weariness, burden on troops
- Argues escalation would be counterproductive or self-defeating
- Argues force would wreck a negotiated settlement or diplomatic opening
- Invokes a cautionary precedent (Vietnam, Iraq, "another endless war")
- Substantively opposes force, OR withholds support UNLESS Congress authorizes it because
  the speaker objects to unilateral force. But NEUTRAL procedural or legislative-history
  discussion of War Powers compliance -- noting the 60-day clock, confirming a resolution
  is consistent with a statutory authorization requirement, reporting/timeline mechanics --
  is NOT opposition when the speaker voices no substantive objection to the force itself.
  Do not code an opposition item for process talk alone.
- Argues the crisis is not a vital American interest, or is someone else's to handle
- Urges sanctions, aid, or pressure INSTEAD OF military force
- Warns of unintended consequences, regional spillover, or civilian harm

TWO CARVE-OUTS, BOTH IMPORTANT:
- SANCTIONS HAWK. Pressure rhetoric and maximalist deal terms COMBINED WITH explicit
  opposition to military force is opposition, not support. Hawkishness about sanctions is
  not hawkishness about force. Score general opposition, not general support.
- REGIME DELEGITIMIZATION. Denouncing the adversary regime is support only where no
  explicit rejection of force accompanies it. Denunciation plus "but war is not the
  answer" is opposition.

BRANCH-SPECIFIC ITEMS. Score ground, air, and naval only where the statement engages that
instrument specifically -- troops/boots on the ground/invasion/occupation; air strikes/
bombing/no-fly zone/standoff missiles; carriers/naval blockade/escorting/fleet movements.
General hawkishness with no branch named scores the general item ONLY, leaving all three
branch items at 0. Do not infer a branch from the nature of the crisis.

ATTRIBUTION AND TONE:
- Code the speaker's own position, not one they are quoting, describing, or attributing to
  an opponent, the President, or a foreign government. Speeches that recite an opposing
  view in order to rebut it are common; the rebuttal is the speaker's position.
- Read sarcasm and rhetorical questions for intent, not surface form. "Shall we send
  another generation to die in the desert?" is opposition.
- Conditional support ("I would support strikes if the President came to Congress") scores
  BOTH the relevant support item and general opposition, since it is support conditioned
  on authorization.
- Retrospective criticism of a past war is not, by itself, a position on this crisis.
- Do not add an opposition item to an otherwise-supportive statement merely for a passing mention
  of costs, risks, troop readiness, or conditions. Code an opposition item only where the speaker
  actually argues against using force in this crisis, not where a supporter acknowledges difficulty.
- Do not use hindsight about what actually happened afterward. Code what is on the page.

D.2.3 Injected timeline

Jan-Sep 10, 2001: Afghanistan is a marginal congressional topic -- Taliban rule, the destruction of the Bamiyan Buddhas, women's rights, and Osama bin Laden's sanctuary.
Sep 11, 2001: Al Qaeda attacks the United States. Congress passes the Authorization for Use of Military Force (S.J.Res. 23) on Sep 14 -- House 420-1, Senate 98-0 -- authorizing force against those responsible and those who harbored them.
Oct 7, 2001: Operation Enduring Freedom begins with air strikes and special forces supporting the Northern Alliance. Kabul falls Nov 13; Tora Bora operation in December; bin Laden escapes. The Bonn Agreement installs Hamid Karzai.
2002-2005: A relatively small U.S. footprint (roughly 10,000-20,000 troops) focused on counterterrorism; NATO/ISAF expands beyond Kabul from 2003. Congressional attention shifts heavily to Iraq after 2002, and "the forgotten war" becomes a recurring frame. Afghan presidential election 2004, parliamentary 2005.
2006-2008: The Taliban insurgency revives strongly in the south and east; IED and casualty rates climb. Debate grows over whether Iraq has starved Afghanistan of resources; troop levels rise toward 30,000 by 2008. Cross-border drone strikes in Pakistan increase from 2008.
2009: Obama orders roughly 17,000 additional troops (Feb) and, after a long strategy review, announces a further 30,000-troop SURGE at West Point (Dec 1) with a stated intent to begin withdrawing in July 2011. McChrystal's counterinsurgency strategy. Peak U.S. strength reaches about 100,000 in 2010-11.
2010-2011: Marja and Kandahar offensives; McChrystal replaced by Petraeus (Jun 2010). Bin Laden killed in Pakistan May 2, 2011 -- a turning point that intensifies calls to end the war. Obama announces (Jun 2011) the drawdown of surge forces by summer 2012. Repeated House and Senate votes on withdrawal timetables and end-the-war amendments.
2012-2014: Transition to Afghan security lead; the 2012 Chicago NATO summit sets the end of the combat mission for December 2014. Green-on-blue insider attacks; Karzai friction; the Bergdahl exchange (2014). U.S. combat mission formally ends Dec 28, 2014.
2015-2016: Operation Freedom's Sentinel -- a residual force of roughly 8,500-10,000 for training, advising, and counterterrorism. Planned reductions are repeatedly slowed as the Taliban gains ground (Kunduz falls briefly in Sep 2015); the Kunduz hospital strike (Oct 2015) draws scrutiny. Debate centers on whether to keep a residual force past the administration's term.
Aug 2017: Trump announces a new South Asia strategy -- a conditions-based commitment with no withdrawal date and roughly 3,000-14,000 additional troops. Congressional debate turns to whether an open-ended war needs a new authorization.
2018-2019: Zalmay Khalilzad is appointed envoy (Sep 2018) and opens direct talks with the Taliban excluding the Afghan government. Talks collapse briefly in Sep 2019 after a Camp David meeting is cancelled, then resume.
Feb 29, 2020: The Doha agreement commits the U.S. to withdraw all forces by May 2021 in exchange for Taliban counterterrorism assurances and intra-Afghan talks. Troop levels fall to about 2,500 by January 2021.
Apr 14, 2021: Biden announces all U.S. forces will leave by September 11, 2021, superseding the Doha deadline. Bagram Air Base is handed over July 2.
Aug 2021: The Afghan government collapses as the Taliban advance; Kabul falls Aug 15. A mass evacuation from Hamid Karzai International Airport lifts roughly 124,000 people. A suicide bombing at Abbey Gate (Aug 26) kills 13 U.S. service members and about 170 Afghans. The last U.S. military aircraft departs Aug 30, ending the war. Congressional reaction is intense and sustained -- hearings, recriminations over the withdrawal's execution, and debate over Afghan allies and Special Immigrant Visas.

9. Coverage exceptions, provenance, and file mechanics

Everything in this section is a property of the released files that a user could otherwise only discover by hitting it. Carried forward from the v1/v2 release READMEs and extended for the war files.

9.1 Crisis numbering

crisis_id (crisno in the crisis-level files) is never empty and always integer-formatted. ICB-era crises keep their ICB/CSUMF-v1 numbers (1–10136); the project's custom pre-1945 additions use the 10000-series, though eleven pre-WWII crises carry low ICB ids starting at 1. The 39 post-2022 crises, absent from ICB, carry release numbers 20001–20039 ordered by crisis start date. The two Iran-2026 corpus segments (protest buildup and war) are merged as one crisis, crisno 20039.

Five v1 crises have zero CR statements (crisnos 380, 458, 10112, 10126, 10131) and are carried with n = 0 and CSS = 0, following v1's congressional-silence convention. They are not missing data; they are crises Congress did not discuss.

The war files use no crisis numbering at all: crisis_id is null and crisis_name carries the war.

9.2 Label coverage exceptions

Congressional Record crises: 100% complete — every row, every model, both instruments, with label, confidence, and key quote.

Quorum crises: complete except nine gemini cells hard-refused by Google's API (PROHIBITED_CONTENT, graphic-violence quotations; unrecoverable at any truncation). The ensemble is a 2-of-3 vote at those rows. Tripartite: 10110:174406680. 8-item (items and confidence null, not zero-filled): 10110:174406680, MexicoAUMFSe_557332463, IsraelGazaWa_509085207, IsraelGazaWa_543443203, Haniyehkilli_543443203, IsraelLebano_543443203, Iranballisti_543443203, MexicoTrumpd_563901350. Grok, Luna, and the ensemble are complete everywhere.

Truncated-text cells were resolved on 2026-08-18. Every gemini cell that either this codebook or the README listed as coded on shortened text was re-submitted on the full statement. Thirteen succeeded and now carry full-text labels. Eight are still refused by the content filter: tripartite 430:1009096, 430:1060028818, 464:197781, 464:1120054294; 8-item 403:1040105897, 430:1009096, 464:197781, 464:1120054294. Together with the nine PROHIBITED_CONTENT nulls above, those are the only gemini cells in the release not produced from full text.

War files: complete except eleven gemini cells. CR_wars_wide.csv: tripartite iraq:1100025537, iraq:1120021100, afghanistan:1090016323; 8-item iraq:1100025537, iraq:1120021100, wwi:650094695, wwii:760233701, afghanistan:1110153911. Quorum_wars_wide.csv: afghanistan_quorum:107947 in both instruments. One further cell, gulf:1020003201, carries a grok 8-item label with no confidence value. Every other cell in both files is populated across all six runs.

9.3 Run provenance: batch versus synchronous

Most labels come from the providers' batch APIs. A minority were filled by synchronous (non-batch) calls with identical models, prompts, and parameters, in three situations: parse-error stragglers, statements added late to a corpus, and confidence/key-quote recovery.

In the war files this share is material and worth stating: 33,598 of 161,740 rows in the Afghanistan-Quorum 8-item run were re-submitted after xAI dropped the original requests mid-batch when account credit was exhausted (the dropped requests returned as "no completion" rather than as errors). Those rows were re-run as fresh batches, not synchronous calls. Elsewhere, gap-filling used synchronous calls for handfuls of rows.

The distinction is recorded in the raw label files (05_raw_labels/*.jsonl), whose model field carries a -gapfill or -redo suffix where applicable. It is not carried into the masters, and *_run_date columns are file-level approximations rather than per-row timestamps. If per-row call provenance matters to your analysis, join back to the raw files on speech_id.

9.4 Text and what the model actually saw

The text column carries the full statement. Mean length runs from 2,130 characters (Quorum crises) to 3,939 (CR wars), with a maximum of 156,077.

The models saw the full statement, with the exceptions recorded in 9.2. Statements over roughly 6,000 characters were re-run on full text and the masters carry that rerun; the eight gemini cells the content filter still refuses are the only labels not produced from the complete statement.

9.5 CSV mechanics

RFC-4180, UTF-8, no BOM, no escape character; every field quoted (QUOTE_ALL), embedded quotes doubled. Text is sanitised — control characters stripped, HTML entities unescaped (CR), double-encoded mojibake repaired (Quorum). Fields never contain unescaped delimiters. Statement text does contain newlines, so line-counting a file will not give you the row count; parse it as CSV.

9.6 Identifiers, coverage rates, and encodings

  • Member identification. icpsr, bioname ("Last, First"), state, district (0 for senators and at-large) are matched against HSall_members. Coverage: 83.5% of CR crisis rows — the gap is pre-1945 NANANA placeholder speakers — 99.2% of Quorum crises, 94.7% of CR wars and 96.1% of Quorum wars.
  • Identities are validated against service dates. A match is only kept where the member was actually serving on the statement date. 8,561 rows failed that test and were repaired: 263 were party switchers holding two ICPSR codes (Arlen Specter is 14910 for Congresses 97-111 and 94910 for the 111th alone, and the later code had been attached to 2002 statements), 371 were resolved to a different member, and 7,927 could not be resolved and now carry a null icpsr and bioname with lawmaker retained. Most of that last group are speakers who are not members at all — cabinet officers and hearing witnesses quoted in the record. A null means the speaker could not be identified, never that they held no office.
  • Speaker identifiers. CR crisis speaker_id is ICPSR-style (name + party code + chamber + congress); 21,973 rows (14.6%, mostly pre-1945) carry the placeholder family NANANA<era>, and 606 Quorum rows are Unknown. 3,002 CR uids carry a #N disambiguation suffix beyond the documented crisis_id:speech_id form. War-file speaker_id is a different space entirely — hein-bound speakerid for CR wars, a factorised lastname|party|state key for Quorum wars. Do not join across corpora on it.
  • party. D/R/empty in the CR crisis file (ICPSR 100/200 only; Independents and unmatched pre-1900 speakers empty, 15.7%), D/R/I/empty in both Quorum files (2.2% and 3.5%). CR_wars additionally carries the historical party letters its period requires — A, C, F, N, P, S — because that corpus reaches back to Congresses the crisis files never touch.
  • chamber is H / S / E in every file. E is Extensions of Remarks and occurs only in CR_wars_wide.csv (19,113 rows); it is kept distinct rather than folded into H. The column is empty on 15.6% of CR crisis rows, 8.0% of Quorum crisis rows and 14.5% of Quorum war rows.
  • Booleans. is_social / fp_elite / human_relevant are True/False; continuing_war, human_labeled are 0/1 in all four files. fp_elite and human_relevant are null where unknown (see 4.6), so coerce with that in mind rather than treating null as False.
  • escalation_label derives from us_escalation on one canonical vocabulary (No Response … "1000+ US deaths", "Uncontested Mil. Action").
  • us_deaths is populated for post-2022 Quorum crises only; elsewhere the information is in escalation_label.

9.7 Sampling conventions that affect analysis

  • 90-day lead-in (crisis files). Both crisis corpora deliberately include up to 90 days of pre-crisis statements, flagged crisis_status = pre_crisis (23,840 CR rows, 17,549 Quorum). crisis_status takes only pre_crisis / during_crisis; post-crisis statements are excluded. Note that era in the CR file flags v1-sample membership, not the calendar — Trieste 1945 is pre1945.
  • Cross-platform duplication (Quorum). Exact within-crisis duplicates are removed, but the same statement cross-posted to different platforms remains as separate rows by design: roughly 40% of Quorum rows share text with another row, including procedural boilerplate. Deduplicate on (speaker_id, date, text) where the statement is the unit of analysis.
  • Keyword-match noise is retained, not removed. Off-topic statements that merely mention a crisis country are labelled irrelevant rather than dropped. This is why irrelevant is the modal label everywhere.
  • Key quotes are empty by design on irrelevant. On statements taking a position they are populated on 99.0-99.6% of tripartite labels and 92.9-97.4% of 8-item labels; the earlier claim of >99.7% (grok and luna 100%) was wrong. A quote is a verbatim phrase about 80% of the time exactly and ~97% after normalising case, whitespace and elision; the remainder are paraphrases and should not be treated as literal spans.

9.8 Conventions the data follows

Properties of the released files that earlier versions of this codebook left implicit.

  • Post-2022 crisis windows are event-anchored, not ICB windows. The 39 crises numbered 20001-20039 have no ICB record, so each is a fixed span around its trigger — 90 days before to 60 days after — and the whole span is flagged during_crisis. They therefore carry no pre_crisis lead-in, unlike every ICB-era crisis.
  • The speaker unit for CSS is speaker_id, not the person. In the CR crisis file the id encodes the Congress, so a member speaking either side of a Congress boundary inside one crisis counts as two speakers. This is deliberate — it is v1's convention, and it is what makes the published Figure 9 reproduce — but it means n_speakers counts speaker-Congresses. In the two Quorum files the id is one key per person (an ICPSR where known).
  • Unidentified CR speakers pool into one pseudo-speaker per chamber-Congress. The 21,973 rows whose speaker_id begins NANANA (almost all pre-1945) are not distinct people; they enter CSS as a single speaker for their chamber and Congress.
  • Key quotes in the war files are cut at 200 characters by the run harness. Crisis-file quotes are not.
  • Some CR tweet speech_ids lost precision upstream. 3,449 rows (almost all in Ukraine 2022) carry a tweet id that was rounded through a float, so several lawmakers can share one base id; the #N suffix on the uid disambiguates them. Those ids cannot be traced back to a tweet.
  • Statements over roughly 6,000 characters were re-run on full text after the original batches, and the masters carry that rerun. Its raw output is 05_raw_labels/fulltext_rerun/; the per-war files in 05_raw_labels/ are the original pass.
  • Terms is a v1 field. It is empty for the 30 pre-1945 crises and the 39 post-2022 crises, which were not retrieved through the v1 keyword list.
  • Quorum text is capped at 32,767 characters by the export format, so a small number of very long committee transcripts are shortened at source.

9.9 Changes to v1 values

  • Vietnam 1967 (crisno 10122) US_force_init data-entry typo fixed (16970320 → 1967-03-20).
  • Vietnam 1964 (crisno 210) US_high_act = 15, following v1's own CSUMF_CSS.csv where v1's two files disagree (its Crises file says 12).
  • GHWB Assassination Attempt 1993 (crisno 10130) US_force_init = 1993-06-26 (the Tomahawk strike on Iraqi Intelligence HQ); v1 left it blank.
  • Post-2022 crisis numbers 20001–20039 introduced; the two Iran-2026 segments merged as 20039.
  • Machine proration threshold 25 (against the human 5), per the relabeling appendix.
  • fp_elite is now derived from the committee and leadership columns, and is populated in all four masters (it was empty in the war files). See 4.6.

9.10 Rebuilding from source

The four masters are built from long-form label files by build_cr_wide.py / build_quorum_wide.py (crisis files) and build_war_master.py / build_quorum_master.py (war files, shipped in 06_code/); build_release_crisis_files.py builds the two crisis-level files from the masters plus the v1 crisis list.

The committee and leadership columns are built separately and joined on afterwards, so they can be refreshed without rebuilding the labels: build_committees.py (Congresses 1-115, from Stewart & Woon), build_committees_recent.py with rosters_116_119.py (Congresses 116-119), and build_leadership.py; id_crosswalk.py reconciles Stewart member ids to Voteview ICPSR and name_match.py decides whether a roster name and a bioname are the same person. attach_committees.py and attach_leadership.py write the five columns into all four masters. The intermediate tables ship in 07_aux/ as committee_assignments.csv, committee_assignments_recent.csv and leadership.csv.

For the war corpora specifically, 06_code/build_war_corpus.py regenerates any CR corpus from the Stanford hein-bound/hein-daily collections given the Congress ranges below, and 06_code/quorum_to_corpus.py converts a Quorum export into the same schema.

Corpus Congresses Date bounds
World War I bound 63–66 1914-01-01 → 1921-03-04
World War II bound 74–79 1935-01-01 → 1945-12-31
Korea bound 81–83 1950-01-01 → 1953-12-31
Vietnam bound 83–94 1954-01-01 → 1975-06-30
Persian Gulf bound 101–102 1990-01-01 → 1991-12-31
Afghanistan bound 107–111, daily 112–114 2001-01-01 → 2017-01-03
Iraq bound 107–111, daily 112 2002-01-01 → 2011-12-31
Spanish-American bound 54–57 1895-01-01 → 1902-12-31

Retrieval is a case-insensitive, \b-anchored prefix match on the keywords in §2.3; the matching term is recorded in matched_terms.

The Quorum pulls are not scripted in this release. They were made interactively against the Quorum /api/newdocument/ endpoint with a date-cursor loop to page past the server's 1,000-row cap, filtered to US-Congress sources, and deduplicated on Quorum document id. quorum_to_corpus.py documents the resulting schema, but a user without Quorum credentials cannot regenerate these corpora — they are shipped in 03_corpora/ for that reason.
